* TODO The ceremony prompt goes quiet on evidence of its first step, and never reports its own completion :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       29734f79-e698-43dd-abf8-14bc86b8b0ae
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 10:28]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-31 Mon 10:19]--[2026-08-31 Mon 10:20] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-31 Mon 10:19]--[2026-08-31 Mon 10:20] measure PLANNING against real use
CLOCK: [2026-08-31 Mon 10:27]--[2026-08-31 Mon 10:29] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-31 Mon 10:27]--[2026-08-31 Mon 10:29] measure PLANNING against real use
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 10:28] \\
  quiet test and completion report
:END:

*Found 2026-08-31 by the user asking whether the ceremonies would run at the next session start.* They will not, and the reason is a seam rather than a bug.

*The report names three things it is waiting on and goes quiet on evidence of one.* Its text is "Waiting: N queued item(s) pending review, N :LOGBOOK: drawer(s) out of order, N finished heading(s) not yet archived". Its quiet test is =--ceremony-reviewed-today-p=, which looks for a =CLOCK:= line dated today on the =Review attention= heading — created by running the *apply* command and nothing else. On 2026-08-31 four applies produced four such lines, the report went silent, and *106 finished headings sat unarchived*, drawers unconsolidated, separation un-normalised.

*This is [[id:806ff394-3ede-4d67-82aa-9c44fe1cf86e][806ff394]] working and reaching slightly short.* That heading replaced "a model remembers to stamp it" with "the act leaves its own evidence", which is strictly more reliable and removed the model from the nag guard. But the act it chose is *apply* — the one step a human always performs, being the one they came to do — while the three that follow are precisely the ones that get skipped. The signal is derived from the step least in need of a reminder.

*Can the ceremony be automated? One quarter of it cannot; three quarters already can.* Mapped rather than assumed:

| step | surface | automatable |
|------+---------+-------------|
| apply the queue | =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review=, no MCP tool | *no, by design* |
| =consolidate-all-drawers= | elisp; from Lisp defaults to writing | yes, via =emacsclient= |
| =normalize-heading-separation= | elisp; same | yes, via =emacsclient= |
| archive | =org_archive= | yes, already a tool |

Apply is human-only because org's native state-change logging completes only inside a genuinely interactive command, and =WAITING(w@/!)= and =CANCELLED(c@)= carry =@= cookies that *prompt* — a non-interactive call hangs on exactly the two keywords. That is the constraint the whole queue exists to route around, not a gap to close.

*So the honest shape is: the human applies, the agent finishes, and the finish is reported.* CLAUDE.md already says the last three steps are "yours if they ask" — and on 2026-08-31 nobody asked, four times, which is how 106 accumulated. "If they ask" is the same remembering-dependency 806ff394 removed from the other half.

*Two changes, and they are separable:*

1. *The quiet test should attest to what the report claims to be waiting on* — pending items, drawer order, unarchived count — rather than to the apply alone. Cheapest form: go quiet when all three counters are zero, which is derived and needs no new signal.
2. *There is no completion report at all.* The ceremony prompts at =SessionStart= and never says what happened, so success and failure look identical from the outside — and a step that silently did not run is the failure mode this project keeps finding. A line per step, success or failure, said in-session when the agent runs them.

*Not proposed: automating apply.* And not proposed: the agent running the last three *unasked* — that is a policy question about the user's attention rather than a defect, and it should be decided rather than assumed.


* TODO Add a body read: return a heading whole, drawers included, and let the caller extract :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a0028a4e-39cb-4653-83cc-446b810234ac
:CREATED:  [2026-08-31 Mon 09:48]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-31 Mon 09:49] \\
  the build ff1352b3 decided
:END:

*The build [[id:ff1352b3-2732-4422-9f0d-4b3cb6fd8ff3][ff1352b3]] decided 2026-08-31*, filed separately because the decision is complete and the implementation is not.

*What it replaces.* Retrieving one heading currently takes three tools and hand-done arithmetic: =grep -n ":ID: 4f8500e6"= to find it, =awk= to locate the next sibling *at the right level* and so compute the subtree's end, then =Read= with the resulting offset and line count. The arithmetic is the fragile part, because the extent depends on finding the next heading at the right depth — which is exactly what org already knows.

*Nearly all plumbing.* =claude-code-ide-org--subtree-text-at-point= already returns a subtree from its heading through its last child without modifying the buffer; it is used on the clocktable path. Scope resolution — full ids, 8-character prefixes, and the shape-based error for an id that resolves to nothing — is shared with =org_outline= since [[id:2d9eeebd-4375-4790-8485-92b244236a21][2d9eeebd]].

*Returns the heading whole, drawers included, and does not filter.* That is the design rather than laziness: the property schema splits two ways, not three ([[id:ff1352b3-2732-4422-9f0d-4b3cb6fd8ff3][ff1352b3]] measured it), so there is no third surface for "metadata". =:CREATED:=, the plan link and the =ARCHIVE_*= provenance all describe *this* heading and travel with it; the caller extracts what it needs.

*Two things to decide while building, neither obvious:*

- *Whether it returns the subtree or only the heading's own body.* The primitive returns the subtree through the last child. For a leaf they are the same, and 92% of level-1 headings are leaves — but for a story the subtree can be hundreds of lines, and "show me this heading" usually means the heading. An argument, or two verbs, or default-to-own-body with an explicit opt-in.
- *What it does about =:PLAN:=.* Readers are told to skip that drawer. Returning it whole is honest and consistent with the above; suppressing it by default would be the first filtering decision the tool makes, which is the thing this design avoids.

*Deliberately out of scope:* shrinking =org_outline='s front matter. Build this first — at that point the front matter's remaining unique content is about one line, and the question answers itself.

* TODO org_capture should accept an initial TODO state, so a heading is not keywordless until apply :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       c74f8663-b14e-4ab9-ad65-d99cb655b3fa
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 17:36]
:CATEGORY: Tools
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 17:37] \\
  companion question, filed on its own
:END:

*Raised as a companion question by [[id:36b811d9-8bd3-4a32-b67d-39ccfe0d66c1][36b811d9]] and filed on its own 2026-08-28.* =org_capture= deliberately writes *no* TODO keyword — its description says to set the state afterwards with =org_set_todo=, which *queues*. So **every heading captured in a session is keywordless on disk until a human applies the queue**, and a capture-then-set is two operations that land a review pass apart.

*The cost is not hypothetical and is not only cosmetic.* A =:BLOCKER:= naming a heading captured in the same session is *inert*: =org-depend= blocks only on an unfinished TODO keyword, and a keywordless heading has none. =bin/lint-org= reports it, [[id:36b811d9-8bd3-4a32-b67d-39ccfe0d66c1][36b811d9]] hit it within a minute of being written, and [[id:798bb7a1-d5a6-4c08-8cf0-cc91a82d08e6][798bb7a1]] is the same gap seen from the slice side — a member captured this session cannot be rendered or blocked on. Measured this session: roughly a dozen captures, every one of them keywordless for as long as it took to ask for an apply, and one =:BLOCKER:= error that cleared itself only when the queue was run.

*The tension, which is why this is a question rather than an obvious fix.* The queue exists because state transitions are the thing that caused every incident, and it is load-bearing that org's *native* state logging runs inside a genuinely interactive command. A capture that writes a keyword directly would write one state change without a =State= log line and outside the review pass — a small, silent exception to the rule the architecture is built on.

*Three shapes, none chosen:*

1. *An =initial_state= argument that writes the keyword with the heading.* Simplest and most useful; the exception above is real but bounded — a *creation* has no prior state to log a transition from, so nothing is lost that a log line would have carried.
2. *Capture queues a =todo= event alongside itself*, so the keyword arrives through the normal path. Keeps one rule, but does not fix anything: the heading is still keywordless until apply, which is the actual complaint.
3. *Leave it, and make the inertness visible instead.* =org_set_property= already warns when a =:BLOCKER:= names a keywordless heading ([[id:36b811d9-8bd3-4a32-b67d-39ccfe0d66c1][36b811d9]]); the lint already errors. This is the do-nothing option and it is not obviously wrong.

*Option 1 is the one worth arguing about*, and the argument turns on whether "a heading is created *with* a state" is a transition at all. If it is not — and a =State \"TODO\" from \"\"= line is arguably noise rather than history — then there is no exception, only a capture that finishes the job.

*Decided by the user 2026-08-31: option 1.* =org_capture= gains an =initial_state= argument and writes the keyword with the heading.

*The objection it overrides, and why that is sound.* Writing a keyword directly puts one state change outside the queue, and the queue is load-bearing because org's native state logging only completes inside an interactive command. But a *creation* has no prior state, so there is no transition to log: =State "TODO" from ""= is noise rather than history. Nothing is hidden from the review pass that the review pass wanted.

*What it fixes, measured:* every heading captured in a session is keywordless on disk until apply, so a =:BLOCKER:= naming one is inert, =bin/lint-org= errors, and =.githooks/pre-commit= refuses. That cost [[id:b36e6369-a210-4540-adc7-0c0c188e9791][b36e6369]] a commit on 2026-08-28 — the slice could not be committed until the queue was applied, because three of its own members were captured in the same session.

*Still queue-routed:* every *subsequent* transition. This is a creation-time argument only, and =org_set_todo= keeps its behaviour unchanged.

*Note for whoever builds it:* the argument should be optional and default to nothing, so a capture with no state still produces a keywordless heading. That is the current behaviour and some callers want it — a heading captured as a note rather than a task.


* TODO Three verification traps hit repeatedly in one session, none of them caught by a check :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       542924c1-9152-4414-8da8-d530d3423611
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 17:36]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 17:36] \\
  session learnings, filed
:END:

*Observed across one long session, 2026-08-28.* Each was hit more than once, each looked like success at the moment it happened, and none is covered by anything in =bin/=.

*1. An unescaped =\"= inside an elisp docstring, and =check-parens= says nothing.* Writing a docstring through a shell heredoc or a Python string puts a bare quote in, which *terminates the string early* and turns the rest of the body into code. Parens stay balanced, so =check-parens= reports the file fine. Hit twice — once in =config.el=, once in =config-test.el= — and the second time cost nine spurious test failures before the cause was found. The reliable check is not =check-parens= but *reading forms*: =(while t (forward-sexp))= from =point-min= reports the line where the reader actually gives up. Sibling of [[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]], where a bare =emacs= silently did not run a batch check at all.

*2. A count of =FAILED= lines is not a test result.* =bin/test= writes to *stderr*, so =bin/test | tail= shows nothing while exiting 0, and =grep -c FAILED= over a run that *never started* answers zero — which reads exactly like success. Hit twice: once piping without =2>&1=, once when a deliberately broken file made the suite exit *255* having run no tests, and the failure count was read as "no failures". The check that works is the *=Ran N tests=* line and the exit code, never the absence of a word.

*3. A mutation test whose mutation does not parse proves nothing.* This project's discipline is to break the code and confirm the test fails. That is only evidence if the broken version *runs*. Twice a break was made by deleting a clause, which unbalanced the file, so the suite did not execute and the run was scored as "the test still passes". A break must be *semantic*, not structural — invert a predicate, return nil, drop a value — and the run count must be checked before the failure count.

*What they share, and why it is one heading.* All three are cases where *the absence of a signal was read as a positive result* — no parse error, no FAILED line, no test failure. The project already has this lesson recorded twice ([[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]], and the =set -g= bug in =bin/check-org-dev-skill= where FAIL printed and the script exited 0), which is the argument that it is a class rather than three incidents.

*Proposed, not yet decided:* a =bin/check-elisp= that reads forms rather than counting parens, wired into =pre-commit= alongside =bin/lint-org=; and a note in the org-dev skill that the two verification commands whose output is *silence* — =check-parens= and a piped =bin/test= — are the two that have lied. [[id:d2a0f54c-b59a-4855-ae3d-c93a6d3aa177][d2a0f54c]] is the natural home for the first, since it already proposes generalising =check-org-dev-skill= into a =check-conventions= wired into =pre-commit=.

*A fourth, 2026-08-31, and it is the purest form of the class.* A hand-written probe asked whether a heading carried a =:PLAN:= drawer:

: (save-excursion (re-search-forward "^:PLAN:" end t) t)

The trailing =t= is the last form of the =save-excursion=, so the whole expression answers =t= *whether or not the search matched*. **A probe that can only say yes.** It reported a =:PLAN:= drawer on [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]], which has none, and that false reading was then used to argue a precedent for wrapping slice bodies — a design decision resting on a probe with no failure mode.

*Caught by a human reading the file*, not by anything mechanical: the user said "we didn't do it as such for c44c21 and it reads just fine". Every one of the four in this heading was caught that way or by a lint rule; none by re-reading the code.

*The generalisation, now that there are four:* the failures are not "the check was wrong" but "**the check had no way to fail**". A silent parse, an absent =FAILED= line, a mutation that never ran, a probe whose value is constant. The question that catches all four is the same one — *what would this have printed if the thing I am testing were false?* — and it is cheaper to ask than any of the fixes.

*Which is also the argument for =bin/check-elisp=* proposed above: a check that reads forms cannot answer =t= by accident.

*And a fifth of the same shape, from the same day, worth recording because it was a write rather than a read.* A splice replaced a region using =body.index(start)= and =body.index(end)= where the end marker sat *earlier* in the string than the start — so =body[:start] + new + body[end:]= silently re-appended everything between them, duplicating a slice's revision links and part of its member list. The cookie went up rather than erroring, which read as plausible on a slice that had just grown. A splice whose markers can be mis-ordered should assert their order; nothing did.


* TODO Remove queue-append's synthetic-event overrides, dead since the ExitPlanMode hook went :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       46d225c7-7541-4867-af72-929a572df2a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 16:02]
:CATEGORY: Queue
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 16:02] \\
  dead branch, filed on its own
:END:

=QUEUE_APPEND_ID=, =QUEUE_APPEND_STATE= and =QUEUE_APPEND_FROM= in =bin/hooks/queue-append= let a caller supply a heading and states for a *synthetic* event — one with no tool call behind it, so nothing to read a =tool_input= or parse a =tool_response= from. Their only consumer was =bin/hooks/exitplanmode-promote-planning=, deleted 2026-08-28 when =PLANNING= was retired ([[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]). The branch has been dead since.

*Left in place that day on purpose, and the reason still holds:* =queue-append= is on the path of *every* hook in the project, so an unrelated edit to the queue writer made while doing something else is a worse risk than a dead branch. This is that edit, done deliberately and on its own.

*What it touches:* the three =override_*= variables, their use in the =jq= object below, and whichever assertions in =bin/queue-append-test= exercise them. The test currently passes, so removing the feature must break it — if it does not, the feature was never covered and that is the more interesting finding.

*Filed 2026-08-28 to make a checked-in comment true.* When the branch was marked dead, the note ended "Removal is filed." It was not: no heading mentioned it, and the only other traces were a debrief paragraph and a commit message — both places CLAUDE.md explicitly says residue goes to die. The rule it broke is the one about closing a heading: *a sentence pointing at another heading is not a filing action*. Writing that sentence into source, where it reads as a promise to the next person, is the same error one layer worse.


* MAYBE Install orgparse for ad-hoc analysis, and stop reaching for regex over org text :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       2c5f7c50-40c9-4d06-b435-add41eb97643
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 15:58]
:CATEGORY: Dev
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "MAYBE"      from              [2026-08-28 Fri 15:58] \\
  offered; not yet installed or decided
:END:

*Offered by the user 2026-08-28:* [[https://github.com/karlicoss/orgparse][orgparse]] is available and could be installed system-wide for Claude to use from Python. Not installed as of that date; =python3= here is 3.14.6.

*The evidence for it is two bugs from a single session, both the same shape.* Extracting a heading's =:ID:= by regex returned =\"...\"== — the pattern matched a line in the *previous* heading's body. And a scan for level-1 headings lacking =:CREATED:= walked *into* the datetree, so the anchor inherited a day node's timestamp; it reported *0* when the answer was *1*, and was caught only because the number contradicted something seen ten minutes earlier. Both are entry-boundary errors, which is precisely the class a real parser removes.

*The sharper finding is about practice rather than the library, and it is the reason this is =MAYBE= rather than =TODO=.* Emacs was the better instrument in both cases and was under-used: =org-map-entries= bounds entries by construction, and it is the *authoritative* parser — the one that actually writes these files. A Python parser is a second implementation of org's semantics that can disagree with the implementation of record, which is the "second copy that can disagree" this slice exists to close. The org-dev skill's §0b rule already says to check what org has before reaching elsewhere, and =python3= was reached for roughly forty times in that session without the question being asked once.

*So the split that would make it worth installing:*

- *Emacs for anything semantic* — entry boundaries, property inheritance, keyword sets, clock arithmetic, agenda behaviour. It is running, and org's own answer is the answer.
- *orgparse for where Emacs is not the right instrument*: when it is down (it was, that morning), whole-corpus counting without touching a live buffer, and transformations worth diffing on a scratch copy before they go near the real file — the [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] taxonomy pass was exactly that.

*One boundary, cheap to state now and expensive to retrofit: nothing checked into =bin/= imports it without declaring it.* This repo has *zero* Python today — every script is bash, fish or elisp — so it would be a new dependency class, and [[id:1ed7b2b4-a809-4547-b04f-099a3111fc1e][1ed7b2b4]] is the standing scar about an undeclared one. Ad-hoc session analysis is free; a committed script would need a declaration and a check in =bin/check-org-dev-skill= beside the org-version one.

*If it is installed*, the org-dev skill should record both halves — that orgparse exists, and that Emacs remains the answer wherever org's own semantics decide the result. Recording only the first half would turn a tool that fixes a bug class into a tool that introduces a worse one.


* TODO Two keyword-set proposals and the review collision are still unadopted :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       dbf70459-bde4-4550-bd81-0d4f9ebc50e3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 13:39]
:CATEGORY: Skill
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 13:40] \\
  residue carried out of c954f650
:END:

*Filed 2026-08-28 as [[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]] closed.* That heading asked whether the whole keyword progression earns its keep, answered the =PLANNING= half by retiring it, and left three questions open. They are carried out here rather than left in a closed heading's =:PLAN:= drawer, which readers are told to skip — the exact path to invisibility CLAUDE.md's filing rule exists to block.

*1. Get =MAYBE= out of the default agenda without moving it after the =|=.* At the 2026-08-10 census it was 23 of 56 keyworded headings, the largest state — a healthy Someday/Maybe list rendered *actionable* by sitting before the separator. GTD is emphatic that Someday/Maybe lives outside the active system and is reviewed periodically. Moving it past the =|= would be semantically wrong, since these are not finished; Bernt Hansen's answer is to keep such states active and filter them from default agenda views. Worth re-counting before deciding — the corpus has roughly tripled since.

*2. Narrow =WAITING= to "waiting on a person".* GTD separates *Waiting For* — delegated, needs chasing — from *blocked*, a dependency. Dependencies here are already structural, via =:BLOCKER:=, so the current gloss overlaps it. Recorded when the keyword had never been used once; that is no longer true, so the evidence has changed and should be re-read rather than reused.

*3. The word "review" means three things.* The =:review:= tag (a kind of work), =claude-code-ide-org-review= (the command that applies the queue), and the =REVIEW= state. "The review buffer shows a REVIEW item tagged =:review:=" is a sentence someone will have to write. Either pick a different word for one of them or adopt the collision deliberately and say so in CLAUDE.md — the one thing not to do is leave it unremarked, which is where it stands.

*Note what is no longer open, so this is not re-litigated.* Whether =DOING= should exist at all was *settled* in c954f650 and the reasoning is in its =:PLAN:= drawer: the org+GTD tradition argues against an in-progress keyword because the clock can say what is active, and that substitute is exactly what the event queue removes here. A future reader citing Bernt or GTD has a well-supported argument and is wrong for this system specifically. =REVIEW='s adoption and =PLANNING='s retirement are likewise decided.


* TODO Sort TODO.org by :CREATED: descending, after each archive pass :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5f1068f9-1452-45e4-9253-568edfce2053
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 13:39]
:CATEGORY: Meta
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 13:40] \\
  for the next slice
:END:

*Asked for by the user 2026-08-28, for the next slice.* After archiving the closed headings, sort what remains in TODO.org by =:CREATED:= descending, so the file reads newest-first.

*It became a one-operation job when the category tier went.* [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]]'s plan already named this and could not do it: sorting used to mean nine separate operations, one inside each level-1 category, with no ordering between them. Flattened, every task is a level-1 sibling and the whole file is one =org-sort-entries= call.

*Where it belongs is the load-bearing part.* The ceremony order is fixed for a reason — apply the queue, consolidate drawers, normalise separation, *then* archive — and this goes *after* archiving, for the same reason archiving goes last: sorting before it would order rows that are about to be removed. It is also idempotent and cheap once the file is already sorted, so it can join the ceremony rather than being a thing to remember.

*Two things to settle when building it, neither obvious:*

- *What to do with headings that have no =:CREATED:=.* Every heading created since the convention has one and =bin/lint-org= errors without it, but archived-in and hand-written rows historically did not — the same gap [[id:b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a][b7b46a26]] is about for =CLOSED:=. Sorting must not silently pile them at one end as though they were coeval.
- *The datetree anchor must not move or be sorted into.* =* Review and planning= is the one structural level-1 heading left, and its year/month/day children have their own ordering question ([[id:0c3f1319-67c0-4b29-a8e7-5ffa12948aaf][0c3f1319]], newest-at-top). Whatever sorts the file has to skip it.

*Adjacent, and worth doing in the same pass rather than twice:* [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]] reorders DONE.org as a datetree descending by =CLOSED:=. Both are "make the file read newest-first after archiving"; they differ only in the key and in whether the result is flat or nested.

*Measured 2026-08-28, and two of the three anticipated exceptions do not exist.*

- *Recurring ceremonies are not an exception.* All three =SCHEDULED= headings — =96a51c2f=, =2aff1e6d=, =cbe282ec= — carry a =:CREATED:= as well. The worry that they would have "no CREATED, rather SCHEDULED" does not hold against this corpus.
- *The datetree anchor is the only level-1 heading without =:CREATED:=.* Exactly one, not a class.
- *And it needs no special handling at all.* Verified in batch org 9.8.7: =(org-sort-entries nil ?R nil nil "CREATED")= with point before the first heading sorts the level-1 entries descending and places the *undated* heading last, on its own. Subtrees travel intact.

*So the whole operation is one call, and org owns the comparator.* =?R= is "by the value of a property, reversed", and the =PROPERTY= argument takes =CREATED= directly — no =getkey-func=, no custom compare. Two preconditions worth knowing rather than discovering: point must be *before* the first heading (org signals ="Nothing to sort"= otherwise, which is how a fixture without a =#+= preamble failed here), and TODO.org satisfies that only because of its header lines.

*The "mostly sorted, so a light operation" premise is right eventually and wrong now.* Measured: of 135 dated level-1 headings, *85 of 134 adjacent pairs are already descending — 63%*, and the longest already-descending run is *17*. The first sort is a large reordering and should be its own commit. Afterwards the premise holds, because =org_capture= prepends and a promotion inserts one heading, so each later sort moves one or two entries.

*Approaches, with what each costs:*

| # | approach | for | against |
|---+----------+-----+---------|
| A | the single =?R= call, anchor last by absence | one line; org owns the comparator; the exception handles itself; idempotent | the anchor's position is *implicit* — it is last because a property is missing, not because anyone said so, and an anchor that ever gained a =:CREATED:= would migrate into the middle silently |
| B | A, plus a sentinel =:CREATED:= on the anchor so it sorts last deliberately | the invariant is declared rather than emergent; the lint's "level-1 task has no =:CREATED:=" rule would then apply uniformly | a fabricated timestamp in a file whose entire value is that timestamps are trustworthy — the shape [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] retired rather than kept |
| C | move the datetree to its own file | TODO.org becomes homogeneous, every heading a dated task; no exception anywhere; =--datetree-node-role= stops sharing a file with real tasks | a third tracked file for =org-agenda-files=, =--tracked-files=, id resolution and the archive target; every doc saying "two files" becomes wrong; and A already handles the anchor for free, so this buys tidiness rather than function |
| D | insert in place on capture/promotion, never sort | no bulk reorder ever; minimal diffs forever | =org_capture= already prepends, which is correct for a newest-first file, so this is only needed for *promotions*, which are rare — and it spreads ordering logic across every writer instead of one call |
| E | sort the view, not the file | zero churn | the stated goal is that the *file* reads newest-first, and capture's prepend already assumes file order carries meaning |

*Recommend A, with B's concern answered by a lint assertion rather than by a fake timestamp:* assert that the =:DATE_TREE:= anchor is the last level-1 heading. That makes the invariant checked without inventing data, which is the same move [[id:8bea4317-c8e7-4939-8224-834bb914d89c][8bea4317]] made for the cookie's position — read the corpus, assert the result, do not encode it as data.

*C stays open on its own merits*, and is worth deciding separately rather than as a side effect of sorting: it is really the question of whether meta-work belongs in the same file as the work, which nothing here has asked yet.

*Decided by the user 2026-08-31: approach A.* One call — =(org-sort-entries nil ?R nil nil "CREATED")= with point before the first heading — and the datetree anchor sorts last on its own, because it carries no =:CREATED:=.

*With A's one real objection answered by a check rather than by data.* The anchor is last by an *absence*, which nobody decided; so =bin/lint-org= asserts that the =:DATE_TREE:= anchor is the final level-1 heading. That makes the invariant checked without inventing a timestamp, which is the move [[id:8bea4317-c8e7-4939-8224-834bb914d89c][8bea4317]] made for the cookie's position: read the corpus, assert the result, do not encode it as data.

*Approach C stays open and is not foreclosed.* Moving the datetree to its own file is a question about whether meta-work belongs in the same file as the work, and deciding it as a side effect of a sort would be deciding it by accident. Nothing in A makes C harder later.

*Sequencing reminder from the measurement above:* the *first* sort is a large reordering — 63% of adjacent pairs are already descending, longest run 17 of 135 — so it wants its own commit. Every later run moves one or two entries, because =org_capture= prepends.

*Asked for by the user 2026-08-28*, on seeing [[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]'s body: "absolutely monstrous... I guess it's because it predates the current task lifecycle and we never touched it." That diagnosis is right and it generalises — the =:PLAN:= lifecycle ([[id:8bcd56f4-22a8-4673-bad6-a99433027b81][8bcd56f4]]) applies at =DONE=, so a *live* heading that has been accumulating for weeks has never met it.

*Measured 2026-08-28 across TODO.org: 90 live keyworded headings hold 4,027 body lines, and the ten longest hold 1,593 of them — 39%.* None carries a =:PLAN:= drawer.

| lines | id | kw | created | title |
|-------+----+----+---------+-------|
| 383 | [[id:02aaae22-0958-4c0f-b5bb-2cbea32416ae][02aaae22]] | DOING | 2026-08-06 | [7/11] Update CLAUDE.md and the org skill for the new architecture |
| 219 | [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] | TODO | 2026-08-15 | Record turns-per-interval as an attention signal |
| 177 | [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]] | NEXT | 2026-08-15 | Make the org skill and its discipline portable to other repos |
| 145 | [[id:b5f94b88-76e3-4a3c-ac73-729f34d33691][b5f94b88]] | TODO | 2026-08-13 | Let a session interject without waiting |
| 141 | [[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] | MAYBE | 2026-08-07 | Let the review command ask Claude to explain the ambiguous gaps |
| 127 | [[id:2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c][2aeb65d6]] | TODO | 2026-08-12 | Concurrent sessions share one git index |
| 105 | [[id:a1d63f52-4c07-4b9e-8f31-6d2b90c4e7a5][a1d63f52]] | TODO | 2026-08-10 | [3/5] Institute a catch-all heading for cross-cutting meta-work |
| 99 | [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]] | TODO | 2026-08-18 | Heading bodies grow into narratives no human will read |
| 99 | [[id:2d9eeebd-4375-4790-8485-92b244236a21][2d9eeebd]] | NEXT | 2026-08-14 | Read a heading's front matter, and take a partial :ID: |
| 98 | [[id:d1cf852a-696a-4195-a4a7-039439c1f4b7][d1cf852a]] | TODO | 2026-08-13 | Bracket queued spans with WAIT transitions |

*The eighth row is the joke and also the point.* [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]] — "Heading bodies grow into narratives no human will read; decide what to stop capturing" — is itself 99 lines. Whatever this heading does should be reconciled with that one rather than done past it: that one asks what to *stop capturing*, this one asks what to do with what already accumulated. They are the prospective and retrospective halves of the same complaint.

*What worked on c954f650, for whoever picks this up.* Compress *first*, while the text is still body, then wrap. CLAUDE.md's rule says wrap first and condense in a separate commit, on the grounds that a bad pare inside =:PLAN:= is invisible because readers are told to skip the drawer. Compressing before wrapping inverts that: the pare shows up in the diff as ordinary body prose, where it is reviewable. The rule's *concern* is right and its *order* may be backwards — worth deciding here rather than assuming either way. 305 lines went to roughly 70 without losing an open question.

*The three things the rule says stay untouched still stayed:* every open question kept verbatim, the debrief kept whole, and anything whose seam was uncertain wrapped rather than pared.

*Not a backlog sweep.* [[id:cbe282ec-10c3-4aa0-8d3a-f30e17a12fa8][cbe282ec]] chose "wrap unedited" for the 30-heading archive pass deliberately, to keep it cheap. This is the opposite case by construction — ten headings, each one read and condensed, all of them live and therefore still being consulted, which is what makes the cost worth paying here and not there.

*Corrected by the user 2026-08-28, and the correction is narrower and better than what it replaces.* The paragraph above argued that compressing before wrapping may invert CLAUDE.md's order in general, on reviewability grounds. That overreaches. The order inverts *for this population specifically*, and the reason is a property of these ten bodies rather than of the rule:

*They were never revised as they were worked — only appended to.* Which is exactly what [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]] is about, and why that heading sits eighth on this list rather than merely adjacent to it. So what "compression" does here is not *paring considered prose*, which is the risky act CLAUDE.md's separate-commit rule guards against. It is *collapsing an append-only transcript*, where later entries routinely supersede earlier ones outright and the superseded text is not a judgement anyone made — it is a sentence nobody went back to delete.

*So the rule stands unchanged for its own case.* A body that was drafted and revised should still be wrapped first and condensed separately, because a pare there discards someone's considered wording where no reader will see it. A body that is a transcript can be condensed first, because the diff is where the supersession becomes visible and there is no earlier judgement being overruled — only an earlier sentence that a later one already replaced.

*Which gives whoever picks this up a test rather than a preference:* ask whether the body was ever *revised*. If it was only ever appended to, compress first. c954f650 was a clear case — five distinct dates of accretion, each opening with a bold lead that qualified or reversed one above it, and nothing ever deleted.

*Measured 2026-08-31, and it converts this from ten judgements into nine mechanical compressions and one that needs care.*

This heading's own test is "ask whether the body was ever *revised*; if it was only ever appended to, compress first". That is answerable from git rather than by reading. Sampling 28 points across all 472 commits that touched TODO.org, with each heading's body bounded at the *next heading of any level*, and counting any drop of more than three lines as a revision:

| id | samples | max | now | shrinks | verdict |
|----+---------+-----+-----+---------+---------|
| 02aaae22 | 25 | 154 | 147 | 1 | *revised* |
| 9e627dc0 | 16 | 211 | 211 | 0 | append-only |
| 9ae4b17e | 17 | 169 | 169 | 0 | append-only |
| b5f94b88 | 20 | 128 | 128 | 0 | append-only |
| 0c8644ff | 25 | 141 | 141 | 0 | append-only |
| 2aeb65d6 | 21 | 123 | 123 | 0 | append-only |
| a1d63f52 | 24 | 94 | 94 | 0 | append-only |
| b07df584 | 13 | 95 | 95 | 0 | append-only |
| 2d9eeebd | 17 | 111 | 111 | 0 | append-only |
| d1cf852a | 19 | 90 | 90 | 0 | append-only |

*Nine of ten have never lost a line.* So for nine, the risky question — am I discarding someone's considered wording? — is answered *no* before any reading begins, and compress-then-wrap applies without a per-heading call. Writing each compression is still work; deciding whether it is safe is not.

*=02aaae22= is the exception and should be done last, or by a human.* It is also the only container in the list, so its single shrink may be a child moving rather than prose being pared — which is exactly the ambiguity that makes it the one to treat carefully rather than the one to skip.

*The first version of this measurement was wrong in two ways and is recorded because both are the same mistake.* It sampled only the most recent 40 commits — 2026-08-27 onward, five days of a five-week history — and it bounded each body with =find("\\n* ")=, which for a heading *with children* runs past them, so =02aaae22= came out as 5 lines against a true 178. Both errors flattered the answer: a shorter window and a truncated body both hide shrinks. A measurement whose errors all point the same way is worth re-running before it is quoted.


* TODO Assign advances past the line the human is about to mark :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a2509a61-4e21-4d8e-8f58-c87935753472
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 12:56]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 13:04] \\
  filed from the user's review-buffer request
:END:

*Requested by the user 2026-08-28, from live review use:* after =a= assigns a span, point should *not* advance — "usually the next action is to mark it". And an alternative worth weighing rather than assuming: have =a= simply *mark and advance*, if that turns out simpler or more idiomatic.

*This contests a deliberate decision, not an oversight, which is why the reasoning matters.* =claude-code-ide-org-review-assign= ends with =(claude-code-ide-org--review-redraw item t)= under the comment *"Advance: assigning **answers** the question this line posed."* That is a coherent model — an unassigned span is a question, answering it retires the line — and it is the wrong model for what the human does next. Assigning does not finish the item; it makes it *markable*. The command's own docstring says so two paragraphs earlier: "Assigning clears =:unassigned=, so the line stops reading as a question and the item becomes markable like any other." The code advances past exactly the line it has just made actionable, and the docstring and the comment have disagreed all along.

*The dired vocabulary the buffer deliberately borrows is the argument for the second option.* The keymap's own comment records that =m=/=u= advance "because that is the muscle memory a person arrives with". In dired the keys that advance are the ones that *record a decision about this line* — and under the corrected model, assigning is a decision about the line rather than a completion of it. So either:

- *=a= stops advancing*, leaving point on the now-markable line, and =m= advances as it always has. Two keystrokes, matching dired exactly. Smallest change.
- *=a= marks and advances*, collapsing the pair. One keystroke, but it makes assignment imply approval — and a human who wanted to assign, look at the interval, and *then* decide has lost the ability to.

*Recommend the first, weakly.* The second is fewer keystrokes for the common path but silently couples two decisions the buffer keeps separate everywhere else, and the review pass exists precisely so a human judges each item. Worth deciding against real use rather than from here.

*Adjacent, and already closed:* [[id:9e80a32d-43e1-4647-a42c-cba10f6e79cc][9e80a32d]] fixed assign and interval-edit sending point to the *top* of the buffer. This is the residue of that — point is no longer thrown away, but it still moves one line further than the human wants.

*Filed late, and the delay is the interesting part.* The capture *queued* rather than writing through, so the heading did not exist and =org_amend= refused it by name — "apply the queue, then amend", which is the guard working. The body was written to the session scratchpad in the meantime rather than promised, and landed unchanged once the pass ran.


* TODO A slice should list incidental work separately from its planned members :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       0086614a-c95c-4618-bba0-c821589dd7f2
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 12:52]
:CATEGORY: Slices
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 12:53] \\
  filed from the user's proposal
:END:

*Proposed by the user 2026-08-28*, after a scan of [[id:979e02b6-2a4c-4328-8b08-ffaec03bd02e][979e02b6]] found 36 headings closed since it opened, of which only 6 were members. Their correction, and it stands: *work that arose in the course of the slice is slice business* — listing it separately as *incidental* keeps that true without pretending it was planned.

*What this fixes is the denominator.* [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]] closed at =[27/27]=, and its own body records that its members were "mostly small defects found while doing other work". So the slice absorbed its incidentals *as members*, and 27-of-27 reads as a plan executed exactly when it was a much smaller plan plus nineteen things that fell out of doing it. Those are different facts and the single number hides the more interesting one — how much unplanned work a plan generates.

*Mechanics, following the rules a slice already has:*

- *A second list, under an =Incidental:= lead*, below the member list.
- *Plain =- = items with no checkbox*, exactly as a cancelled or deferred member is written. Incidentals are closed by definition, so a checkbox could only ever be =[X]= and would inflate both halves of the cookie. The cookie keeps meaning *plan completion*, which is the only thing it can mean and stay useful.
- *Not in the =:BLOCKER:=*, which names cookie-carrying members and must not gate a slice on work already finished.
- *Same link form as a member* — full-UUID target, 8-character description — with keyword and title as derived text alongside.

*The open choice, and it is the whole design.* How the set is picked:

| | rule | cost |
|--+------+------|
| *strict window* | every heading closed between the slice's =:CREATED:= and its close that is not a member | none to compute, zero judgement, cannot drift — but sweeps in genuinely unrelated workstreams |
| *curated* | the subset someone judges related | needs a judgement per heading, and a judgement that is re-made differently each time is the thing this project keeps getting wrong |

*Recommend the strict window*, and the recommendation is not about which list is prettier. An incidental list is *emergent*, not declared: it is derivable from the closing timestamps, so by this project's own axis it should be *detected* rather than written down, and a derived thing must have a rule that needs no opinion. The curated version reintroduces exactly the hand-maintained second copy that [[id:979e02b6-2a4c-4328-8b08-ffaec03bd02e][979e02b6]] exists to eliminate. That the strict window sweeps in the ceremony and footnote stories is a feature: it says truthfully what else was going on, which is the question the list answers.

*Which makes this generated, not maintained.* =claude-code-ide-org-refresh-slice= should compute the incidental list the same way it already regenerates member lines — on every refresh, from the corpus, discarding whatever was there. A hand-kept incidental list would go stale by the second day.

*Measured on the live slice 2026-08-28:* 36 closed in the window, 6 members, *30 incidental*. Five of the thirty are children of [[id:478d6ec9-1b99-4c6c-88e9-c793ad4b2f22][478d6ec9]], which the [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] plan named as one of its two prerequisites — its sibling prerequisite [[id:8183fc7c-905e-40c8-81cb-10c56158c6ce][8183fc7c]] *is* a member, so identical work is tracked one way and invisible the other. That asymmetry is what prompted the scan.

*Decided by the user 2026-08-28, and it overrules the recommendation above on one point.*

*Strict window: yes*, for the reason given — the rule needs no opinion, so the list cannot drift.

*Checkbox cookies: kept on incidental items too, and the denominator grows.* The "keeps the denominator honest" argument above is overruled as splitting hairs, and on reflection it was arguing for a distinction nobody asked the cookie to make. The user's reading is the simpler and more defensible one: *=[n/m]= says what was closed between =:CREATED:= and =CLOSED:=*, which is a clear statement about a bounded window rather than a claim about plan fidelity. A separate "plan completion" number would be a second statistic to keep true, and the whole point of the strict window is to avoid maintaining anything.

*The user's assumption — that splitting the list in two has no effect on the aggregate cookie — was verified rather than accepted.* Batch org 9.8.7, a heading with two sibling lists separated by a prose line, 3 planned (2 done) and 2 incidental (2 done):

: * TODO [4/5] A slice

*And =:COOKIE_DATA:= is not what makes that work*, which matters because the slice convention mandates it and a reader would credit it. The same shape with *no* =:COOKIE_DATA:= at all also yields =[4/5]=: org counts every checkbox in the entry regardless, and =checkbox recursive= governs *nested* lists and subheadings, not sibling ones. So the two-list layout needs nothing added.

*One consequence that still needs deciding, because it is an invariant rather than a preference.* =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= says a slice "carries a =:BLOCKER:= naming exactly its cookie-carrying members", and =bin/lint-org= reports a disagreement in *either* direction as an error. If incidentals now carry cookies, that rule pulls them into the blocker — roughly forty ids on this slice, every one of them already closed and therefore inert. Two ways out:

- *Narrow the rule to planned members*: "naming exactly its cookie-carrying **planned** members". The blocker keeps meaning "what still has to finish", which is its only job.
- *Leave the rule alone* and let the blocker carry the closed ids too. Harmless — =org-depend= blocks only on unfinished work — but it makes a machine-readable property forty entries long to express eleven facts.

Recommend the first. The blocker is not a record of membership; the checkbox list is.

*Inherits [[id:9651d4c8-6887-4b0a-91d9-562c3bc49a75][9651d4c8]], cancelled 2026-08-29 as superseded by this heading.* That one asked for a convention marking which of a *container's* children were in its original plan and which accrued during execution. The planned/incidental split answers the same question one level up — if the slice is the planning unit rather than the container, a container's children were never covered by its plan in the first place.

*So this heading now carries a capability that exists nowhere else*, which raises its stakes: until it is built, original-versus-accrued has no home in the files at all. That is why 9651d4c8 was cancelled as *superseded* rather than as unnecessary.

*The case study travels with it.* [[id:406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4][406e9200]] was planned with five children and had ten two days later, and its plan file still opens "Covers all five children" — true when written, false since, recorded once in a document nobody revises. Any convention that requires editing the container each time a child is added will drift exactly as that plan file did, which is the argument for the strict window: it is *derived* from closing timestamps and so cannot go stale.

*Proposed by the user 2026-08-28, and no heading existed for it* — checked before filing. Three neighbours touch it and none is this:

- [[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] judged the structure question 2026-08-17 as *"keep both"* — two files rather than one. That settles the *file* question and says nothing about DONE.org's internal order, which is what this is.
- [[id:4c0044bf-1b6c-44ea-807a-8f9e3523e3fd][4c0044bf]] is =CANCELLED=, but it is where the org fact lives: =org-archive-location= supports a =datetree/= target (=org-archive.el:284=).
- [[id:0c3f1319-67c0-4b29-a8e7-5ffa12948aaf][0c3f1319]] is this same problem for the *meta-work* datetree, and its measured finding should transfer: no custom sort function is needed, =(org-sort-entries nil ?A)= on the month node gives descending order directly.

*The retcon the user anticipated is a hard prerequisite, not a tidiness item, and the mechanism says why.* =org-archive-subtree= dates its datetree entry from =(or (org-entry-get nil "CLOSED" t) time)= — measured and recorded in [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]]. A heading with no =CLOSED:= therefore files under *today*, not under an unknown date, and nothing warns. Run against the file as it stands, that is 39 headings silently collapsing into one wrong day node.

*Measured 2026-08-28: 39 of DONE.org's 97 finished headings carry no =CLOSED:=.* But the work is smaller than that implies, because a third of it was already done and audited:

| population | n | source |
|------------+---+--------|
| already covered by [[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]]'s completion-time manifest | 13 | =git log --reverse -S=, its T2 tier |
| not covered — archived before the 2026-08-17 sweep | 26 | nothing yet |

The 13 are exactly that manifest's git-derived rows; its other 28 rows were T1 (an in-file =State= line) and were consumed by [[id:f4b07fc0-187a-4975-aa3b-b9bd4b1c0799][f4b07fc0]]'s backfill. The 26 are the earliest headings in the project — the five original tool headings (=org_query=, =org_clock_report=, =org_capture=, =org_refile=, =org_sort_children=), the hook and trigger batch, =Set git identity=. They predate the =!= cookies in =#+TODO:=, so no in-file evidence exists and =git log --reverse -S= is the only source.

*One trap already found and fixed once, in the manifest's own body, worth not rediscovering:* the in-file scan must be scoped to a heading's *own* body. Scanning the subtree dates a parent from one of its children — =b5f7c5c7= came out two days early that way, and it would have mis-ordered the two largest entries with nothing downstream to notice.

*Blocked on [[id:b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a][b7b46a26]], which is being reopened rather than duplicated.* It was =CANCELLED= 2026-08-24 with the note "nothing consumes =CLOSED:= on those headings" — and this heading is exactly that consumer, so the ground for cancelling it is void. Its body already holds the two questions that still have to be answered first: whether a commit-derived =CLOSED:= is worth having at all (a commit timestamp dates when a change was *recorded*, which in this project can be far from when work stopped, and [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] is the precedent for refusing a plausible guess), and if so how the approximation is marked so a reader can tell the 39 from the 58 that are exact.

*A known consequence, not an objection.* A second datetree in DONE.org is something =bin/lint-org= does not currently cover — its datetree rule is written for the one in TODO.org. That was foreseen while shipping the rule; see the child heading "bin/lint-org will drown in datetree scaffolding it can never satisfy", which measured it hours after the rule landed.


* TODO Apply logs a self-transition when a queued event's target already matches reality :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       05c71d99-2839-447b-a536-60eb4bf43b73
:CREATED:  [2026-08-28 Fri 07:56]
:CATEGORY: Apply
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-28 Fri 07:57] \\
  filed from the decisions pass
:END:

*Measured across both files 2026-08-28:* 4 of 463 =State= lines record a transition to the keyword the heading already held — 0.9%. Both =DONE -> DONE= cases are dated [2026-08-11 Tue], the day the staleness guard shipped, and they sit on =Build the interactive review-and-apply Emacs command= and — pointedly — on =Apply must not replay a state event that reality has moved past=, the guard's own heading. Since the guard: *2 in 17 days* — =TODO -> TODO= on 420816ec [2026-08-20], =DOING -> DOING= on 29439196 [2026-08-27].

*The staleness guard is blind to this by construction, not by oversight.* =--review-state-stale-p= asks whether reality moved *away* from what the event expected:

#+begin_src elisp
(and (not (eq current 'unresolved))
     (not (equal (claude-code-ide-org--review-normalize-from from) current)))
#+end_src

A self-transition is the one shape where the answer is emphatically no. =from= matches reality perfectly, which is exactly why it sails through — the event is a *correctly-non-stale no-op*.

*Cause.* Two events assert the same target. Post-cutover nothing moves an org file until apply moves it, so every =org_set_todo= in a batch reads =from= off an unmoved file; =--review-projected-staleness= then projects the batch forward and the second event sees =from= = projected current = =to=. On 29439196 the pair was almost certainly the =ExitPlanMode= hook's queued =todo DOING= plus an explicit =org_set_todo DOING= for the same heading.

*The fix is not in the staleness guard and should not be called one.* Stale means the event is *dangerous* — it would regress the record. This means the event is *redundant* — it would change nothing but still writes a log line. Conflating them would either let real staleness through or teach the reader that the flag means "harmless". Separate test, comparing =to= against the projected current state, skipping the item rather than refusing it.

*Worth deciding at the same time:* whether a skipped no-op is reported in the review buffer or silently dropped. Dropping is defensible — nothing was lost — but a queued event that vanishes without trace is the shape this project has repeatedly regretted.

*Related:* [[id:2d100d0d-2d04-43b9-af1e-f4d6b52bbada][2d100d0d]] is the same class of gap one layer down — a check that has to run *across* items in a batch rather than within one item.

*The queue-side half of this already shipped, and the dates say it does not cover this case.* [[id:cc0c17a7-a554-426d-afeb-09cbee7339f0][cc0c17a7]] — "A no-op TODO -> TODO transition should never be queued" — closed [2026-08-20 Thu 07:38], having taken the first of the two options its own body laid out: "=org_set_todo= could refuse to queue when =from= equals =to=", with the second, "apply could treat a no-op as satisfied rather than failed", left as defensible-but-not-done.

The =420816ec= self-transition is stamped [2026-08-20 Thu 07:49] — *eleven minutes after that refusal shipped.* So this is not the same defect recurring; it is the half cc0c17a7 explicitly deferred, arriving within the hour.

*Why the queue-side guard cannot reach it.* It compares =from= to =to= at *queue* time, against a snapshot of a file that then does not move until apply. The case here is a heading that arrives at =to= *in between* — because another event in the same batch lands it there, or a hand-edit does. At queue time the event is not a no-op by any test available then; it becomes one before it is applied. And org writes the state it *observes* as the =from= on the log line, not the one the event recorded, which is why the artifact reads =from "TODO"= even where the queued event said otherwise.

So cc0c17a7 owns "do not queue an event that is already a no-op" and this owns "do not apply an event that has *become* one". Same defect, two clocks, and only the second needs the projection =--review-projected-staleness= already computes.

:PROPERTIES:

:CREATED:  [2026-08-04 Tue 18:27]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from "MAYBE"      [2026-08-14 Fri 13:51] \\
  confirmed surviving; three dependents
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/clock-marker-desync-guard.md][Plan]]

Captured 2026-08-04, hit live during this session's TODO.org commit-
splitting work: writing TODO.org's content directly via =git
hash-object=/=update-index= (bypassing Emacs) and relying on
=global-auto-revert-mode= to pick up the change desynced Emacs's
*in-memory* clock state from the file text. =org-clock-marker= kept
pointing at its old raw character offset, which after the rewrite
landed on an unrelated =:SESSIONS:= line instead of the =CLOCK:= line
— =org_clock_out= then failed outright with "Clock start time is
gone", and the statusline (which reads live =org-clocking-p=/
=org-clock-marker= state, not the file text — see
=claude-code-ide-org--statusline-task-string=) kept showing "clocked
in" even after a text-level recovery via
=claude-code-ide-org-close-open-interval= had already closed the
=CLOCK:= line on disk.

Worked around manually this time: reset
=org-clock-marker=/=org-clock-hd-marker=/=org-clock-start-time=
directly via =emacsclient=, then called
=claude-code-ide-org--clock-status-hook-out= by hand since normal
=org-clock-out= never got far enough to fire it. The existing "Stale
interval recovery" design (CLAUDE.md) only repairs the *text* side of
a stale interval (crash/shutdown case, where Emacs's own in-memory
state is simply gone after a restart) — it has no equivalent for *this*
case, where Emacs is still running but its live clock state has gone
stale relative to a file it didn't itself edit. Whether the real fix
is "never write live org files outside Emacs, full stop" (a
process/discipline fix, not code) or something more automatic (e.g.
detecting the marker/text mismatch and self-healing) is a question for
planning.

*Second, distinct incident, 2026-08-05*: on
[[id:4cda6bf7-441b-47b2-b3d5-b8c7e7a207a5][Show the TODO keyword, not just clocked in/out, in the statusline]],
after a cross-session clock resume (see the =d150c02e= sibling below)
followed by a proper =org_clock_out=, the =:LOGBOOK:= briefly
contained a genuinely malformed line — bare =CLOCK:= with *no*
timestamp at all — sitting between two valid entries, before
=claude-code-ide-org-consolidate-history= rebuilt the drawer and it
disappeared (the arithmetic of what remained checks out, so nothing
was silently lost, but *why* a timestamp-less =CLOCK:= line got
written in the first place is unconfirmed — a race between two
near-simultaneous writers to the same drawer is the working
hypothesis, not proven). A second, structurally different way the
same "shared mutable file" pressure can corrupt =:LOGBOOK:=, worth
folding into whatever planning addresses this cluster.

Also worth noting for whoever plans =4cda6bf7='s own fix: swapping
=status-label= for =org-get-todo-state= *at the clock marker* is
still marker-dependent — it inherits this exact desync mode (a stale
TODO keyword shown instead of a stale in/out label), it doesn't
route around it.

*Kept and narrowed 2026-08-13, by the user's decision.* Reviewed against
its two partial owners rather than as keep-or-cancel, because that is
what it actually is: a gap between them.

*What is retired.* The original =org_clock_out= failure is unreachable —
that function opens and closes nothing since
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]]'s cutover. So is
the concurrent-writer =CLOCK:= corruption:
=claude-code-ide-org-consolidate-history= has no production caller left,
only tests.

*What survives, and is the whole of this heading now.* Two surfaces,
each only partly owned elsewhere:
1. =--trigger-auto-clock-in= (config.el:1778, registered :1941) still
   opens a real clock on *any* transition to =DOING=/=PLANNING=,
   including a human's own =C-c C-t=. Apply suppresses it via the
   re-entrancy guard; a hand-edit does not.
   [[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] covers
   *container* headings only, so leaf hand-edits remain.
2. =--statusline-task-string= (config.el:659) still reads
   =org-clocking-p=/=org-clock-marker=/=org-clock-history= directly.
   [[id:290b6fc5-3ebb-4827-9a97-5a3542e354b6][290b6fc5]] is scoped to
   the statusline's *data source*, not to marker desync generally.

Cancel this and the uncovered middle belongs to nobody. The deciding
fact against cancelling is that the desync was observed *live on
2026-08-12*, after the cutover, and recorded on =ab75d6d2= — not a
theoretical residue.

*Newly established 2026-08-13, and it raises this heading's stakes.*
Reading =org-clock.el= for
[[id:803314aa-eda9-4d48-9e43-b3eb4802a4e8][803314aa]] found that
=org-clock-out= signals =(error "Clock start time is gone")=
(org-clock.el:1841) exactly when =org-clock-marker='s line is no longer
a =CLOCK:= line — i.e. precisely this heading's desync. That is the same
error incident 1 above recorded. So this desync is not merely a display
problem: it is the reachable trigger for apply's fabricated-interval
bug. The two headings are one causal chain, and this one is upstream.

*Confirmed surviving, and promoted out of =MAYBE=, 2026-08-14 by
[[id:e51d6ba1-cc1d-4745-8aa7-e4b1183a230b][e51d6ba1]]* — the heading
whose job was to decide whether this one is superseded. It is not, and
three separate things now depend on it, which is more than "someday"
can carry:

1. Observed *live on 2026-08-12*, after the cutover, so it is residue of
   nothing.
2. It is the upstream half of the causal chain to =803314aa=, per the
   paragraph directly above.
3. [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]]'s decision to
   *keep* =claude-code-ide-org-close-open-interval= rests explicitly on
   this heading — a shipped decision now cites it as a reason.

* MAYBE Coarsen :SESSIONS: to session-level, not turn-level :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       c6b598ed-9e92-4a3a-99fb-9c5194d290e4
:CREATED:  [2026-07-28 Tue 11:06]
:END:
:SESSIONS: currently inherits the same Stop/UserPromptSubmit hooks as
:LOGBOOK:, so every turn boundary writes a Paused/Resumed pair — fine
for CLOCK, whose "many short intervals instead of one long one"
granularity is intentional (see the dual-timekeeping design note), but
not what :SESSIONS: was meant to show: the *session-level* bracketing
arc, not per-prompt noise. Wanted instead: a genuine close only at
DOING → DONE with the work tested and committed, at the end of a
working day, or on an explicit signal — sketched as a command like
=/dfd <task>= ("done for the day").

*Open question:* does "end of a working day" need its own detection
(e.g. against =claude-code-ide-org-working-hours=), or is =/dfd=
always a manual call — auto-detecting day boundaries crosses into
guessing user intent, exactly the kind of call CLAUDE.md's existing
rules say only the model/user should make, not a hook.
