Deferred out of the tracked-files fix above: once =DONE.org= (or any
archive file) is ever reachable from
=claude-code-ide-org--tracked-files=, retrospective =org_clock_report=
calls without an =:ID:= should account for archived work's clocked
time too — completed tasks' =:LOGBOOK:= history moves into =DONE.org=
on archive, so excluding it undercounts. Not urgent now: =DONE.org=
isn't symlinked into =~/org= today, so it isn't in scope either way.

Org's clocktable machinery already has a built-in mechanism for
exactly this — the =:scope= parameter accepts ='agenda-with-archives=
(or ='file-with-archives=), which follows each agenda file's
=#+ARCHIVE:=/=org-archive-location= automatically. Prefer that over
symlinking =DONE.org= alongside =TODO.org= in =~/org= if/when this
becomes worth building — it keeps the interactive prospective agenda
and the retrospective clock-report scope independently correct
instead of coupled to the same file list.


* MAYBE Worktree-based session partitioning, with a canonical-file caveat :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       2e09adb7-6e29-499c-a40e-98cd2b327cff
:CREATED:  [2026-08-04 Tue 13:29]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-04 Tue 13:30] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/worktree-session-partitioning.md][Plan]]

Raised in conversation 2026-08-04: can separate Claude Code sessions
work on distinct TODOs simultaneously by each operating in its own
=git worktree= of a project repo? For the *code* side, yes — that's
exactly what worktrees are for, one checkout + branch per session,
no shared-file collisions. Emacs' own uniquify already disambiguates
same-basename buffers at different paths, so that part isn't even in
question.

Where it doesn't extend: =TODO.org=/=DONE.org= themselves must stay a
single canonical copy in the main checkout, not one copy per
worktree. Two failure modes rule out a per-worktree copy: (1) =:ID:=
lookups go through =org-id-locations=, a single global hash table
keyed by ID — a heading =:ID:= duplicated across two on-disk copies
(which a worktree checkout produces automatically) means whichever
copy was scanned last silently wins, with every =org_*= tool
targeting an unpredictable copy; (2) CLOCK/=:SESSIONS:= data written
into a worktree-local copy is invisible to =org_clock_report=/
=org_query=/the agenda until that branch merges, and is lost outright
if the worktree is torn down first — fragmenting exactly the
attention-tracking data the clock exists to keep whole. The single
clock living in one Emacs process reading one canonical file is the
mechanism that enforces "one attention at a time," not a limitation
to route around.

Related: [[id:337f7fb2-b9e9-4c02-82dd-d88e60df364b][Concurrent Claude Code sessions share one global org-clock with no session identity]]
already solved the narrower "which session owns the running clock"
problem this would otherwise collide with. This entry is scoped to
whether/how to formalize worktrees-for-code as a documented pattern
(e.g. in CLAUDE.md's Emacs integration section) alongside that
existing single-canonical-file constraint — not yet decided as worth
building anything for; may just be a documentation note rather than
code.

*Update 2026-08-05:* the core premise here — "the single clock is the
mechanism that enforces one attention at a time, not a limitation to
route around" — is exactly what's being stress-tested by real
concurrent-session activity found this same day:
[[id:582cc7f4-41a2-4666-ad3f-1b76b459147e][session-resume blindly inherits any session's last-clocked heading]],
[[id:53b0047d-c55c-486c-8eed-ba4994d97a1a][Direct file writes to a live org buffer can desync org-clock-marker]],
and
[[id:d150c02e-06fe-4514-bf69-e2cc5d8df4c7][org_clock_in doesn't record clock ownership, unlike session-resume]].
Not reopening the conclusion here — just flagging that if/when this
gets planned, it should be read against that fresh evidence, not just
the original 2026-07-30 reasoning.


* TODO Stale entries in org-clock's persisted history hang the first clock-in after a restart :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       001fb310-69f7-4417-81be-a224a3bf83e0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-10 Mon 11:51]
:END:

Hit live 2026-08-10 while setting =f9f61c04= to =DOING=. The
=org_set_todo= MCP call timed out, while Emacs itself stayed responsive
to =emacsclient -e= -- a recursive edit still runs a command loop, so
the server keeps answering. That combination makes a blocked Emacs look
like a tool fault.

*Mechanism, fully root-caused:* Doom advises =org-clock-in= with
=+org--clock-load-a=, a =:before= advice that lazily calls
=org-clock-load= on the *first* clock-in of an Emacs session.
=org-clock-load= visits every file named in the persisted history
(=org-clock-persist-file=, here
=~/.config/emacs/.local/etc/org-clock-save.el=). That history still
named =/private/tmp/planning-verify/scratch.org=, a scratch file from an
earlier verification session whose directory /tmp reaping had since
removed. Visiting it produced a buffer whose save asked
=Directory .../planning-verify/ does not exist! Create it? (y or n)= --
and a =y-or-n-p= is *not* an error, so no =condition-case= in this
module could have caught it. It simply waited.

*Why it first looked like leaked state:* a separate =emacsclient= query
reported =claude-code-ide-org--auto-clock-in-active= as =t=, which reads
exactly like a re-entrancy guard that failed to unwind. It was not --
the =let=-binding was still live on the blocked call's stack. Reading it
that way is what located the hang inside =--trigger-auto-clock-in=, and
it generalizes: a dynamic binding observed as set from outside means the
frame that bound it has not returned.

*Cleared 2026-08-10*: =abort-recursive-edit=, killed the stale buffer,
filtered =org-clock-history= of markers into nonexistent files, and
called =org-clock-save= to rewrite the persist file from the cleaned
live history. Verified -- the file no longer mentions the path, and the
next =org_clock_in= returned immediately.

*What is NOT fixed, and is the actual task here:* nothing prevents a
recurrence. Any verification pass that clocks into a scratch org file
under /tmp writes that path into the persisted history, and the first
clock-in after the next restart hangs once /tmp is reaped. =bin/test=
runs =emacs --batch -Q= and cannot pollute the real history, but hand
verification in a live Emacs does exactly this -- and
[[id:3d576d29-747f-40b9-a5b3-ecc76679daad][3d576d29]] established that
live verification is *necessary* here, because batch behaves
differently. Options, unevaluated: prune nonexistent files via an
=org-clock-load= advice; keep live-verification scratch files somewhere
/tmp reaping does not reach; or set =org-clock-persist= to nil and
accept losing clock history across restarts.

Relevant to [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]: a
hang in the clock path is the exact failure class that refactor exists
to eliminate, and this one survives the cutover untouched, since the
apply command clocks in through the same advised =org-clock-in=.


* MAYBE Let the review command ask Claude to explain the ambiguous gaps between guideposts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e
:BLOCKER:  ids(5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704 406e9200-c3e7-49ac-a8d0-d6ff8e061ce4)
:CREATED:  [2026-08-07 Fri 08:05]
:END:

Captured 2026-08-07 from conversation. The idea as posed: while
reviewing a heading in
[[id:720b2dcf-6af1-45f3-96a7-aa841e5651e1][720b2dcf]]'s command, Emacs
offers "Would you like some help with this one?" and, on approval,
calls back to Claude for a recommended aggregation of that heading's
guideposts.

*Transport, researched 2026-08-07 — the protocol answer is "yes, but
not here."* MCP's =sampling/createMessage= is exactly this primitive:
a *server* asks the *client* to run an LLM completion on its behalf,
so no server-side API key is needed, and the spec's own message-flow
diagram puts a human approval step on both the request and the
response. It has existed since protocol version =2024-11-05=, which is
what =claude-code-ide= declares. (Distinct from =elicitation/create=,
added in =2025-06-18=, which asks the *user* for structured form input
rather than the model — not relevant here, since Emacs already has its
own UI and needs no protocol to prompt its own user.)

Three independent blockers, in increasing order of severity:
- =claude-code-ide.el= implements none of it — zero matches for
  =sampling=/=createMessage=/=elicit= across all ten of its =.el=
  files, and nothing in its open issues or PRs upstream proposes it.
- More fundamentally, it has no *outgoing request* machinery at all.
  It can send fire-and-forget notifications
  (=claude-code-ide-mcp--send-notification=) but has no pending-request
  table and no id→callback correlation, so it cannot issue a JSON-RPC
  request and await a reply. (=--send-ping= is a websocket-level frame,
  not a JSON-RPC request — easy to mistake for evidence otherwise.)
  Adding sampling is therefore "build bidirectional request/response
  correlation," upstream, not "add a handler."
- Sampling only works if the *client* declares the =sampling=
  capability, which Claude Code may well not. Unverified.

*The architectural blocker that outranks all three:* sampling is
server→client over a *live session*. This refactor exists precisely so
the human can review whenever they choose — very often with no Claude
Code session connected at all. There would frequently be no client to
sample from. The practical transport, if this is ever built, is simply
shelling out to =claude -p= asynchronously (the CLI is already on PATH
and supports =--print=/=--output-format=); it needs no protocol work,
no upstream change, and works with nothing connected.

*Design objection, and a reframe that answers it.* Recommending an
aggregation is a more confident version of exactly what =720b2dcf=
forbids. The danger is not that the model writes anything — it is
*anchoring*: a fluent, plausible-sounding proposed decomposition is far
harder to reject than a transparently mechanical 15-minute threshold,
which degrades the very safety property the review step exists to
provide. Worse, on the queue file alone the model would have *strictly
less* information than the threshold algorithm — the same timestamps
and heading IDs, minus any domain knowledge — so it cannot outperform
the heuristic; it can only add variance and unearned authority.

The version worth building instead: don't ask Claude to *recommend
intervals*, ask it to *explain the gaps*. Given the session transcript
(=~/.claude/projects/<project>/*.jsonl=) as input, "what was happening
between 10:02 and 10:35?" is a retrieval-and-summarization task that
models are genuinely good at and no heuristic can do at all — and it
leaves the interval decision entirely with the human, preserving
=720b2dcf='s core principle rather than eroding it. That reframing also
changes the dependency: the load-bearing question becomes whether the
transcript is in scope as an input, not whether Emacs can reach a
model.

Sequence well after =720b2dcf= ships and real queue data exists —
there is no way to judge whether the plain threshold is already good
enough until it has been used in anger.

*That condition is now met, 2026-08-13, and the answer is no — but the
remedy is smaller than this heading assumes.*

The threshold has been used in anger: 23 unattributed spans were
assigned by hand in one sitting. The mechanical suggestion built for
[[id:3d0487f4-b8f8-451c-88bf-ab537663cbd0][3d0487f4]] (nearest preceding
=DOING=) managed 18 of 23 and was wrong in instructive ways — it latched
onto a =CANCELLED= heading for eight hours until taught to release, and
offered one heading for a 45-minute span that actually covered five.

*A third source of evidence, discovered by using it before proposing
it.* Asked "how should I identify what a span was circling?", the answer
that worked was neither the transcript nor a model: *git commits and
=:CREATED:= stamps falling inside the span's window.* Three shell
commands resolved three of four spans unambiguously — the 15:30--15:34
span brackets a heading created 15:33 and a commit at 15:34; the
12:36--12:59 span contains three commits and three heading creations.

*Why that beats both options this heading records.* It needs no
sampling, so every blocker above is moot — no upstream request/response
machinery, no client capability, no live session. It needs no transcript
either, so the "is the transcript in scope as an input" question does
not arise. And it answers the design objection outright: commits and
=:CREATED:= stamps are *mechanical evidence*, not a fluent
recommendation, so there is nothing to anchor on. The human still
decides; they simply stop having to run =git log= by hand.

*The version worth building first,* therefore, is not this heading:
render each unassigned span alongside the commits and headings created
in its window, in the review buffer. Small, proven by hand, no new
infrastructure. Filed as
[[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]] — a *sibling*,
deliberately, not a child of this heading: it shares the goal but none
of the mechanism, needs no sampling and no transcript, and is what
narrows this heading rather than something this heading leads.

*What remains for this heading* is the residue that mechanical evidence
cannot resolve: a span straddling two tasks, where commits exist on both
sides and only the narrative says where the boundary fell. The
54-minute 18:12--19:07 span was exactly that shape. So this stays
=MAYBE=, but its scope narrows from "explain the gaps" to "explain the
*ambiguous* gaps" — a much smaller and better-targeted feature, and one
whose value can be judged only after the mechanical version exists.

The heading title was narrowed to match on 2026-08-13. Until then the
narrowing lived only in this body, so =org_outline= and the agenda both
still advertised the original scope — which is how a heading gets picked
up later and built to the wrong size by someone reading the index rather
than the prose.

*Promoted to =**= 2026-08-15, and its home changed in the process.*
Evaluated for cancellation, immediate implementation, or promotion.

=5ff5a4b8= is =DONE= — the mechanical version shipped — so the
=:BLOCKER:= is satisfied and the narrowing this body already performed is
complete: what remains is the residue commits and =:CREATED:= stamps
cannot resolve, a span straddling two tasks where only the narrative says
where the boundary fell.

*Not cancelled:* that residue is real and was identified concretely (the
54-minute 18:12--19:07 span). Nothing since has made it go away.

*Not built:* judging its value requires the mechanical version to have
been used enough to show how often the residue actually occurs, and that
has not happened. Six unassigned spans were sitting in the review buffer
on 2026-08-15, but they are unassigned because of
[[id:0d055205-a670-45c6-b4af-980961603a7c][0d055205]] — the assignment
prompt excluding headings created after the span — not because anything
was ambiguous. They are evidence about a defect, not about this feature.

*The reframe, which matters more than the promotion.* This heading is
scoped to the *review command* — phase 1, queue → =:LOGBOOK:=. But the
question it answers, "what was happening between 10:02 and 10:35", is the
*attention pass* question — phase 2, =:LOGBOOK:= → attention intervals,
where the human reconstructs a day from evidence
([[id:4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba][4f8500e6]]). That is where a
narrative explanation is wanted and where no heuristic can substitute.
[[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] independently
concluded that the useful form of assistance there is *a recall cue, not
a metric* — which is exactly what this proposes. Re-point it at phase 2
before building anything.

*One open question here got a partial answer on 2026-08-15:* "is the
transcript in scope as an input?" The transcript is readable at
=~/.claude/projects/<slug>/<session>.jsonl= and its structure is
countable — 276 =type:"user"= entries of which 25 were real prompts, in
one session. So the input exists and is parseable. Whether it *should* be
read remains a judgement, not a capability question.

The transport conclusion recorded above still stands and is worth not
re-researching: not MCP sampling — there is frequently no live session at
review time — but =claude -p= asynchronously, which needs no protocol
work and no upstream change.


* MAYBE Give the review buffer a transient menu, once it has filters worth composing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       118373e5-b89b-4001-ad52-1f68374745fb
:CREATED:  [2026-08-07 Fri 15:54]
:BLOCKER:  ids(720b2dcf-6af1-45f3-96a7-aa841e5651e1)
:END:

Captured 2026-08-07 from conversation, after pressing =h= in a live
=*org-review*= buffer and finding the help thin. Two cheap fixes landed
immediately — the mode docstring was rewritten as user-facing help
rather than maintainer notes, and =?= now calls
=which-key-show-major-mode= (with a =describe-mode= fallback, since
which-key is absent under =emacs --batch -Q=). This heading is the
*deferred* remainder.

*Availability, checked rather than assumed:* =transient= is loaded, and
=claude-code-ide= itself ships =claude-code-ide-transient.el= with 16
transient prefixes — so it is already the idiom in this exact
ecosystem, not a new dependency. =which-key= is loaded too. =hydra= and
=casual= are not installed.

*Why deferred rather than done:* transient earns its machinery when
there are *options and switches to compose* — that is what makes
magit's popups worth it. The review buffer currently has seven flat
commands and no state to toggle, so a transient menu today would be a
prettier =which-key= at roughly ten times the cost. =which-key= gets
most of the benefit for one line.

*What would change that*, and the real trigger to revisit: the filters
the buffer plausibly wants once it meets real volume — filter by
session, filter to only =(suggested)= items, mark-all-within-a-heading,
apply-with-confirmation. Those are switches, and transient handles
switches far better than a flat keymap does. The
[[id:720b2dcf-6af1-45f3-96a7-aa841e5651e1][720b2dcf]] plan already
named magit-status's staging area as the model, so this is a genuine
direction — it should just follow the filters rather than lead them.
Blocked on =720b2dcf= being used enough to know which filters are
actually wanted; guessing at them now is how the machinery gets built
for a UI nobody needs.

*Promoted to =**= 2026-08-15, evaluated for cancellation, immediate
implementation, or promotion — and promotion is the honest answer.*

=720b2dcf= is =DONE=, so the =:BLOCKER:= is satisfied and this is no
longer *structurally* blocked. It stays =MAYBE= because its own trigger
has not fired: the condition written here is "filters worth composing",
and the review buffer still has flat commands with no state to toggle.
Nothing in real use has demanded a filter. The friction actually
encountered on 2026-08-15 was the assignment prompt
([[id:0d055205-a670-45c6-b4af-980961603a7c][0d055205]]) and candidate
ranking ([[id:49cbe319-f7db-413a-ade8-c4850c1fab8c][49cbe319]]) — neither
of which a transient menu would have helped with.

*Not cancelled,* because the direction is sound and cheap to revisit:
=transient= is already this ecosystem's idiom, =claude-code-ide= ships 16
prefixes of its own, and the trigger condition is written down precisely
enough that a future session can check it rather than re-derive it.

*Not built,* because this heading's own argument forbids it — "guessing
at them now is how the machinery gets built for a UI nobody needs." That
reasoning has not weakened; if anything the two defects found on 08-15
suggest the buffer's next improvements are correctness, not composition.

*Promoted rather than left in place* on the reasoning
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] has now applied
five times: this is a capability that epic *enables* rather than one it
*requires*, and an epic which keeps every downstream capability can never
close. With this and
[[id:0c8644ff-f7b1-4789-ac1e-babf2e9fbc8e][0c8644ff]] out, that epic's
children are 29 =DONE= and 2 =CANCELLED=, and it can close.

[[file:~/.claude/plans/nested-gathering-wozniak.md][Plan]]

Once implemented: rewrite CLAUDE.md's "State transition rules",
"Session tracking", and "MCP tools" sections, and the org skill's
state-transition table, to describe the queue+review model rather
than the live-driving model they currently document. Large enough
that it should not be a drive-by edit tacked onto another task.


*Vocabulary settled 2026-08-10, ahead of the rewrite.* Prompted by
noticing that "per-turn clocking" describes something that stops
existing at the cutover, so the docs cannot simply be updated around it.

The current phrase conflates two things that separate cleanly, and the
right words for both are already in this codebase — they just are not yet
accurate, because the =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= hooks presently do two
jobs at once: drive a live clock *and* append an event.
[[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] deletes the first.

| today                    | after the cutover                        |
|--------------------------+------------------------------------------|
| per-turn *clocking* —    | per-turn *guideposts* — hooks append     |
| hooks open/close real    | bare timestamps; nothing clocks          |
| CLOCK intervals every    |                                          |
| turn boundary            |                                          |
|--------------------------+------------------------------------------|
| CLOCK lines land         | *confirmed intervals* land per review    |
| continuously, one per    | pass, one per cluster of guideposts      |
| turn                     |                                          |

So the *emission* stays per-turn; the *record* stops being per-turn.
Replacement wording should name the emission ("turn-boundary
guideposts") and say nothing about intervals, since intervals become
per-decision.

*Retire the word "clocking" rather than qualify it.* By feba67eb's own
reckoning a clock is only ever live for the microseconds between the
paired =org-clock-in=/=org-clock-out= calls inside apply. There is no
background clock state left to name, so "clocking" as an ongoing
condition is not a thing that happens any more.

*Churn relocates; it does not disappear.* Guideposts still accumulate
every turn, into the queue file — cheap, disposable, no Emacs required.
What ends is churn in the *org record*. Worth stating explicitly in
CLAUDE.md, since the obvious reading of "the cutover stops per-turn
churn" is wrong in a way that would mislead the next reader.

*Concretely wrong at the cutover, in CLAUDE.md's "Session tracking"
section:* it describes =:LOGBOOK:= CLOCK entries as tracking "active
Claude work time only," paused the moment Claude stops and resumed on
the next prompt. After the cutover no hook writes a CLOCK entry at all —
that section describes the mechanism this refactor removes.


*Additional scope, requested 2026-08-11: a NEXT-nomination rule for the
org skill.* Two halves, proposed by the user:

1. Every transition *to* =DONE= should nominate a =NEXT=, when clear
   candidates exist.
2. When there is an obvious =NEXT= whose blockers live in a *different
   subtree*, call those blockers out.

*Why it is coherent rather than just tidy.* This repo already enforces
*at most* one =NEXT= per level, mechanically:
=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo= promotes the last remaining =TODO= in
a sibling group. Part 1 supplies *at least* one, for the case a trigger
cannot decide — several candidates, requiring judgement. Together they
give exactly one, which is the GTD invariant proper: a live project
always has a next action, and a project without one is the canonical
defect a weekly review exists to catch.

*The evidence is the session that produced this note.* It opened with the
user asking "what's next?", and the answer had to be reconstructed by
walking states and =:BLOCKER:= properties, because the epic had stretches
with *zero* =NEXT= while carrying two =DOING=. The rule would have
pre-answered the question that took the first twenty minutes to rebuild.

*Part 2 is the more valuable half.* =:BLOCKER:= is machine-checkable but
only visible to someone already looking at the blocked heading.
Same-subtree blockers are cheap to notice — =feba67eb= being blocked by
=720b2dcf= was obvious all along. Cross-subtree ones are not:
[[id:d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5][d9132c57]] under Observability
depends on [[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]] under
Skill logic, and that is discoverable today only because it happens to be
written in prose. Those are exactly the dependencies that rot unnoticed.

*Two failure modes to write into the rule, not discover later:*
- *NEXT-churn.* "When clear candidates exist" is load-bearing. Without
  it the rule degrades =NEXT= from "decided, up next" into "whatever
  was adjacent." The rule must explicitly bless *declining* to nominate,
  and saying so, over promoting a placeholder.
- *It can silently demote.* Nominating a =NEXT= may trigger
  =--trigger-demote-conflicting-next= against an existing one. Acceptable,
  but the nomination should read as a deliberate choice between
  candidates rather than a surprise reshuffle.

*Placement: the org skill, not CLAUDE.md.* The skill already carries the
transition table and =TODO -> NEXT= semantics, and the precedent is
established — the Plan Mode checkpoint rule lives there "since it's a
way-of-working for Claude Code's Plan Mode generally, not an org-file
convention specific to this repo." This is the same shape: behaviour at a
transition, generalising to any org+GTD project. CLAUDE.md keeps the
repo-specific parts it already has, the keyword set and the
single-NEXT-per-level enforcement.

*Fix the drift in the same edit.* The skill currently documents
=TODO NEXT DOING WAIT MAYBE | DONE CANCELLED= (SKILL.md:43) and is
missing =PLANNING=, which CLAUDE.md has carried since the state was
added. Adding a rule to a table that is already wrong would bake the
error in.

*Four concrete drifts found 2026-08-11, post-cutover.* Recorded here
rather than fixed piecemeal, since this heading owns the rewrite. Each
was found the same way — a claim in CLAUDE.md taken as current and acted
on, then contradicted by the artifact:

1. *The session hooks.* Documented as calling =emacsclient -e=, with
   =session-pause= "a thin alias for =org_clock_out=" and
   =session-resume= calling =org-clock-in-last=. Both are now one-line
   =exec=s to =bin/hooks/queue-append= with no =emacsclient= at all.
   This drift actively misled: three symbol names taken from these
   sections (=claude-code-ide-org-session-pause=, =--review-queue=,
   =--clock-owner-session-id=) all came back unbound, which read as
   "the module is not loaded" when in fact 102 of its functions were
   and the names were simply retired.
2. *Power assertions framed as noise.* See
   [[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]] — the framing
   is backwards for attribution.
3. *=DOING= with no running clock.* Still implied to be an
   inconsistency. Post-cutover it is the expected steady state: live
   =CLOCK:= lines are only ever written by the review pass, so every
   =DOING= heading sits clockless between passes.
4. *The =#+TODO:= header* (CLAUDE.md line 188) matches neither file.
   TODO.org carries =!=/=@= logging markers on every keyword; DONE.org
   has no =#+TODO:= line at all, despite the same section saying every
   =.org= file in the project should start with one. The documented
   version — bare keywords, no markers — was used as a test fixture on
   2026-08-11 and manufactured a false "org logs nothing" finding.

*The same header block gained a fourth line, 2026-08-12:*
=#+STARTUP: logdrawer=, added to both =TODO.org= and =DONE.org= at the
time of writing. CLAUDE.md's "File header" section must document it
alongside the three it already lists — same block, same files, so it
belongs with drift #4 above rather than as a separate edit.

*Why a file header and not a =setq=.* =org-log-into-drawer= defaults to
nil, so org's *native* state-change logging writes notes bare, just
after the property drawer, while this module's apply path lands them in
=:LOGBOOK:= — it =let=-binds the variable at =config.el:2708=,
deliberately and locally, so the user's own interactive =org-todo= keeps
their configured behavior. The result was two paths disagreeing, visible
on [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]], whose =DOING=
note sits above the drawer while older entries sit inside it.

Verified 2026-08-12 that =#+STARTUP: logdrawer= sets the variable
*buffer-locally* — effective value ="LOGBOOK"= inside the file, global
still nil — which is why it is the right instrument: it claims exactly
the scope this project owns, and changes nothing for a user's other org
files. The alternatives were weighed and rejected: a global =setq=
reconfigures org for everyone who installs the module (see
[[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]]), and a
=:LOG_INTO_DRAWER:= property — which does work, and does inherit —
would have to sit on the top-level category headings, which this same
CLAUDE.md section forbids from carrying a =:PROPERTIES:= drawer at all.

*Note the verification precondition for anyone re-checking this:*
=#+STARTUP:= is read when org initializes a buffer, so a file already
open in Emacs keeps the old value until it is reverted. Saving is not
enough, and neither is =global-auto-revert-mode= noticing the change if
the buffer is unmodified — the mode has to re-run.

The pattern is worth naming in the rewrite, not just the four
instances: CLAUDE.md is loaded into every session by default while the
artifacts it describes are not, so a stale claim in it outranks the
truth until someone deliberately checks. Sections describing mechanisms
that have been cut over are the highest-risk kind.

*Added to scope 2026-08-13, for the org-dev skill specifically:
=load-file= does not pick up a changed =defcustom= default.* Measured
that day — the guidepost threshold was committed as 1200, =load-file=
returned =t=, and the live value stayed 900, because =defcustom= only
sets a default when the symbol is unbound. It needed an explicit
=setq=. Anyone changing a default and then "verifying" it against the
running Emacs measures the old value while every signal says success,
which is exactly the failure the user's own reload-precondition rule
exists to prevent. The org-dev skill is where this belongs, since it
owns reload-and-verify for this project's code.

*Added to scope 2026-08-13: CLAUDE.md's "Dependencies between tasks"
misleads about enforcement.* It says this project's =org-blocker-hook=
function "only blocks on a running clock today, not on =:BLOCKER:=".
True of *that function* and false as a statement about the file: measured
2026-08-13, =org-blocker-hook= also carries =org-depend-block-todo= and
=org-enforce-todo-dependencies= is =t=. Claude read that sentence and
told the user "nothing enforces =:BLOCKER:= in this repo" — the
sentence is accurate and its natural reading is not, which is the
hardest kind of doc bug to catch. It also underpins
[[id:087e73e9-bb24-43b6-a972-b7f103d04017][087e73e9]], whose premise is
now falsified. The rewrite should say what is on the hook and what each
part blocks on.

Also worth documenting there: a =:BLOCKER:= on a =MAYBE= heading is
dormant, because blocking is evaluated against the blocked heading's own
state.

*Added to scope 2026-08-11: mechanically check the checkable claims.*
Rewriting the prose fixes those four and leaves nothing preventing the
next four — CLAUDE.md is a hand-maintained description of a system that
keeps moving, so drift is the steady state, not an incident. But all
four were *decidable by a script*: does =claude-code-ide-org-session-pause=
exist as a function; does the documented =#+TODO:= line match TODO.org's
line 1; does every =.org= file carry the header the doc says every
=.org= file carries. None needed judgement.

The precedent is already in this repo. =bin/sync-plans --check= plus the
=.githooks/pre-push= guard exist for exactly this shape of problem — a
document that silently stops matching what it describes — and refuse a
push while the two disagree. A =bin/check-claude-md= in the same shape
would catch the class rather than the instance. Start with the cheapest
assertions: symbols named in prose are =fboundp=, the =#+TODO:= block
matches the files, documented file paths exist.

Worth doing *with* the rewrite rather than after it, since writing the
checks is what forces the prose into checkable claims — and a claim too
vague to check is usually the kind that goes stale unnoticed.

*And a caution to write into the rewrite itself.* An accurate CLAUDE.md
makes answering from it more often correct, which makes the habit of
answering from it instead of from the artifact harder to notice — the
same behaviour with better odds. On 2026-08-11 only four of roughly a
dozen wrong claims traced to this file; the rest came from unchecked
inference, from a session's own earlier summaries, and three times from
reading a silently-failing command's empty output as a result. The
rewrite should say plainly that the file is a starting point and the
artifact is the authority, rather than implying that accuracy here makes
checking unnecessary.

*Added to scope 2026-08-12: reading a timestamp out of these files must
be drawer-scoped.* This repo's =.org= files quote =CLOCK:= and
=- State "..."= lines constantly in *prose* — as worked examples, as
recorded experiment output, as evidence in an outcome summary. A pattern
match over the raw file therefore returns real-looking timestamps that
were never real, and the wrong answers are indistinguishable from the
right one by inspection.

*Cost, measured 2026-08-12* while estimating when work on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]'s children began.
Three attempts, two wrong:

1. =[2026-08-06 Thu 17:41]= — matched a =- State "DOING"= line inside
   [[id:3d576d29-747f-40b9-a5b3-ecc76679daad][3d576d29]]'s *body*, part
   of the write-up of the backdating experiment itself.
2. =[2026-08-06 Thu 22:35]=, verified by grepping the file for that
   =CLOCK:= line — which returned line 1538, *also* example prose, and
   in a different heading outside the subtree entirely. The extraction
   was right by then; the *verification* repeated the original mistake.
3. Correct only when both scoped to the subtree *and* restricted to
   lines between =:LOGBOOK:= and =:END:=.

*The rule to write:* timestamps and clock data come from inside a
=:LOGBOOK:= drawer, or from =org_clock_report=/=org_outline=, never from
a bare pattern match. This is the same failure as the anchored-drawer
caution — =grep -c ':SESSIONS:'= counting prose mentions — generalised
from "does this drawer exist" to "what does it contain."

*Placement: the org skill.* Same reasoning the NEXT-nomination rule
above uses — it is a way of reading any org file whose prose discusses
org, not a convention specific to this repo. Note that *neither* the
skill nor CLAUDE.md currently says anything about this; verified
2026-08-12, both contain no guidance on pattern-matching org files at
all. A session handoff asserted the skill already covered it, which was
itself an unchecked claim of exactly the kind the section above warns
about.

*Drift found 2026-08-14 by
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]], not fixed there:*
CLAUDE.md's "Emacs integration" block shows
=(add-hook 'kill-emacs-hook #'org-clock-out)= and the same for
=suspend-hook=, but the live Doom config installs
=claude-code-ide-org--clock-out-if-clocking= on both. Load-bearing rather
than cosmetic — whether a clean exit closes a hand-opened clock is
exactly what decides how much the stale-interval detector still has to
catch.

*Also for the rewrite:*
[[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] adds an exception
to CLAUDE.md's "any transition *to* =DOING= or =PLANNING= must open a
clock" — container headings change state without clocking. Recorded
there in full; noted here so the rewrite does not have to be reminded by
the other heading.

Overlaps the enforcement half of
[[id:d7849119-8224-4993-a8ab-83e1434df53c][d7849119]], which wants
README/CLAUDE.md consistency guarded by the same machinery. Whichever
lands first should build the harness so the other only adds assertions.

*Added to scope 2026-08-13: document the sibling-survey idiom, in the
org skill.* Pairs directly with the "it can silently demote" warning
above — that warning says a =NEXT= nomination may demote a sibling, but
never says how to find out *which* sibling before committing to it.

The idiom: *to survey a heading's siblings, call =org_outline= scoped
to the _parent's_ =:ID:=*, with =active_only= and a =max_depth=. One
call, one screen:

: org_outline scope=<parent id> active_only=true max_depth=3

*Why it needs writing down rather than being obvious.* Twice on
2026-08-13 this check was done with a hand-rolled Python scan of
=TODO.org= instead, by someone who had used =org_outline= correctly an
hour earlier. The tool's =scope= parameter already documents "an =:ID:=
to index only that subtree" — the capability was never missing, only the
reflex. That is exactly the pattern
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]] exists to catch,
and it recurred *inside* a session that had already invoked the tool.

*The one real limitation to document alongside it, so the idiom is not
mistaken for a full answer:* =org_outline= navigates *down* only.
Scoped to a leaf it returns that leaf and its descendants, never its
ancestors — so the caller must already know the parent's =:ID:=. There
is no upward navigation from a child. Adding a =relation: siblings=
parameter was considered on 2026-08-13 and *rejected in favour of this
documentation*: a new parameter would only shorten an idiom that already
works, on a tool whose value is being cheap to call.

*A second reason the survey matters, beyond demotion.* Scanning the file
with =grep= reads what is *on disk*, which post-cutover is not the same
as what has been *decided* — queued transitions have not landed yet.
=org_outline= reports the applied state, so the difference between "I set
this to NEXT" and "this is NEXT" stays visible. The Python scan on
2026-08-13 silently reported =TODO= for a heading whose promotion was
sitting in the queue; the answer was right, but only by luck of what was
being asked.

*The buffer-side half of the same idiom, added 2026-08-13.* =org_outline=
is a thin wrapper: the work is done by org's own =org-map-entries=, with
=org-id-find= to resolve a scope and about ten lines of ours to format
each line. Everything it does is therefore available directly from an
org buffer, and *more* is — which resolves the upward-navigation
limitation recorded above rather than leaving it open.

Two things =org-map-entries= offers that the MCP tool does not expose:

1. *Positional scopes.* =tree= (the subtree at point), =region=,
   =file=, =agenda=, or an explicit file list. So the sibling survey has
   a buffer-side form that needs no parent =:ID:= at all:

   : (save-excursion (org-up-heading-safe)
   :   (org-map-entries #'org-get-heading "/NEXT" 'tree))

   That is exactly the "navigate up from a child" case the tool cannot
   do, available for free from point. It is the reason a
   =relation: siblings= parameter stays rejected: the capability is not
   missing, only ID-addressed access to it.

2. *org's full MATCH query syntax* — the =/NEXT= above, and tags/property
   queries generally. =org_outline= has no MATCH argument and filters
   after the fact with =active_only=, so anything more selective than
   "drop DONE and CANCELLED" is easier from a buffer than through the
   tool.

*Two caveats to write down with it, both quiet failures.*
=org-map-entries= respects narrowing, so a narrowed buffer silently maps
only the visible portion and reports a confidently short answer. And
with a *file list* scope it visits those files; =org_outline='s docstring
records that this was verified not to move point or mark buffers
modified, which is what makes it safe against files the user has open —
worth preserving in the rewrite rather than rediscovering.

*Added to scope 2026-08-14: document =.claude/hooks/=.* Tracing where the
session-start report is defined turned up a directory CLAUDE.md does not
mention at all. There are *two* hook directories —

- =bin/hooks/= (documented in the repository layout): =queue-append=,
  =session-pause=, =session-resume=, =session-start-recovery-check=,
  =exitplanmode-promote-planning=
- =.claude/hooks/= (undocumented): =session-context.sh=

— and no stated rule for which a new hook belongs in. Both are wired
from =.claude/settings.json=; both are shell scripts calling
=emacsclient=; nothing distinguishes them but history.

The omission is not incidental. =session-context.sh= produces the
"what was I last doing" context injected at every session start, and the
user had never seen its output — the file that generates the least
visible artifact in the project is also the one the docs do not mention.
Worth either consolidating the two directories or writing down why they
differ.

*Promoted to =**= and split into children 2026-08-14,* out of
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]], on the reasoning
that epic's body already records twice: an epic that absorbs everything
downstream of the code it produced can never close. This was its last
non-=MAYBE= child, so promoting it leaves only the two =MAYBE=s and the
epic becomes closeable — which is what
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] has been waiting
on. The split itself is for a plainer reason: this is three passes with
three plans, and a heading carries one plan link.

*Still owed, audited 2026-08-17.* Recorded here because
[[id:ab75d6d2-0e59-405d-92c6-2c67488db133][ab75d6d2]] named this heading
as where its consequence lands, and the consequence has not landed.
Every item below was checked against the live files, not recalled.

1. *The container exception is undocumented.* ab75d6d2 concluded that
   "any transition to =DOING= or =PLANNING= must open a clock" needs a
   stated exception for container headings, and said it belonged with
   this rewrite rather than as a separate edit. CLAUDE.md still lists
   exactly one exception there, =PLANNING= -> =DOING=.

2. *The passage this body already called "concretely wrong" is still
   there, verbatim.* "Session tracking" still describes =:LOGBOOK:= CLOCK
   entries as tracking active Claude work time, paused when Claude stops
   and resumed on the next prompt. No hook has written a CLOCK entry
   since the cutover. This was predicted here on 2026-08-10 and survived
   the rewrite anyway — worth noting as evidence that naming a defect in
   a heading body does not by itself get it fixed.

3. *The org skill's state-transition table still describes the
   live-driving model.* Its rules say a transition to =DOING= "always
   opens a new CLOCK entry", with no mention of queueing — where
   CLAUDE.md's equivalent table now carries a preamble stating that the
   side effect is the call you must make, not something that happens to
   the file.

4. *The NEXT-nomination rule taken on as scope 2026-08-11 is absent from
   the org skill*, both halves of it.

*Not blocked.* All three =:BLOCKER:= IDs — 7771fc63, e51d6ba1, c084553c
— reached =DONE= on 2026-08-14. This heading is unfinished because it is
a multi-part rewrite being consumed in pieces, with individual edits
landing under it as they come up (today's "confirm emacs-tools is
reachable" rule among them), not because it is waiting on anything.

  start CLAUDE.md correctness pass
- [2026-08-14 Fri 20:11]--[2026-08-14 Fri 20:11] assigned at review
- [2026-08-14 Fri 20:11]--[2026-08-14 Fri 20:15] correct CLAUDE.md architecture claims
- State "DONE"       from "DOING"      [2026-08-14 Fri 20:15] \\
  all ten defects corrected and verified
- [2026-08-14 Fri 20:15]--[2026-08-14 Fri 20:15] assigned at review

:PLAN:

The factual pass. CLAUDE.md still files the whole queue design under a
heading reading "Direction (*not yet implemented*)" and still documents
the tools as acting on the buffer — =org_set_todo='s own docstring says
"Changes no TODO keyword", =org_clock_in='s says "Opens no clock". A
session reading CLAUDE.md today is told that calling =org_set_todo=
changes state. That is a false behavioural instruction, not a stale
detail, and it is why this child is =NEXT= rather than queued behind the
others.

Also in scope, each verified against source rather than inferred: three
registered tools missing from the table (=org_amend=, =org_outline=,
=org_review_apply=); =session-pause= described as "a thin alias for
=org_clock_out=" when it is =exec queue-append pause=; no mention
anywhere of =PermissionRequest=/=PermissionDenied=, =block_start=/
=block_end=, or the =.block-open= sentinel, all shipped in =c8c33a1=; a
paragraph at =CLAUDE.md= L518--524 left garbled by a botched edit; and a
repository layout omitting 7 of 10 =bin/= scripts and all 7 of
=bin/hooks/=.

*The =#+TODO:= line is the one with teeth.* CLAUDE.md's template omits
=REVIEW= — which =org_set_todo= accepts, and which appears nowhere in
CLAUDE.md at all — and omits every logging cookie. The real line is
=TODO(t!) NEXT(n!) DOING(d!) REVIEW(r!) WAIT(w@/!) MAYBE(m!)
| DONE(D!) CANCELLED(c@)=. The =@= means *prompt for a note*, so =WAIT=
and =CANCELLED= prompt while every other transition only timestamps: a
transition driven non-interactively through =emacsclient -e= hangs for
those two keywords and not the others. That is the exact hazard the queue
architecture exists to route around, and it is currently written down
nowhere. Record =REVIEW= as experimental
([[id:c954f650-b92f-4920-9967-ab6c44577b30][c954f650]]) rather than as
settled.

:END:
*Shipped 2026-08-14* (=871f415=), and the debrief is written 2026-08-24, backfilled during [[id:7fbab3b3-7843-4290-94a5-d421413f2be4][7fbab3b3]]'s sweep -- this heading closed before the convention requiring one existed.

All ten defects the body enumerates were corrected in CLAUDE.md in a single pass and verified against the live files rather than against recollection, which is what the state-change note "all ten defects corrected and verified" records.

*What the correction was actually about, since the title understates it:* CLAUDE.md described a queue architecture it did not have. The tool table said =org_set_todo= changed a keyword and =org_clock_in= opened a clock, when after the 2026-08-11 cutover all three queue an event and change nothing. A reader following the file would have expected a state change, seen none, and concluded the tool was broken -- which is why the corrected version now opens by saying so outright and tells the reader that a stale read is *expected, not a failure*.

*Written from evidence rather than memory,* and the distinction matters for a backfilled debrief: the commit, the two state-change notes and the body's own enumeration are the sources. Nothing here reconstructs what the pass "must have" done.

*Shipped 2026-08-14*, planned in Plan Mode and carried out the same evening -- =PLANNING= at 20:43, =DOING= at 21:22, =DONE= at 21:26. Debrief written 2026-08-24, backfilled during [[id:7fbab3b3-7843-4290-94a5-d421413f2be4][7fbab3b3]]'s sweep, since this heading closed before the convention requiring one existed.

The dedupe ran as the body describes: content CLAUDE.md was carrying that the org and org-dev skills already owned was removed from CLAUDE.md, and *both skills were corrected in the same pass* rather than merely being deferred to. That second half is what the state-change note records with "dedupe done, both skills corrected", and it is the part that makes the removal safe -- deleting from CLAUDE.md while trusting an uncorrected skill would have lost the content rather than moved it.

*The test that decided what stayed is worth restating, because it still governs:* a rule that must hold when no =.org= file is open cannot live in a path-scoped rule, since a rule that does not load is a rule that does not apply and the failure is silent. That is why the state transitions and the queue architecture stayed in CLAUDE.md while the file-header template and tag conventions left for =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md=.

*Written from evidence:* the commits, the three state-change notes, and the body's own scope. Nothing is reconstructed.

Blocked on both siblings deliberately: the decision takes the resulting
file size and shape as input, and the user's standing instruction is to
raise the question again *after* the content changes rather than predict
it now.

*The obvious mechanism does not work, which is why this is a decision and
not a task.* =@path= imports look like the answer and are not — per
Claude Code's memory docs, "splitting into =@path= imports helps
organization but doesn't reduce context, since imported files load at
launch." Imports organise; they do not defer. What actually loads lazily
is =.claude/rules/*.md= carrying =paths:= frontmatter, which loads only
when Claude reads a matching file, and skills, which load on demand.

If it goes ahead, org conventions under =paths: ["**/*.org"]= is the
natural first candidate. The caution is that =.claude/rules/= is unused
in this repo, and a rule that silently fails to load is indistinguishable
from one that loaded and was ignored — so adopting it needs a fresh
session to verify, not an assertion that it works.

The documented target is under 200 lines. CLAUDE.md is 640. This may
still close as won't-do.

*Decided yes, and shipped 2026-08-20* as =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= carrying =paths: ["**/*.org"]=. It took the file header template, the keyword and tag policy, the top-level-headings-are-categories rule, and =:BLOCKER:= usage. CLAUDE.md keeps a pointer where the content used to be.

*The split test that decided what moved, and it is not size.* A rule that must hold when no =.org= file is open cannot live in a path-scoped rule, because a rule that does not load is a rule that does not apply and the failure is silent. So the state transition table, "create a heading for any newly described task", and the queue architecture all stayed in CLAUDE.md regardless of how much room moving them would have bought.

:END:
*Verified 2026-08-21 by the standard this heading set for itself* -- "adopting it needs a fresh session to verify, not an assertion that it works." A session with no prior knowledge of the file read =TODO.org= and the rule was injected unprompted at that moment, not at launch. That is the lazy-load behaviour the decision rested on, observed rather than asserted.

*Unblocks [[id:9ae4b17e-278c-482f-9114-df28b2254806][9ae4b17e]]*, which was waiting on exactly this confirmation: its body records that the path-scoped rule "turns out to be the *portability* mechanism, not merely a context-saving one", since a user-level =~/.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= costs nothing in repos with no org files.

* TODO Read power assertions as a review-time attribution signal :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 15:52]
:END:

CLAUDE.md records =pmset -g log= as "not yet attempted" and frames the
per-app power assertions as noise obscuring clean sleep/wake
transitions. For *this* question the framing is backwards: a laptop that
never sleeps emits zero transitions and a rich assertion record, and the
assertion holder is the answer.

Tried 2026-08-11 against a known-answer window. The log named the
competing activity exactly:

: 14:01:56  Created   NoDisplaySleepAssertion  (zoom.us, PID 58601)
: 14:42:14  Released  NoDisplaySleepAssertion  00:40:17

The user's own estimate was "roughly 14:00 to roughly 14:45" — the log
was right to the second, and the 40m17s sits entirely inside the 54m11s
permission-block of
[[id:f4e628ce-e653-429a-8f3c-0f5d4241b139][f4e628ce]].

*Not a validated method, on one observation.* Zoom happened to hold a
cleanly-named assertion; =coreaudiod= held 158 of the 361 entries in the
same window and is a far noisier proxy. An assertion also is not
inattention — people work through calls. The most it can honestly do is
prompt the human reviewer, never decide.

*Retention bounds it:* the log went back ~7 days (earliest 2026-08-04).
That puts a deadline on "the human applies at a moment of their own
choosing."

Revises the disposition recorded in
[[id:9d2fcdad-9bf7-47b6-8018-223b13ec4577][9d2fcdad]]'s neighbourhood,
which proposed closing the =pmset= lead as won't-do — correct for the
stale-interval guess heuristic, wrong for attribution.

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] on
2026-08-14,* as a capability rather than a requirement. Note it is
explicitly *not* the =pmset= lead that
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] closed as
won't-do: that one guessed when a stale clock stopped, this one assigns
an existing span to a heading at review time. Same data source,
different question, and only the first was refuted.

*A ground-truth absence window, reported by the user 2026-08-25 and worth keeping because this heading has none.* They started a =/next-session= run and left immediately, confirming at 17:59 that they had been away since roughly 16:56 and still were. So the interval =[2026-08-25 Tue 16:56]--[2026-08-25 Tue 17:59]+= is a *known* absence, stated rather than inferred -- which is exactly the kind of label any proposed signal has to be scored against, and exactly what the corpus lacks.

*Note what the session's own guideposts said about that hour, because it is the whole problem.* The agent ran continuously and productively throughout it -- two headings worked, a commit landed, four probe scripts ran -- so =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= bracketing produced one long unbroken span of genuine *agent* work. Nothing in the queue distinguishes that hour from an hour with a human watching every turn. Both look identical from the queue, and both are correctly credited as agent time; what is missing is orthogonal to the clock rather than an error in it.

*Which sharpens what a power-assertion signal would be for.* Not correcting a clock -- the clock was right. It would answer a different question the review pass asks constantly: whether a span had a human *available to be interrupted*, and therefore whether the work in it has had any human judgement applied to it at all. Everything this session queued is unreviewed in that stronger sense, and no existing field records the difference.

*Caveat on using this datum.* It is one window, reported to the minute by memory, with a soft start ("immediately after kicking off" -- the session's first event was 16:56) and no end yet. Good enough to sanity-check a signal's shape; not a benchmark.

*Tested 2026-08-25 against a known event, and the result corrects this heading's own proposal.* The user reported that the Mac slept mid-session because the clamshell was closed, and could not say for how long -- so there was ground truth for the *fact* and none for the *bounds*, which is exactly the case this heading is for.

*The signal is real, but it is not the one proposed here.* This heading asks for *power assertions*. Those are precisely the noise =7771fc63= dismissed: =pmset -g log= for the session window is dominated by =PID 5048(Claude) Summary NoIdleSleepAssertion "Electron"= every fifteen minutes, =AddressBookSourceSync=, =storagekitd=, =coreaudiod= -- hundreds of lines, no transition. What answered the question was the =Notification= domain in the same log, two lines:

: 2026-08-25 17:06:53  Notification  Display is turned off
: 2026-08-25 17:50:00  Notification  Display is turned on

*It agrees with the queue to the second.* =clock_in= on [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was queued at 17:06:54, one second after the display went off; the =resume= guidepost is at 17:50:14, fourteen seconds after it came on. Neither number was available to the other mechanism.

*Cross-validated against a third, independent source* rather than trusted on the coincidence: mtimes of the probe scripts this session wrote to its scratch directory show execution at 17:07:07, 17:07:35, 17:08:10, 17:08:55, 17:09:39, 17:09:45 -- and then nothing until 18:02. So work continued for about three minutes after the lid closed and stopped somewhere in 17:09:45--17:50:00.

*The crucial limitation, and it is what stops this being a drop-in fix.* =Display is turned off= means *the lid closed*, not *the machine stopped*. The same session has a counterexample two hours later: display off 18:05:47, on 18:10:34, with continuous work throughout ([[id:f421c5c3-f81f-4ebf-99c9-42c53cc00558][f421c5c3]]'s probes wrote at 18:06:08, 18:10:09 and 18:11:33). Something held an assertion that time and the machine kept running.

*So the two edges are not equally useful, which is the finding worth keeping:*

- *The wake edge is a reliable *resume* signal.* Display-on means someone opened the lid, and work can restart from there. Cheap and unambiguous.
- *The sleep edge is *not* a reliable stop signal.* It bounds when work *may* have stopped and must be combined with evidence of absence -- no queue events, no file writes -- to say that it did.

*What it would have bought today.* [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] was credited 0:03 for roughly seven minutes of real work. The wake edge anchors the post-wake segment exactly; the pre-sleep segment is bounded below by its own =clock_in= and above by the last artefact, giving 17:06:54--17:09:45. Neither bound is a guess. The sleep *onset* stays unbounded within a forty-minute window, and should stay that way in any record.

*Note the shape of the correction.* This is the second time the useful signal turned out to be a different line in a log that had already been dismissed wholesale. Worth re-reading a rejected source for the *specific* record wanted, rather than re-litigating the rejection.


* TODO Re-measure the guidepost gap threshold against a month of queue data :code:research:
SCHEDULED: <2026-09-13 Sun +1m>
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7
:CREATED:  [2026-08-13 Thu 12:20]
:END:

*Promoted out of [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]
on 2026-08-13.* It was filed there because the threshold it re-measures
belongs to that epic's queue — but it carries a =+1m= repeater and so
never reaches =DONE=, which meant the epic could not either, and
[[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]] is blocked on that
epic. A recurring measurement has no terminal state and therefore no
completable parent.
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-13 Thu 12:21] \\
  scheduled re-measure, one month out
:END:

Scheduled rather than left to memory: the finding below is real but
thin, and the failure mode is adopting a four-day number as permanent
because nobody re-ran it.

*The survey, 2026-08-13.* 422 queue events, 2026-08-10 to 08-13, across
six sessions. Measured the *queue* rather than =:LOGBOOK:= deliberately:
=:LOGBOOK:= intervals are reconstructions produced by the very threshold
under test, so measuring them would partly measure the parameter.

Decomposed each session's stream into the two gaps that mean different
things:

- =resume= -> =pause= — how long the agent worked. n=111, median *81s*,
  p90 *211s*, one outlier over an hour (the concurrent subagent run).
- =pause= -> =resume= — how long the human took to reply. n=150, median
  *181s*.

*The result, and it is sharper than expected: the second is bimodal with
an empty band between the modes.* Sorted gaps run =… 949, 971, 1061=
and then jump straight to =2070, 2103, 2135 …=. *No gap anywhere in the
corpus falls between 1061s and 2070s.* A threshold sweep sees the same
hole from the other side: span count falls steeply, then sits flat at 29
across 1200-1800s, then resumes falling. Every value inside the band
produces an identical reconstruction.

Acted on immediately: =claude-code-ide-org-guidepost-gap-threshold= 900
-> *1200s*, the round number inside the band, at the start of the
plateau with margin either side. 900 sat just below the band and split
five spans nothing justifies splitting.

*A stated assumption the data contradicted, which is the more useful
half.* The survey was proposed on the premise that silence from ~18:00
to ~08:00 is almost certainly AFK. True, but not where the long gaps
are: of 19 gaps over 2000s, *11 start inside working hours* — 07:00,
10:00, 12:00, 14:00, 15:00, 16:00, 17:00 — including the two longest
(151 and 158 minutes, starting 12:59 and 15:34). Daytime absence is
indistinguishable from nighttime absence. Gap *length* separates the
modes cleanly; clock hour does not. Recorded against
[[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]], whose
working-hours guess rests on exactly the premise this refutes.

*What to re-run on the scheduled date*, and the reason the number is not
yet settled: n=150 gaps, four days, one person, during an unusually
intense stretch of work on this very project. The band may be an artifact
of that. Re-derive the histogram and the sweep; if the band has moved or
closed, retune again and say so here rather than defending 1200.

*This is a moving target, not a one-off calibration* — the user's own
framing, 2026-08-13, and it changes what the re-measure is for.
Proficiency, setup and work objectives all evolve, and the habits
generating these gaps evolve with them. A faster operator, a different
mix of long-running subagent work, or a stretch of shallow triage rather
than deep implementation would each shift the modes and could close the
band entirely. So the finding above describes *August 2026*, not this
project. The scheduled task is not "confirm 1200 was right"; it is
"re-derive, and expect a different answer."

*Made a =+1m= repeater at the user's instruction, 2026-08-13*, which is
the honest encoding of "moving target": a one-shot date would have
implied the number gets settled once. It does not. Each occurrence
re-derives the histogram and the sweep against whatever the preceding
month's habits actually were, and either confirms the current value or
moves it.

Practical note for whoever handles an occurrence: marking it =DONE=
rolls the =SCHEDULED= stamp forward a month and returns the heading to
=TODO= — org's own repeater behavior — so the record of *that* month's
finding has to be written into this body before closing it, or it is
gone. Append; do not overwrite the previous month's numbers.

Charts from the original survey are an artifact, not committed here —
regenerate rather than trusting a stale rendering.

*Retarget before the September run — this is now pointed at the wrong
knob.* Since 2026-08-18 (=226ed53b= step 2(b)) the gap threshold governs
only *grouping and display*: it decides how many items a review buffer
shows and how wide each reads, and moves no recorded minute. Its
docstring says so. Re-measuring it on a month of data would answer a
question whose answer changes nothing.

What now decides recorded duration is the pair of floors:

- =claude-code-ide-org-span-idle-floor= (120s) — how much idle is
  absorbed into a run.
- =claude-code-ide-org-span-minimum-interval= — the floor on what gets
  written, named 2026-08-20 by
  [[id:e0c55d26-706c-42b6-9405-54e9d5ed4750][e0c55d26]]. It is 0 today,
  meaning the two rendering conditions alone; its docstring carries the
  sweep this review needs. *Do not cite a stored number.* 120s measured
  at 6.5% of recorded time on 2026-08-18 and at 3.03% two days later on
  a grown corpus — the estimate halved without the code changing.

Both rest on eight days of data. This heading's own body already names
that failure: /"adopting a four-day number as permanent because nobody
re-ran it."/ Same trap, one knob over.

Keep the =+1m= repeater and the parentless placement — the reasoning
recorded above still holds, and applies to the floors identically.
