*Observed in the applied record 2026-08-22.* [[id:82df2a6c-881d-4117-a752-5b3b04c9656d][82df2a6c]] carries =CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 14:53]--[15:42] => 0:49=, and the day node =2026-08-21 Friday= carries four intervals -- 15:06--15:07, 15:11--15:13, 15:25--15:30, 15:34--15:35 -- every one of them *inside* that window. About nine minutes is clocked against two headings at once.

Both items were offered in the same review buffer and both were correct in isolation: one an assigned span, the other the suggested rendering of an explicit =clock_in=/=clock_out= pair. Nothing checked them against each other.

*Not a rounding concern.* The second co-equal goal of this project is a trustworthy record of where attention went, and a clocktable that sums to more than the elapsed day is the plainest possible violation of it. A remainder-style guard already exists for editing one item's interval; the missing check is across items in one batch.

*Related and distinct:* [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] would make explicit pairs authoritative, which shrinks this problem by construction -- a partitioned span cannot overlap the pair that partitioned it -- but does not remove the need for the check.

*Retitled 2026-08-28, because the old title had been overtaken.* It read "A span and an explicit clock interval can both apply, double-counting the minutes", which names the general phenomenon — and the same-heading instance of it was fixed by =--subtract-intervals=. The index was therefore advertising a solved problem, and the natural reading of the title was the fixed one.

What survives is the case this body already documents and subtraction structurally cannot reach: the two overlapping intervals live in *different* =:LOGBOOK:= drawers. =--subtract-intervals= reads the target heading's own drawer, so an interval on 82df2a6c and one on the day node are each correct in their own drawer and invisible to each other. The check that is missing is across items in one batch, not within one heading — which is what the body said before the fix and still says.


* TODO An unassigned span's readout omits the evidence that would make its heading obvious :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       5a6d2c75-9a42-4dd4-b4d6-670aeebc5b46
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 08:08]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-21 Fri 08:09] \\
  add transcript evidence to span readout
:END:

Stated by the user 2026-08-21, as the one hitch left in an otherwise straightforward review pass: the readout "lacks adequate context of what was happening immediately before it to make the assignment obvious, but I believe it's there in the data."

*Not a re-file of [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]].* That one shipped 2026-08-14 and put commits and heading creations inside an unassigned span's window on screen. It helped and did not close the gap; this is the next increment against the same complaint.

*Where the data is, and the distinction that decides the approach.* The queue carries timestamps, =session_id=, =agent_id=, =agent_type= and =source= -- and 0c8644ff's objection that this is *strictly less* than the threshold algorithm already has is correct and not disputed here. The session *transcripts* are a different corpus: [[id:420816ec-677e-45a5-b898-fb5db70d214b][420816ec]]'s research half read 69 of them. A transcript records what the session was asked and which files it touched in a given window, which is exactly the missing context, and extracting it is mechanical rather than a judgement call.

*Candidate evidence, cheapest first:* files touched in the window, taken from the transcript's tool calls; the prompt text that opened the enclosing turn; and, for the case where the competing activity was not this project at all, [[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]]'s power assertions.

*Render evidence, do not recommend a heading.* "This session edited =config.el= around 10:20" is a fact the human weighs; "this span was probably c6fc6f46" is a proposal, and 0c8644ff's anchoring argument applies to the second and not the first. Holding that line is what keeps this heading independent of the automation decision in [[id:361e2708-8124-4bd1-9c44-f97ca97aca4a][361e2708]] rather than a prerequisite for it.

*Test corpus is already sitting in the queue:* three unassigned spans as of 2026-08-21 -- =16:28--16:54= and =17:18--17:22= on 08-20, =21:08--21:11= on 08-20. Whether the added evidence makes each one's heading obvious is answerable against those three before anything is generalised.

*Two cues the user asked for by name, 2026-08-22, after allocating a batch and only afterwards realising what it contained.* Both are about what the buffer *already knows* and does not show, which is this heading's subject rather than a new one.

*"Am I actually catching meta-work? I didn't even notice because there's no visual cue in the review dialog."* A span assigned to the =Review and planning= day node and a span assigned to an ordinary task look identical, so the one measurement the datetree exists to produce is invisible at the moment it is being made. The day node is mechanically distinguishable -- it is the =:DATE_TREE:= heading's descendant -- so this is a rendering decision, not an inference.

*Coverage, per span rather than per item.* Once [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] makes explicit =clock_in= pairs authoritative, most runs in a span will already have an owner. The buffer should say which: "12 of 15 runs attributed, 3 need you" turns an hour-long yes/no into three small local questions, and shows the human where their judgement is actually required. Rendered from the same data, and it is the difference between allocating and *confirming*.

*Why this pairing matters more than either alone.* The evidence this heading was filed about answers "which heading?" The coverage line answers the prior question -- "is this even mine to decide?" -- which on the measured day was the one that went wrong: an hour that the queue had already attributed was presented as a single undifferentiated choice.


* TODO Recover a close time from git for the headings with no in-file evidence :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       b7b46a26-2fd6-4784-bed3-d0112a917c0a
:CREATED:  [2026-08-24 Mon 10:14]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-24 Mon 10:20] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
- State "CANCELLED"  from "TODO"       [2026-08-24 Mon 15:59] \\
  nothing consumes CLOSED: on those headings
- State "TODO"       from "CANCELLED"  [2026-08-28 Fri 11:38] \\
  cancellation ground void: a consumer exists
:END:

*Filed 2026-08-24, the residue of* [[id:f4b07fc0-187a-4975-aa3b-b9bd4b1c0799][f4b07fc0]]. That pass filled 58 of DONE.org's 97 finished headings from their own =:LOGBOOK:=. *39 have no in-file evidence at all* -- mostly early headings from before the =!= cookies in =#+TODO:= were adopted, e.g. "Implement the ten pending MCP-tool tasks in parallel worktrees", "Add a PLANNING TODO state, driven by the ExitPlanMode matcher", and the original =org_query= / =org_clock_report= / =org_capture= tool headings.

*The one honest source left is git.* The commit that first shows the heading carrying a done keyword is real evidence, reachable with =git log -S= over the heading's =:ID:= line or its title. Nothing else in the tree knows.

*Two things to settle before building, and they are the whole of this heading:*

1. *It dates a different event, and the marker must not pretend otherwise.* A commit timestamp is when the change was *recorded*, not when the work stopped -- and in this project those can be far apart, since state changes reach the file only when a human runs the review pass. A =CLOSED:= written from a commit is therefore an upper bound, not a measurement. Whether that is worth having is the open question, not a detail: =7771fc63= retired an entire feature on the finding that a plausible guess is harder to reject than no guess at all, and this is the same shape of decision.
2. *If it is worth having, the approximation has to be visible in the file*, or the next reader cannot tell these 39 from the 58 that are exact. A property recording the derivation -- or simply not using =CLOSED:= for them at all -- is the choice to make.

*Note the third option, which does not require this to be built:* leave the 39 blank and record explicitly that no close time is recoverable, so the gap is a stated fact rather than an absence somebody tries to fill again in six months. That was offered alongside the git approach and deferred rather than rejected.

*A hazard already measured, and it applies directly here.* f4b07fc0's pre-tool estimate said 59 rather than 58, because a body-wide scan matched a =- State "DONE" ...= line quoted *in prose* on [[id:3d93021d-1d8a-4657-bc5c-77734f91fb9d][3d93021d]], a heading that has no =:LOGBOOK:= and was diagnosing the bug that produced that line. Any git-side matching on heading text or on =State= lines in a diff faces the same trap, with no drawer boundary available to bound it.

*Cancelled 2026-08-24 by the user's decision, and the reasoning is the point rather than the verdict.*

The heading posed the question as "is a commit timestamp a good enough upper bound", and answering *that* would have taken the git archaeology to find out. It turns out not to be the deciding question.

*Nothing mechanical consumes =CLOSED:= on these 39 headings, and nothing ever will.* [[id:8bcd56f4-22a8-4673-bad6-a99433027b81][8bcd56f4]]'s lint rule is scoped to headings closed on or after 2026-08-24, so these are exempt *permanently, by design* -- the scoping is what makes that rule usable at all. Archive routing is per-category =:ARCHIVE:=, not datetree, so nothing files by =CLOSED:=. No clocktable reads it. The only consumer left is a human reading DONE.org, and 35 of the 39 already carry an =:ARCHIVE_TIME:=.

So the work would derive a date from a *different event*, write it into a field nothing reads, on headings in a file that CLAUDE.md says is reference and never orientation. That is the shape [[id:7771fc63-9374-4c8a-9e80-24ceec21977c][7771fc63]] settled against, arrived at from the other direction: not "the guess is unreliable" but "nothing is asking".

*The measurement that was expected to decide it, recorded because it did not:* the 39 are not uniformly pre-cutover. By =:ARCHIVE_TIME:= they split 19 / 3 / 13 / 4 across 2026-07-29, 07-30, 08-17 and none. A pre-cutover close is tightly bounded by its commit, since =org-todo= applied live; a post-cutover one is bounded only by when a human next ran the review pass, which today reached back two weeks. So the bound would have been tight for some and arbitrarily loose for others, with nothing in the file to say which -- a worse outcome than a uniform absence.

*Option (c) is also declined,* for the same reason rather than a different one: marking 39 headings as "no close time recoverable" costs 39 property writes to record an absence that is already visible as an absence. The absence is not ambiguous here -- there is no "nobody looked" reading to resolve, which is what made that distinction worth paying for on [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]].

*What would reopen this:* a consumer appearing. If archive filing ever moves to a datetree ([[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]]'s question), =CLOSED:= becomes load-bearing for placement and these 39 become genuinely undatable rather than merely undated.

*Decided by the user 2026-08-31: option B.* Write =CLOSED:= from git for the 39 headings that have none, and mark each one as derived.

*Both of this heading's original questions are now answered, and by different means.* Whether a commit-derived =CLOSED:= is worth having: yes — every consumer uses it as a placement key, a threshold or a presence test, so nothing mechanical is misled, and the enumeration above is the evidence. How the approximation is marked: with a property, which keeps all three consumers untouched because none of them looks at it.

*The property is =:CLOSED_SOURCE: git=*, absent on the 58 measured ones. Checked before coining: no derivation marker exists in either file today, and the asymmetry is the right way round — a measured =CLOSED:= needs no annotation, so only the 39 carry a line.

*Implementation, mostly already done once.* [[id:38b92521-bba4-4bb5-9cd9-465862471a72][38b92521]]'s completion-time manifest computed git-derived times for 13 of the 39 on 2026-08-17, via =git log --reverse -S= over the heading line, and used them to order the archive sweep. Those 13 can be lifted from that table rather than recomputed. The remaining 26 are the project's earliest headings — the five original tool headings, the hook and trigger batch, =Set git identity= — and predate the =!= cookies, so git is genuinely the only source.

*One trap already found and paid for, recorded in that manifest and worth not rediscovering:* the in-file scan must be scoped to a heading's *own* body. Scanning the subtree dates a parent from one of its children — =b5f7c5c7= came out two days early that way, and it would have mis-ordered the two largest entries with nothing downstream to notice.

*What this unblocks:* [[id:33864a0f-5e13-4235-85a6-0bd9d2fe5252][33864a0f]], which is blocked on this alone. Without it, =org-archive-subtree= would date all 39 from =time= — today — and pile them into a single wrong day node, silently.

*Every consumer of =CLOSED:= enumerated 2026-08-31, because the decision hinges on whether any of them reads it as a measurement.* Three, and none does:

| consumer | how it uses the value |
|----------+-----------------------|
| =org-archive-subtree= (org-archive.el:294) | =(or (org-entry-get nil "CLOSED" t) time)= — the datetree *placement key* |
| =bin/lint-org='s =:PLAN:= rule (config.el:9491) | compared against =claude-code-ide-org-plan-drawer-since= — a *threshold* |
| [[id:f4b07fc0][f4b07fc0]]'s backfill (config.el:9953) | tests *presence*; never reads the value |

*So a value wrong by hours or a day breaks nothing mechanical.* It lands in the right day node or occasionally the adjacent one; it falls on the correct side of a threshold set weeks earlier; it satisfies a presence test. The machine case for refusing an approximation does not exist.

*Which narrows the question to the human, and that is exactly [[id:7771fc63][7771fc63]]'s ground.* That heading retired a guess because "a plausible suggestion is harder to reject than no suggestion at all". The 39 approximations would be indistinguishable from the 58 measurements, permanently, for any reader.

*And the marker costs almost nothing.* Option B — write =CLOSED:= plus a property recording the derivation — leaves all three machine consumers untouched, since none of them looks at the extra property, and gives the human the one thing 7771fc63 says matters. Thirty-nine extra lines, all of them in DONE.org.

*This heading's own second question already assumed marking* — "how the approximation is marked so a reader can tell the 39 from the 58 that are exact" — so B is not a new option, it is the one the heading was already reaching for before it knew the consumers were indifferent.

*What is left to decide is therefore one thing only:* whether the human-facing marker is wanted, given that nothing mechanical needs it. Everything else about this heading is now measured.


* TODO Auto-mark the state items that carry no judgement :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       b6e229c7-3035-4c0e-943b-710a12bfd7d1
:CREATED:  [2026-08-24 Mon 19:08]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-24 Mon 19:08] \\
  measure staleness rate before building
:END:

*Proposed by the user 2026-08-24, after a long review pass:* "it seems like state transitions never require any judgment anymore. could those be applied invisibly or at least marked automatically?"

*Mostly true, and the exception is the whole design.* A =state= item carries exactly one judgement: *staleness*. =--review-state-stale-p= exists because on 2026-08-07 applying a transition whose premise no longer held wrote plausible, correctly-formatted, wrong history -- and that is the failure this project exists to prevent. Everything else about a state item is mechanical.

So the rule is clean, and the predicate for it already exists: =--review-markable-p= encodes precisely "can be marked without asking the human anything". Auto-marking is that predicate applied at *render* time rather than when =M= is pressed.

- *Auto-mark* non-stale =state= items, so they arrive selected.
- *Leave stale ones unmarked*, exactly as =--review-set-all= already refuses them.
- *Do not auto-apply.*

*That last line is load-bearing rather than cautious.* Apply must stay human-triggered: org's native state-change logging only completes inside a genuinely interactive command, which is the constraint the entire queue architecture was built around, and a non-interactive apply hits the hang it exists to route around. Auto-*marking* removes keystrokes without touching that; auto-*applying* would dismantle it.

*A second reason, which the same afternoon supplied.* When an apply reported "Applied 0 item(s); 5 failed", the human had no independent expectation to compare against -- they had pressed =M= and did not know how many it had marked. Pre-marked items make the mismatch immediate: five marked, five failed, and the discrepancy is visible rather than inferred from a truncated message.

*Worth measuring before building:* how often a state item is stale in practice. On 2026-08-24 after two partial applies it was *every* item -- =markable 0= -- because the applies had already moved those headings to the states their events described. If that is typical rather than an artefact of a bad day, auto-marking buys less than it appears to and the honest answer is that the staleness check is doing most of the work.


* TODO Lead every review-buffer rendering with the :ID: prefix, rather than trailing it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       46e4ce2b-fdc8-447b-ad09-b4845602540f
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 08:34]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 08:34] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Reported by the user 2026-08-25*, using =a= in the review buffer: the completion list should carry the =:ID:= prefix the way other renderings do.

[[id:c2132d3f-dd78-4498-ba87-0ca814dc2f77][c2132d3f]] established the convention and its reason: "the review buffer groups items under a heading title alone, so acting on a line means looking its ID up elsewhere -- and this project's own convention cites 8-character prefixes in prose." The assign completion is the same problem in the same buffer, unfixed because it was a different call site.

*Current shape*, from =--assign-candidates=: =(format "%s %s%s  {%s}" range state title short-id)= -- the id trails in braces after the title. That is where every other rendering puts it too, so "begin with the prefix" is a change to the *convention* rather than a divergence from it, and it should be applied everywhere at once or not at all.

*The actual reason, corrected 2026-08-25 by the user.* It is not reachability -- the id is perfectly reachable where it is. It is that *every other place they read an id puts it first*, so the eye arrives expecting a prefix and finds a title. The footnote convention in this project's own conversational replies is the strongest trainer: =^1 `46e4ce2b` -- TODO *title*=, id first, every time.

So this is a *scanning* consistency, not a functional gap, and that changes what a fix has to achieve: matching the shape the reader is already trained on, rather than making anything newly possible.

/An earlier draft of this paragraph argued the id was hard to reach in a/ =completing-read= /and therefore hard to type. That was invented -- a plausible functional justification substituted for the one actually given -- and is recorded rather than deleted because a manufactured rationale in a heading body outlives the conversation that would correct it./

*Note the one entry that has no id and must stay legible:* the meta-work category, offered first, whose "id" is the title =Review and planning= ([[id:9575e65b-b589-465a-830c-286541780f96][9575e65b]]). Whatever prefix format is chosen has to leave that row readable rather than showing a truncated title where a prefix belongs.


* TODO "writes 0:09 in 1" reads as two swappable numbers; "in 1" has no unit :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 08:34]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 08:34] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Reported by the user 2026-08-25* as "it looks like the arguments are reversed in the string interpolation".

*They are not, and checking mattered.* =--review-written-summary= is =(format "writes %d:%02d in %d" (/ minutes 60) (% minutes 60) (length runs))= -- hours, minutes, run count, in that order. Verified against an independently computed case rather than by reading the format: the 2026-08-24 span 16:43--18:06 had nine runs, which had been enumerated separately and summed to about 36 minutes, and it rendered =writes 0:36 in 9=. Duration first, count second, correct.

*What is actually wrong is that nothing says so.* ="in 9"= carries no unit, so ="0:36 in 9"= and ="0:09 in 36"= are equally readable and the two numbers look interchangeable. The reader has to already know the convention to parse the line, and the line exists to tell them something they do not know.

*A person who reads this buffer daily read it as a bug*, which is the measurement that matters -- not whether the code is right but whether the reader can tell. Every candidate fix is a wording change and none touches the computation: ="writes 0:36 across 9 runs"=, ="writes 0:36 in 9 runs"=, or splitting the count onto the annotation where the runs are actually listed.

*Another instance of [[id:6cc71c36-0fa6-4773-9b35-c0582f4cee82][6cc71c36]]*, filed the previous evening: the code knows the second number counts runs, and the message does not say it. That heading proposed an audit of exactly this -- every place the review buffer reports a value, ask whether it distinguishes something the wording does not. This is the first case the audit would have caught, found by a human the next morning instead.

*Worth doing in the same pass as that audit* rather than alone, since a one-word fix here and an unchanged wording three lines below would leave the buffer inconsistent in a way that is worse than uniformly terse.

*Decided 2026-08-28, and the decision is larger than the wording.* Every candidate above is a better label for the same claim — a prediction of what apply will write. That claim is the actual defect, and it turned up independently while judging a second, apparently unrelated bug: the figure is computed *before* =--subtract-intervals= runs, so the buffer can promise 0:11 and apply write less. The obvious repairs were to recompute per refresh (a file read per span item) or to flag items whose target already holds overlapping intervals. Both are features. The cheap answer is to stop making the claim.

*State the evidence, not the outcome.* The span envelope, the runs inside it, and the turn count are all in hand at render time and none of them can disagree with apply, because none of them asserts what apply will do. A readout that reports what was observed needs no recomputation and no staleness flag; the render/apply gap does not get fixed so much as it stops existing.

*This also repairs a misreading the current wording invites.* The user, who reads this buffer daily, took the span to be what lands in the =:LOGBOOK:=. It is not — the span is the envelope, and what lands are the =resume -> pause= runs inside it. The live pending item at the moment of this decision reads =writes 0:01 in 1= over the span 07:44--07:51: seven minutes of wall clock, one minute claimed. Nothing in the line says the other six were not lost, or ignored, or a bug. Reporting the envelope alongside the runs answers that without arithmetic.

*And it is the position [[id:9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5][9e627dc0]] already argued to*, from the other direction: "never present a total that looks complete", and "report the exceptions, not the measurements". That heading also supplies what the gap between envelope and runs *means* rather than leaving it as unexplained shrinkage — a dense run of turns is joint attention, the human reading and typing while the agent works, and the inter-turn gaps are not idle. Its recall-cue framing ("a number they must weigh is heavier than a word they can recognise") is the shape this readout should take, so the two should be built together rather than reworded twice.

*Unchanged:* this still belongs in the [[id:6cc71c36-0fa6-4773-9b35-c0582f4cee82][6cc71c36]] audit pass rather than alone, for the same reason as before.


* TODO Flipping a span's timestamps to active is hand-editing; give the review buffer a key :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 08:52]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 08:52] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Requested by the user 2026-08-26:* "consider adding a keystroke to org-review that flips an inactive interval to active. that edit is tedious for me."

*What is tedious, concretely.* A span is rendered with *inactive* timestamps, and turning =[2026-08-26 Wed 08:12]--[2026-08-26 Wed 08:31]= into the active form means editing four bracket characters by hand in two places, after the item has already been assigned. The review buffer has =e= for editing an interval's *times* but nothing for its *kind*.

*Why it is inactive today, and why the request is not simply a display preference.* =b8e6007a= (in DONE.org) settled that the queue records *agent* activity and the agenda must not absorb it, and the active branch was dropped from =--review-format-annotation= outright. So flipping to active is not a formatting choice -- it asserts *this interval was the human's attention*, which is a different quantity and the one thing the agenda should show.

*Which makes this the manual half of [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]].* That heading proposes clocking the human's review attention from the review command itself. Until it exists, the human is doing by hand exactly what it would automate, and the same day this was requested the user observed they had "forgotten to represent my attention as active spans" -- so the tedium is already producing omissions, not just friction.

*Design questions worth settling before binding a key.*
- *Does the flip mean "this was mine" or merely "show this in the agenda"?* If the former, the marker should probably be recorded on the item rather than inferred from bracket style, and the rendering follow from it.
- *What does it apply to?* A single item, the marked set, or every span in a cluster. Marking a whole review pass as the human's attention in one keystroke is likely the real want.
- *Which key.* =a= is assign, =e= is edit-interval, =N= is note, =d= is dismiss. =t= (for *type*) and =A= (active) both look free; check the keymap before choosing rather than trusting this list.

*Do not let this quietly become [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s substitute.* A key that makes the manual path bearable reduces the pressure to automate it, and the automated version is strictly better -- it knows when the review command was running, which a human retrofitting brackets afterwards is reconstructing from memory.

*Correction [2026-08-26 Wed]: the premise above is stale.* "Until it exists, the human is doing by hand exactly what it would automate" was written while [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]] was open. It is now =DONE= -- the review pass clocks itself onto =Review attention= ([[id:335dfa97-c1b0-4e98-804f-81495eb63f20][335dfa97]]), and the command *is* the event. The automated path exists, so this heading is no longer the only way the human's attention gets represented.

*Which sharpens this heading's own closing caution rather than retiring it.* "Do not let this quietly become 961f15b6's substitute" was a warning about a future; it is now a live constraint. A key that flips brackets by hand is *worse* than what already ships for any interval a review pass covers, because the pass knows when it was running while a human retrofitting brackets afterwards is reconstructing from memory. Binding the key for that case would be building the inferior half of a thing already built.

*So the question narrows to a real one: what attention is neither an agent turn nor a review pass?* Measured 2026-08-26 rather than supposed. The span =[2026-08-26 Wed 13:05]--[2026-08-26 Wed 13:22]= is backed by six guideposts and nothing else. =--span-work-runs= extracts *one minute* of agent work from it, correctly, since only a =resume= -> =pause= adjacency counts. The other fourteen minutes are the user reading a session report: real attention, on this project, captured by nothing. Not agent activity, so [[id:b8e6007a-ae13-4b13-bb2e-a5df39435af0][b8e6007a]] rightly keeps queue-derived spans out of the agenda; not a review pass, so 961f15b6's clock never opened.

*That gap is the actual want behind the original request*, and it is worth separating from the tedium that prompted it. The user's words were "that edit is tedious for me" and, the same day, that they had "forgotten to represent my attention as active spans". The tedium argues for a key. The forgetting argues a key will not fix it either -- an omission caused by having to remember is not repaired by making the remembering cheaper.

*Two neighbours now carry pieces of this.* [[id:996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3][996fd2bd]] proposes reclaiming such a gap when an apply timestamp falls inside it, which is the automatic answer to the same question. [[id:44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc][44cef181]] records that the review buffer *names* an item by that 17-minute window while writing only the 1-minute payload -- so today the readout implies the gap is already accounted for, which is part of why it goes unnoticed.

*Still open and unchanged by any of this*: the three design questions above -- what the flip *means*, what it applies to, and which key.


* TODO A guidepost gap containing an apply timestamp is provably the human's attention :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 10:09]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 10:10] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Found 2026-08-26* while the user asked whether a span they had flipped to active notation "fits the data", and whether such spans should always be offered that way.

*The question a =pause=--=resume= gap cannot answer on its own.* Such a gap says the *agent* was not running. Human attention is a subset of that and the record has never been able to separate them -- the sleeping-laptop gap of 2026-08-25 16:56--17:50 is byte-identical in shape to an hour of careful reviewing.

*But an apply timestamp inside the gap settles it.* [[id:21c91613-76e0-4590-affe-e2af9f1321ca][21c91613]] made every consumed event carry the time of the pass that consumed it, so "was a review pass running between 09:49 and 09:59?" is now a lookup. An apply cannot happen without a human at =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review=; it is the one act in this system that is *definitionally* human. So a gap containing one provably contained human attention.

*Three cases, and it discriminates on all three:*

: 09:23:09--09:29:06   contains pass 09:23:26   -> human
: 09:49:22--09:59:16   contains pass 09:56:09   -> human
: 16:56   --17:50      (2026-08-25, laptop asleep)   no pass   -> not human

The third is the important one: it is the known negative, confirmed independently by =Display is turned off/on= and by scratch-file mtimes, and the rule correctly declines it.

*So the answer to "should they always be offered as active" is no, and the qualifier is mechanisable.* Not every gap -- only one containing an apply. That is a predicate over data the queue already holds, not a judgement, which is what makes it offerable rather than merely arguable.

*What it would change.* [[id:01849bef-fdc9-4c30-b467-00ca8606e8e0][01849bef]] asks for a keystroke because flipping the notation by hand is tedious. This says the review pass can often skip the asking: when a span contains an apply timestamp, render it active and say why. The keystroke remains for the cases the predicate cannot reach -- reading, thinking, deciding without applying anything -- which are real and are exactly what [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s clock now captures going forward.

*Its limits, stated so it is not over-trusted.* It finds only gaps in which someone *applied*; a pass that reviews carefully and dismisses everything writes no apply timestamp and would be missed, which is the same blind spot the "apply completes" stop-signal had in [[id:961f15b6-d782-40b8-8ceb-ba086d188a3b][961f15b6]]'s design. And it is retrospective by nature: it explains gaps already recorded rather than measuring attention as it happens.

*Worth applying to the backlog once*, since the ledger reaches back to 2026-08-18 and the gaps are all still there to be scored.


* TODO g in the review buffer discards unapplied judgement without asking :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       8d0716fe-ca88-429d-b46b-e2f880f5b2cc
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 11:13]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 11:13] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Reported by the user 2026-08-26:* "I have a tendency to hit =g= when I mean =x= and I lose all of my review work." =g= and =x= are adjacent intentions -- refresh and apply -- and one of them is destructive.

*The loss is already documented; what is wrong is the assumption around it.* =claude-code-ide-org-review-refresh='s own docstring spells out the cost -- "every unapplied decision, not only marks: assigned headings, edited intervals, notes and stale confirmations all live in =--review-items= until =x= applies them, and a rebuild replaces that list wholesale" -- and then concludes: *"=g= is typed deliberately, so discarding here is correct; it just has to say so."* That conclusion is what this heading falsifies. It is typed deliberately and it is sometimes typed *instead of* =x=.

*This is the same loss the 2026-08-24 recovery already paid for once* ([[id:7148cddc-aca3-416e-8bcb-f164d4d2bba0][7148cddc]]): "Assignments and edited intervals live in =--review-items=, in memory, never in the queue. Rebuilding items from the queue restores the *events* and discards the judgement applied to them." The user lost a hand-made assignment and an edited interval then, and was told the rollback would "cost the review pass again" -- which sounded like keystrokes and meant decisions.

*Detectable, and checked before filing rather than assumed.* Three of the four kinds leave an unambiguous flag on the item plist:

| judgement | set by | flag |
|-----------+--------+------|
| marks | =review-mark=, =review-mark-all= | =:marked= |
| assignment | =review-assign= | =:assigned t= (and =:id=) |
| note | =review-edit-note= | =:note= |
| edited interval | =review-edit-interval= | *no flag of its own* |

*The fourth needs one line.* =review-edit-interval= overwrites =:start= and =:end= in place, and both keys exist on every span item straight from the queue, so presence proves nothing. It does set =:suggested nil= with the comment "an edited interval is a confirmed one, not a suggestion" -- which is *close* to the signal wanted but not the same claim, since an accepted-as-suggested span may also be confirmed. Adding an explicit =:edited t= beside it is the honest fix.

*So the predicate is:* any item carrying =:marked=, =:assigned=, =:note= or =:edited=. Cheap, exact, and it can also say *how much* is at stake -- "3 marked, 1 assigned, 1 note" reads better than a bare "are you sure?", because the answer depends on the amount.

*Prompt only when there is something to lose.* A confirmation on every =g= trains the reflex that dismisses it, which is how a guard becomes decoration. With nothing pending, =g= should stay instant.

*Worth considering instead of, or alongside, the prompt:* make the discard recoverable. The items are a list in memory; stashing the pre-refresh copy in a buffer-local variable and offering =C-/= or an =undo-refresh= command would make the slip cost a keystroke rather than a decision. That is strictly better than asking, and the two are not exclusive.


* TODO A span's headline interval is not the interval it writes :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Apply
:ID:       44cef181-8dac-4735-8860-f123e132fdbc
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 13:41]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 13:42] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Found by the user 2026-08-26, from a readout they correctly refused to believe:* "I sense a contradiction. you just confirmed that [2026-08-26 Wed 13:05]--[2026-08-26 Wed 13:20] is my attention but something else says otherwise? who wins?"

*Nothing wins, because the two claims are about different intervals -- and the review buffer prints only one of them.* An unassigned span item carries:

- a *window*, rendered as its headline: =[2026-08-26 Wed 13:05]--[2026-08-26 Wed 13:22]=, 17 minutes, being the outer bracket of a guidepost cluster;
- a *payload*, rendered as =writes 0:01 in 1=: one CLOCK line of about a minute, extracted by =--span-work-runs= as the sole =resume= -> =pause= adjacency inside it.

*The two belong to different people.* The payload is agent activity, and [[id:b8e6007a-ae13-4b13-bb2e-a5df39435af0][b8e6007a]] correctly rules it inactive and out of the agenda. The remaining ~14 minutes of the window is the human reading a session report -- real attention, discarded by the extraction rather than recorded as anything. So b8e6007a never sees it, and the natural reading of the item ("this 17-minute range is about to be written down") is false in both directions at once: that range is not what gets written, and the part of it that *is* the human's is not written at all.

*Measured, on the item that produced the complaint.* Six guideposts back it and nothing else -- =pause= 13:05:50, =pause= 13:05:58, =resume= 13:20:21, =pause= 13:20:32, =pause= 13:20:43, =resume= 13:22:51. No =todo= or =clock= event falls inside, which is also why it is unassigned: there is no heading in the window to attribute it to.

*Why this is a readout defect rather than a pipeline defect.* The pipeline is behaving exactly as designed and every number it produces is right. What is wrong is that the item is *named* by a quantity it does not write, so a reader infers the headline is the record. The fix is presentational -- show the run(s) the item will actually write, or mark the window as a bracket rather than an interval -- and deliberately does not touch what is extracted.

*Distinct from the three neighbours it will be mistaken for.* [[id:5a6d2c75-9a42-4dd4-b4d6-670aeebc5b46][5a6d2c75]] wants the *evidence* that would make an unassigned span's heading obvious; [[id:98c302e0-ab1b-40bf-92d0-d2f2e16ac05d][98c302e0]] is about the units in "writes 0:01 in 1"; [[id:46e4ce2b-fdc8-447b-ad09-b4845602540f][46e4ce2b]] is about where the =:ID:= prefix sits on a line. All three are about a line that is hard to read. This one is about a line that is *misleading when read correctly*, which is the sharper failure and needs a different fix.

*Related but not the same*: [[id:996fd2bd-be90-44b3-b2c5-9018255940b3][996fd2bd]] proposes reclaiming a guidepost gap as the human's attention when an apply timestamp falls inside it. That is about *capturing* the missing 14 minutes; this is about not implying they are already captured. If 996fd2bd ships, this heading still stands -- a window and its payload would still be two quantities.

*Filed with a finding attached.* Writing this body hit [[id:478d6ec9-1b99-4c6c-88e9-c793ad4b2f22][478d6ec9]] live: =org_amend='s link checker expanded all four TODO.org prefixes above and refused =b8e6007a=, which is identical in form and differs only in living in DONE.org. So the expander is scoped to TODO.org, and a cross-file reference has to be written as plain text -- which is why one citation here is not a link.


* NEXT A clocktable never shows a parent's own time, only own-plus-subtree :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Clock
:ID:       64d34a64-d336-40a8-a8e8-da8133d0da06
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 14:46]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 14:46] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-26 Wed 14:47] \\
  direct continuation of what 3964c575 decided
:END:

*The reporting half of [[id:3964c575-50bc-4cc9-abeb-bdd1c0162c2c][3964c575]], split out when that heading was decided 2026-08-26.* That one asked whether a grouping may carry its own clock; the answer is yes, a parent's own coordination time is real work. This is what makes that answer *legible*.

*Measured, not inferred.* A parent carrying 1:00 of its own, with children of 2:00 and 0:30:

: | A story        | 3:30 |      |
: | \_  Child one  |      | 2:00 |
: | \_  Child two  |      | 0:30 |

*The parent's own 1:00 appears nowhere.* This is worse than the usual framing, which is that own time is "mixed in and cannot be told apart" -- it is not printed at all. It is recoverable only as =parent_row - sum(child_rows)=, which is arithmetic the reader performs silently and which =:maxlevel= truncation breaks without warning: cap the depth and the subtraction quietly returns the wrong number, with nothing on the page indicating that it did.

*So the 1:08 on [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] was never a rounding artefact.* 3964c575's body recorded "parent row 14:52, ten children summing 13:44, the 1:08 difference indistinguishable from a rounding artefact". It was the parent's own two CLOCK lines, and it was indistinguishable only because the table declines to name it.

*No native parameter does this.* Checked in org 9.8.7: the clocktable has no own-time or non-recursive option, and =:properties= only adds property columns, not a derived quantity. =org-clock-sum= is recursive by definition and writes one =CLOCKSUM= per heading.

*Scope: presentation only, and deliberately so.* Nothing about what is extracted, stored or applied changes. Candidate shapes, none chosen -- a =:formatter= adding an own-time column; a project-level clocktable wrapper; or surfacing it only where it matters rather than in every table. Whether every parent row wants a second number, or only the nine that currently carry own lines, is the first thing to settle.

*Nine headings currently make this visible*, measured 2026-08-26: 46 own CLOCK lines across four active and five finished groupings.


* TODO Orient the datetree newest-at-top, by re-sorting after day-node creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Meta
:ID:       0c3f1319-67c0-4b29-a8e7-5ffa12948aaf
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 14:57]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 14:58] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
CLOCK: [2026-08-26 Wed 15:33]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:36] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-26 Wed 15:33]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:36] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-26 Wed 15:43]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:44] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-26 Wed 15:43]--[2026-08-26 Wed 15:44] assigned at review
:END:

*Requested by the user 2026-08-26*, while noticing that the [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] flattening analysis "both forgot about datetrees". The ask was for a custom sort function giving the datetree a newest-at-top orientation, to match the same recency ordering proposed for TODO.org and DONE.org.

*No custom sort function is needed, measured.* =(org-sort-entries nil ?A)= on the month node produces =("2026-08-21" "2026-08-17" "2026-08-14")= in one call. Org's day nodes are ISO-prefixed, so lexical order *is* date order and reverse-alphanumeric *is* newest-first. Same for month nodes within a year and year nodes within the anchor. The comparator this needs already ships.

*The work is placement, not comparison.* =org-datetree-find-date-create= assumes ascending and inserts accordingly, so a newly created node lands in the wrong place in a descending tree -- measured: filing 2026-08-25 into a descending month appended it at the *bottom*. One =org-sort-entries= call afterwards corrects it, verified end to end:

: before create : ("08-21" "08-17" "08-14")
: after create  : ("08-21" "08-17" "08-14" "08-25")
: after resort  : ("08-25" "08-21" "08-17" "08-14")

*So the change is a re-sort at one call site*, =claude-code-ide-org--ensure-day-node=, which already creates the node, stamps =:CREATED:=, takes an =:ID:= and saves. Sorting the three scaffolding levels before that save is contained and idempotent.

*The hazard, which is real but not on this project's path.* Raw =org-datetree-find-date-create= does a *sorted scan with early exit*, so against a descending tree it passes the target, concludes the date is absent, and creates a **duplicate day node**. Measured: filing an already-present 2026-08-17 produced =("08-17" "08-21" "08-17" "08-14")=. Two nodes for one day, each taking its own =:ID:=, would split that day's meta-work clock silently -- the worst available failure for this subsystem.

*It does not reach us because =--ensure-day-node= never calls org's finder blind.* It looks the node up first via =claude-code-ide-org--find-day-node=, which scans *every* heading in the subtree matching on exact rendered title, with no early exit and no order assumption. Verified against a descending tree: all three present days found, a nonexistent one correctly nil. Org's finder is reached only when the day genuinely does not exist, which is the one case where insertion order cannot duplicate anything.

*What remains exposed is anything that bypasses that wrapper* -- an =org-capture= template targeting =file+olp+datetree=, or a hand =C-c C-j=. Neither is configured here today. If the tree is reoriented, that becomes a standing constraint worth writing down rather than a latent bug: *the day node is created through =--ensure-day-node= or not at all.*

*Independent of the flattening decision.* This is wanted whether or not [[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]] goes ahead, and touches none of the same code.

*Superseded [2026-08-26 Wed], before anything was built.* The re-sort-after-creation plan above is a workaround for a limitation org removed in 9.8. Use the supported extension point instead.

*=org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= takes a comparison function per level*, and =org-datetree-comparefun-from-regex= is public specifically to build them. The find-or-create scan is driven *entirely* by that comparator -- =--find-create-subheading= walks siblings for the first one where the result is =>= 0=, inserts before it, and detects an existing entry by the same function returning =0= or =t=. So inverting the comparator inverts insertion *and* lookup together, which is why the duplicate hazard disappears rather than being worked around.

*Measured on org 9.8.7, comparator simply negated:*

| filed date       | found existing | resulting order         |
|------------------+----------------+-------------------------|
| 08-25 (newest)   | nil, created   | 25, 21, 17, 14 -- top   |
| 08-16 (interior) | nil, created   | 21, 17, 16, 14 -- slot  |
| 08-17 (present)  | *t*            | unchanged, no duplicate |

*So the change is a call-site swap, not a sort pass.* =--ensure-day-node= currently calls the legacy =org-datetree-find-date-create=, which hardcodes ascending. Passing three descending comparators to =org-datetree-find-create-hierarchy= with ='subtree-at-point= replaces it. The =--find-day-node= title pre-check can stay -- it is order-independent and cheap -- but it stops being load-bearing, since org's own finder now handles the existing-date case correctly.

*The community history, since it explains why this took sixteen years.* Evan asked for exactly this on emacs-orgmode in April 2010 -- "a date tree with the recent dates on the top in contrast to the normal date tree". Carsten Dominik declined it as deliberate rather than missing: "I have a hard time why, if you are creating a new node every day, you'd like the subnodes first on top." The gap was filled out-of-tree by =org-reverse-datetree= (akirak), still on MELPA.

*And it was never granted on its own merits.* What landed in 9.8 is a generalisation of =:tree-type= to arbitrary subsets of =(year quarter month week day)= plus arbitrary functions, motivated by lunar, academic and retail 4-4-5 calendars. Reverse ordering is an *incidental* capability of the comparator seam that generalisation required. Worth knowing, because it means there is no first-class =org-datetree-reversed= option to wait for and probably never will be -- the seam is the answer org intends.

*Consequence for the flattening question* ([[id:29439196-bbb8-4b64-b8d5-3bf9d457bf6c][29439196]]): none. This is orthogonal and was already noted there as independent.


* TODO Name the work a slice forgot: detect the omission, never fix it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Slices
:ID:       c60a1c53-ba7f-40a2-979a-6ce0a16ce3c8
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 15:35]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 15:35] \\
  the user had to remind me; a mechanism should
:END:

*Prompted 2026-08-25 by the user asking whether they have to remind me to add a worked heading to the open slice.* They did. [[id:0acc1df2-6916-4180-867a-99bfa551806b][0acc1df2]] was built, committed, and left out of [[id:c44c2119-dbf8-4510-99e2-6aaa7f259d94][c44c2119]] -- against a membership rule written earlier the same day, by me, saying a member is anything the slice *committed to or actually worked*.

*It must not add anything, and that is the design rather than caution.* Membership is what a slice *declares*; it is the entire difference between a slice and an epic, which is derived. A mechanism that swept in every heading touched would produce a backlog wearing a slice's clothes, which is the specific thing the user asked to resist while this slice finishes. So the job is to make forgetting *loud*, not to make remembering unnecessary.

*Choosing the signal is the whole problem, and it was measured rather than guessed.* Three candidates, against today as the test case:

| signal                                   | candidates | catches today |
|------------------------------------------+------------+---------------|
| state changed during the apply            | --         | *no*          |
| id appears anywhere in a commit since the slice opened | 27 | yes    |
| id appears in a commit *subject*          | 3          | yes           |

*The state-change signal fails on the case that prompted this*, which is what makes it worth recording. Nothing was queued for 0acc1df2 -- no keyword moved, no clock ran. It was built, and building leaves no trace the queue can see.

*Commit-body mentions are useless at 27*, because this project's commit prose cites ids constantly as cross-references. A report that lists 27 things stops being read, which is [[id:5ff5a4b8-2fee-472a-b5e1-c9de8cf96704][5ff5a4b8]]'s finding arriving again.

*A subject line is a claim that the commit is **about** that heading*, and it narrows to three. Today's own commit reads "Build the slice regenerator: 0acc1df2". Cross-references stay in the body where they belong and are excluded for free.

*Where it runs: the end of a review pass.* Two reasons rather than one. A human is already there making per-item decisions, so asking costs nothing extra; and the =--review-settle-auto-promote= seam already exists for exactly this shape -- something that must run once, after the batch, against the settled state.

*Open, and worth deciding before building:* whether the report should also fire when *no* slice is open, which would be a different and probably more annoying question -- "you did work and no slice claims it" is sometimes just true.

*The same signal answers a second question, found 2026-08-25 when the user asked whether the session's work was attached to any task at all.* It was not: 52 state changes queued that day and *zero* clock events, the last having been 2026-08-24 16:42.

*The mechanism is visible in those two numbers.* Every heading went =TODO= -> =DONE= in one step. That is not a listed transition -- the table has =DOING= -> =DONE=, =PLANNING= -> =DONE=, =REVIEW= -> =DONE= -- so skipping the clocked state means no rule was ever triggered to open a clock. The omission is structural rather than forgetful: nothing asks for a clock on a transition that was never made.

*So the detector proposed here generalises for free.* An id in a commit subject is evidence that the commit was *about* that heading. Cross that against the day's clock intervals and two questions fall out of one scan:

- *which worked headings no slice names* -- this heading's original subject; and
- *which worked headings carry no clock interval that day* -- the attribution gap.

*The second is the more valuable of the two, on the day's evidence.* A forgotten slice member is a bookkeeping error someone notices at review. Un-clocked work is invisible: it lands on the meta-work day node as an unassigned span, indistinguishable at review from planning, and the human attributing it has no way to tell that three of those hours were implementation on a specific heading.

*And it is exactly the residue [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] shipped to eliminate*, which is the sharp part. That heading made explicit clock pairs authoritative so attribution would stop needing a human -- and it is the first member of the slice during which the pairs stopped being produced at all. It closed the consumption half; the discipline half regressed behind it.


* TODO Make task lifecycle answerable from one timeline, starting at creation :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       d9132c57-9dcf-47e9-ba74-7f5a1ced3ae5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-11 Tue 10:32]
:END:

Prompted 2026-08-11 by a concrete reporting question — "how long did it
take from capture time to the PLANNING phase, for each of these tasks?"
— which is the first properly articulated demand on this project's
second stated goal, trustworthy tracking of where attention actually
went. It cannot be answered today, and the reason is not the one it
looks like.

*Measured 2026-08-11:*

: TODO.org  headings carrying :CREATED:   56
: TODO.org  "- State ..." lines            7
: DONE.org  "- State ..." lines            0

All seven come from the two hooks that write their note explicitly
(=--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=, =--promote-planning-to-doing=). Not
one came from =org_set_todo=, which binds =org-inhibit-logging= to =t=
unconditionally for every state. So the transition history barely
exists, and in DONE.org — where post-hoc analysis would actually look —
it does not exist at all. That zero is *not* the archiver stripping
drawers: =org-archive-subtree= moves the whole subtree intact. Nothing
was ever written.

The example question is the worst case of this. =PLANNING= is transient
— auto-promoted to =DOING= the instant a plan is approved — and the
same census found *zero* headings sitting in it. Current state can
therefore never answer "when did this reach PLANNING"; only a
transition log can.

*So the blocker is the missing log, not =:CREATED:='s location,* and
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]] already fixes it:
apply runs inside a genuinely interactive command, which is what lets
org's native logging fire instead of being suppressed. This heading is
the remaining half — making the timeline start at the right place — and
is close to worthless before the cutover, since there would be a
creation line and then silence.

*Proposal: duplicate =:CREATED:= into =:LOGBOOK:= as a creation entry.
Do not move it.* As a property it is cheaply queryable — org-ql filters
and sorts on properties directly — and drawer text is not. Moving it
trades an easy query for a parse. Duplicating buys one timeline and one
parser: analysis becomes "read the entries in order" rather than "read
a property, read a drawer, reconcile two timestamp conventions." It also
gives [[id:af7d3687-6d1e-4480-a9e2-df772785713f][af7d3687]]'s single
chronological drawer somewhere to begin.

*org already has the shape, so nothing needs inventing.* With no
previous state org renders the field as an empty string — =org.el= line
11092, =(or org-log-note-previous-state "")= — producing a native
creation entry with no previous state.

*Corrected 2026-08-11, twice, both by direct test rather than reading.*

1. *The literal above was wrong.* The empty string is padded, not
   quoted, so the real rendering carries no ="= pair at all:

   : - State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-11 Tue 15:46]

   A parser keyed on =from ""= matches nothing. The claim that "existing
   parsers, and org-ql, handle it unmodified" was never verified and
   should be re-checked against that actual string before anything is
   built on it.

2. *=TODO= alone carried no =!= marker*, so the one transition this
   feature most needs — into =TODO=, the default keyword a capture
   receives — logged nothing, silently and without error. Interactivity
   is necessary for native logging but not sufficient; logging also has
   to be *requested*. Fixed by
   [[id:90a5a0cf-a81d-45dc-a453-0bfe61e3ef93][90a5a0cf]]. Had this
   shipped as written, it would have produced no creation entries for
   precisely the common case and looked like a bug in apply.

*The alternative, which may well be better and is not yet weighed:*
synthesise the creation entry *at read time*, in whatever reporting
function consumes this, from the =:CREATED:= property that already
exists. Nothing in any file changes, there is no duplication to diverge,
and no migration. The cost is that every consumer must know to do it,
where writing it once puts it in the record for anything that reads the
drawer — including a human. Decide this before writing anything.

*If the write-it-down option wins, two consequences:*
- *Backfill.* 56 headings have =:CREATED:= and no creation entry. A
  one-time pass can synthesise them from the property — mechanical and
  safe, with precedent in the retrospective consolidation pass recorded
  in DONE.org. Without it the analysis has a cutoff date.
- *It depends on [[id:cdcc065c-628d-4350-b1ba-fcf686e9c4c1][cdcc065c]].*
  =org_capture= does not write =:CREATED:= at all, so headings born from
  the tool have no creation time for a backfill to read. Fix that first
  or those headings have no origin in either place.

*Mechanism, if written at creation time:* it need not be special. Under
the queue model creation can simply *be* the =capture= event proposed in
[[id:7fa68d5c-b6c0-43df-85c6-764b1d56511f][7fa68d5c]], with apply
writing its =:LOGBOOK:= entry exactly as it writes a state transition.
The two headings are one feature approached from different sides —
capture durability there, timeline origin here.

*One semantic caveat for whoever writes the parser:* "created" and
"acquired its first TODO keyword" are not the same event in general.
They coincide throughout this project because every task heading is born
with a keyword, but the equivalence is a local accident, not a rule, and
a heading that gains a keyword later would make them diverge.


* TODO Hook-injected context is only as reliable as the model choosing to relay it :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Skill
:ID:       c5b02503-1700-47fd-bf89-bd3296b24af2
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 10:32]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-14 Fri 10:32] \\
  captured from conversation, unexamined assumption
:END:

*Raised 2026-08-14, tracing where the session-start report is defined.*
Both =SessionStart= hooks work by injecting =additionalContext=, and
*nothing makes the model relay or act on it.* If a session reads the
context and says nothing, the outcome is indistinguishable from the hook
never having run — silent on both sides. CLAUDE.md already states the
expectation without noticing it is an assumption: the report "is injected
as =additionalContext=, which Claude is expected to relay to the user as
a question — the hook itself has no way to literally prompt."

*Evidence from the session that found it, cutting both ways.* The relay
worked: this session opened by surfacing
[[id:b5f94b88-76e3-4a3c-ac73-729f34d33691][b5f94b88]] from the injected
context. But it worked partly because the user's first prompt was "what's
next?", which made the line directly relevant. Had the session opened
with a narrow request, the same line would have sat in context with no
reason to mention it, and the user would have had no way to know it
existed. n=1, and the one trial was the favourable case.

*Two hooks already depend on it,* and both are load-bearing:
=.claude/hooks/session-context.sh= (what was I last doing) and
=bin/hooks/session-start-recovery-check= (a CLOCK interval left open
before today, which needs the user to be *asked* when work actually
stopped — the whole point is a question).

*A third is about to be added on the strength of the first two.*
[[id:d7849119-8224-4993-a8ab-83e1434df53c][d7849119]] part 2 specifies a
=PostToolUse= hook injecting a reminder to check the sibling file, and
justifies the choice as mirroring "the existing =SessionStart=
stale-interval-recovery hook's pattern (inject context, let Claude
relay/act on it) rather than hard-blocking the edit, which would be
heavier-handed than this warrants." That reasoning is sound about
*blocking* and silent about *reliability*: it adopts inject-and-hope as a
house pattern citing a precedent whose relay rate nobody has ever
checked. Deciding this heading first is what stops the assumption being
copied a third time.

*Options, none chosen.*

- *A channel the human reads directly,* which removes the model from the
  loop entirely. The statusline is the only one that exists —
  [[id:4cda6bf7-441b-47b2-b3d5-b8c7e7a207a5][4cda6bf7]] and
  [[id:290b6fc5-3ebb-4827-9a97-5a3542e354b6][290b6fc5]] both live there,
  and *neither names this as a reason to want them.* The fix exists in
  the codebase as a capability and nowhere as an argument.
- *Make the hook's own output user-visible,* if Claude Code surfaces hook
  stdout to the user for any hook event. *Unverified* — worth checking
  against the current hook documentation before it is either relied on or
  dismissed.
- *Accept it and measure.* Nothing today records whether injected context
  was relayed, so "usually works" is not a claim anyone can currently
  make. The audit log and the event queue both record tool calls; neither
  records what a session chose to say.

*The sharpest version of the problem:* the recovery hook exists to ask a
question only the human can answer, and its delivery depends on the party
that cannot be held to it. That is the same shape as the reason apply is
human-triggered — a mechanism whose correctness rests on the model doing
something it was merely asked to do.


* TODO Reconstruct coarse day-level attention intervals for the agenda :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       4f8500e6-b468-4fd4-a0f6-ebf04c7a57ba

  manual pass, no blockers found

CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 11:51]--[2026-08-14 Fri 11:52] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-14 Fri 11:51]--[2026-08-14 Fri 11:52] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 12:07]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:09] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-14 Fri 12:07]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:09] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 12:16]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:18] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-14 Fri 12:16]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:18] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 12:22]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:28] =>  0:06
- [2026-08-14 Fri 12:22]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:28] suggested span, accepted at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-14 Fri 12:31]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:36] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-14 Fri 12:31]--[2026-08-14 Fri 12:36] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:

*Named 2026-08-14, after establishing what the system does and does not
record.* The queue records *Claude sessions*; it has never recorded the
user's own attention. Clocking manually in Emacs writes a =CLOCK:= line
and nothing else, because the active annotation is this module's
apply-time addition rather than org's. So solo work lands in the same
agenda-invisible bucket as unattended subagent work, for an entirely
different reason:

| producer                    | CLOCK | annotation   | clocktable | agenda |
|-----------------------------+-------+--------------+------------+--------|
| Claude session, via apply   | yes   | active =<>=  | yes        | yes    |
| subagent, via apply         | yes   | inactive =[]=| yes        | no     |
| the user, clocking in Emacs | yes   | none         | yes        | *no*   |

Measured the same day: TODO.org holds 84 =CLOCK:= lines and 52 active
annotations, and the ~33 without one all predate the 2026-08-11 cutover.

*The intended granularity is coarse, and this is the load-bearing
constraint.* Stated by the user: "I expect my attention intervals to be
coarser and just a bulk representation of what did I do that day." *Not*
one annotation per CLOCK line, and not an attempt to align with the
recorded intervals at all. That rules out the obvious helper — a command
that annotates the CLOCK line at point — which was offered and declined
for exactly this reason. A day-level summary is a different artifact
from a reconstruction of the clock.

*Doing it through the review buffer does not work, and the reason is
structural.* Spans are reconstructed from =pause=/=resume= guideposts,
which come only from Claude Code's =Stop=/=UserPromptSubmit= hooks. No
session running means no guideposts, means no span to review. The buffer
can *correct* a span that exists (=e= for endpoints, =a= to assign) but
cannot *create* one for a period the queue never saw. Time spent alone in
Emacs is therefore outside it in every era, not merely before the
cutover.

*So this is manual work in Emacs, by design and by preference,* and what
would help is small: =RET= from the review buffer now unfolds the target
=:LOGBOOK:= (2026-08-14), and
=claude-code-ide-org--consolidate-drawer-at-point= will re-sort a drawer
after hand-editing — though it is not =(interactive)= yet, so it needs
=M-:= or a two-line wrapper. Org inserts CLOCK lines newest-first while
notes land in insertion order, so a hand-edited drawer drifts out of
chronological order quickly.

*Sequencing, worked out 2026-08-14.* Nothing pending changes how
intervals are *derived*, so nothing blocks this: =ab75d6d2=,
=d1cf852a=, =c954f650= and =2aeb65d6= are all irrelevant to it. The one
apparent dependency, [[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]
(re-measure the guidepost gap threshold), is weaker than it looks: its
own data found span count flat across 1200-1800s with no observations
between 1061s and 2070s, so a re-measure is unlikely to change any
reconstruction.

The asymmetry that governs the order: the queue keeps every raw event
and can be re-clustered at will, while annotations written into
=:LOGBOOK:= *cannot be re-derived* — redoing them means hand-editing org
files. So do this after anything that changes derivation, and before
anything that puts raw material out of reach.

*One thing to avoid meanwhile:* =M-x claude-code-ide-org-archive-finished-queues=.
It is manual, so it will not fire on its own, but it has already moved
seven sessions (back to 2026-08-08) out of the review buffer's sight,
and its own docstring notes that a restored file which lost its
watermark supports "only all-or-nothing" reprocessing.

*The producer table above is superseded, same day it was written.*
=claude-code-ide-org--review-format-annotation= used to branch on an
item's =:agent:=, writing an *active* timestamp for anything without one.
That is the row reading "Claude session, via apply | active =<>= | agenda
yes". The branch was removed on 2026-08-14 — measured: all 68 events of
one session carried =agent_id= nil, so every span it produced was
published to the agenda as though the user had been at the keyboard. Apply
now writes inactive timestamps unconditionally.

*Which dissolves the contrast this heading was named around.* The
paragraph above objects that solo work "lands in the same agenda-invisible
bucket as unattended subagent work" while Claude-session work reaches the
agenda. After the correction all three producers are agenda-invisible, so
solo work is no longer the anomaly — it is the rule. The heading's purpose
survives, but its motivating asymmetry does not; the reason to build
attention intervals is now that *nothing* populates the agenda, rather
than that the wrong thing does.

*Left behind, and this is the part that bites:* 55 active =<>= annotations
remain in TODO.org, dated 2026-08-10 through 2026-08-14, against 14
inactive ones written since. The fix stopped the bleeding and cleaned
nothing. Those 55 are agent-derived spans currently rendering in the
agenda as human attention — and they occupy precisely the namespace this
heading intends to fill. A first attention pass would be reading an agenda
that is almost entirely mislabelled agent activity, which makes the
cleanup a precondition rather than tidying. Tracked separately.


* TODO Record turns-per-interval as an attention signal, and decide where it surfaces :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Queue
:ID:       9e627dc0-d31b-42cc-857a-b1e5a77f44a5
:CREATED:  [2026-08-15 Sat 14:07]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
