Observed 2026-08-19: an apply pass refused
[[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]]'s =none -> TODO=
because =--at-id= could not find the heading. The heading existed; the
index did not know where.

*Cause.* It was captured with no target, so
[[id:97696fc2-f07b-4586-b280-de6a7d26fb87][97696fc2]] put it in
=~/org/notes.org=, and it was then moved into =TODO.org= by cutting and
re-inserting the subtree text. That never touches =org-id-locations=, so
=org-id-find= kept resolving to a file the heading had left.

*Wider than its cause.* Any hand move between files does this — a
=git= operation, an =Edit= tool call, a refile done as text. Only
=org-refile= maintains the index, and this project edits org text
directly all the time.

*Candidate fixes, none chosen.* Call =org-id-update-id-locations= after
any tool that relocates a subtree; or have =--at-id= fall back to a scan
of the tracked files when the index misses, which is self-healing and
costs a scan only on the failing path. The second also covers moves made
outside Emacs entirely, which the first cannot.

*Left as a question:* whether the =Error:= that reaches the review
buffer should distinguish "no such :ID: anywhere" from "not where the
index says", since the two want different responses and currently read
identically.


* DOING [0/1] Review how the emacs-tools are advertised, against which ones get used :code:research:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       420816ec-677e-45a5-b898-fb5db70d214b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 07:41]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 07:41] \\
  seven of thirteen tools never called
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 07:47]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:52] =>  0:05
- [2026-08-20 Thu 07:47]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:52] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-20 Thu 07:49] \\
  re-queued; keyword was dismissed
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 07:57]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:58] =>  0:01
- [2026-08-20 Thu 07:57]--[2026-08-20 Thu 07:58] assigned at review
- State "DOING"      from "TODO"       [2026-08-20 Thu 21:10] \\
  measuring usage across 69 transcripts
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 07:49]--[2026-08-21 Fri 07:56] =>  0:07
- [2026-08-21 Fri 07:49]--[2026-08-21 Fri 07:56] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 07:59]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:02] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-21 Fri 07:59]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:02] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-21 Fri 08:43]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:45] =>  0:02
- [2026-08-21 Fri 08:43]--[2026-08-21 Fri 08:45] assigned at review
:END:

Thirteen tools are advertised. Over a two-day session that did almost
nothing *but* org work, *seven were never called once*: =org_query=,
=org_refile=, =org_move_sibling=, =org_sort_children=,
=org_clock_report=, =org_archive=, =org_log_background_plan=.

*And the work they exist for was done anyway, by other means.* That is
the finding — not that they went unneeded, but that they went unreached:

| what happened                                    | tool not reached for |
|--------------------------------------------------+----------------------|
| cross-file searches, dozens of them              | =org_query=          |
| moved three subtrees between files and levels    | =org_refile=         |
| ordered a composed epic's children               | =org_sort_children=  |
| totalled clock time over the whole corpus        | =org_clock_report=   |

Each was done with =grep=, =python=, or hand-written =org-cut-subtree=
/=org-paste-subtree= elisp. In every case the tool would have been
shorter and safer; the hand-rolled subtree move is exactly what left
=org-id= stale in
[[id:8ddd7fa8-e847-42e9-b82c-63bbe4c11b7b][8ddd7fa8]].

*Four specific mis-advertisements, each with a cost already paid:*

1. =org_capture='s =target= is optional and omitting it writes *outside
   the repository*, to the capture file. The description does not say
   so. Cost: a heading filed where the user could not see it
   \([[id:97696fc2-f07b-4586-b280-de6a7d26fb87][97696fc2]]\).
2. =org_capture= can only target an =:ID:= or a top-level heading *of
   the capture file*, so filing under a real category is impossible —
   categories deliberately carry no =:ID:=, and =bin/lint-org= enforces
   that. The natural call cannot be expressed, so headings get
   hand-written instead.
3. =org_amend= only ever appends. Nothing says so at the call site, and
   it is the mechanism behind
   [[id:b07df584-3adc-433c-ab49-e104f88aecf7][b07df584]]: every summary
   and correction lands furthest from where a reader starts.
4. =org_set_todo= accepted a transition to the state already held, four
   times in one day, before =c2cf203= refused it.

*The question this heading asks* is not "are the descriptions accurate"
— mostly they are, and several are excellent. It is whether they are
written so a model reaches for them *at the moment of need*. A
description read once at session start competes with =grep=, which needs
no recall at all. What would change that is unproven: naming the
alternative it beats, stating the failure it prevents, or something
else.

*Measurement to repeat, since it is cheap:* the queue's =source= field
records every tool that queues, and this session shows =org_set_todo= 36,
=org_clock_out= 10, =org_clock_in= 9. The immediate tools leave no trace
at all, which is itself worth fixing before judging them — right now
"never used" and "used and forgotten" are indistinguishable for seven of
the thirteen.

*Measured 2026-08-20 across all 69 session transcripts in this project.* The measurement half of this heading is now done; what remains is the child and whatever follows from finding 4.

*1. The original premise held for a session and does not hold for the corpus.* Only *one* tool of fourteen has never been called in any session: =org_sort_children=. =org_query=, =org_refile= and =org_move_sibling=, named above as never called, have since been used -- 27, 14 and 2 times. The "seven of thirteen" observation was true of the two-day session it was made in, and that scoping is what makes it interesting rather than wrong.

*2. Per-session breadth is the real number, and it is low.* Of 20 sessions making 10 or more org tool calls, the median reaches for *5 distinct tools of 14*. No session used more than 8. So the surface is not unused; it is unused *at any given moment*.

*3. =org_amend= is the sharpest case of recall rather than utility.* 121 lifetime calls -- the third most-used tool -- yet absent from 16 of 20 active sessions. When it is remembered it carries a session; most sessions never think of it. Also absent from most active sessions: =org_log_background_plan= (19/20), =org_archive= (18/20), =org_refile= (18/20), =org_move_sibling= (18/20), =org_clock_report= (17/20).

*4. The finding that reframes this heading: a standing rule works and a good description does not.* CLAUDE.md requires calling =org_pending_updates= before the first state or clock call of a session. Compliance since that rule landed on 2026-08-17 is *4 of 4 sessions, 100%*; before it, 3 of 34, and those three were coincidence. Small n and worth re-measuring, but unambiguous so far.

Set against it, on the same day: =org_capture='s description states in capital letters that it writes NO TODO keyword and that =org_set_todo= must follow. That instruction was ignored five times in one session by a reader who had the description in context, losing four heading bodies ([[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]]).

*So the title's framing may be wrong.* This heading asks how the tools are *advertised*. The evidence says advertising is not the lever that moves usage -- a directive in CLAUDE.md is, because it is read at session start rather than at the moment of a call that is never made. If under-use is worth fixing, the intervention to test is a standing rule naming the tool for a situation, not a better =:description=.

*What not to conclude.* Low breadth is not obviously a defect. Sorting a heading's children is genuinely rare, and =org_sort_children='s zero calls may be honest rather than a discovery failure. Deciding which of the six under-used tools deserve a rule, and which are simply niche, is the remaining judgement and is not answered by any of the above.


** TODO An MCP tool accepts arguments it does not have, and says nothing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 16:45]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 16:45] \\
  silent parameter drop; found by its own consequence
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 17:18]--[2026-08-20 Thu 17:22] =>  0:04
- [2026-08-20 Thu 17:18]--[2026-08-20 Thu 17:22] assigned at review
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 21:08]--[2026-08-20 Thu 21:11] =>  0:03
- [2026-08-20 Thu 21:08]--[2026-08-20 Thu 21:11] assigned at review
:END:

=org_capture= takes =(title target tags note)=. On 2026-08-20 it was called five times with two invented arguments, =todo_state= and =body=. Every call succeeded, returned /"Captured: ... (ID: ...)"/, and silently dropped both.

*The measured damage, all in one session.* Three headings landed with no TODO keyword. *Four landed with no body at all* -- =41cf5830=, =c6fc6f46=, =5a5e87c9= and this one -- losing every word of analysis written into them. Only =a729c32a= survived, and only because =org_amend= was called on it later for an unrelated reason.

*Neither loss announced itself.* A keywordless heading is absent from every agenda TODO view, invisible to =org_query todo:TODO=, uncounted by its parent's statistics cookie, and ignored by =--trigger-auto-promote-sole-todo=. An empty body looks exactly like a heading someone has not written up yet. Both were reported to the user as filed work, with content quoted back that was never in the file.

*It went unnoticed for five hours and was found by accident* -- the user asked where a concept had been parked, and the heading holding it was empty. Nothing in the tool's replies, the lint run, the test suite or the review queue could have surfaced it, because from every one of those angles a heading with a title and an =:ID:= is well-formed.

*The tool was not at fault and this is the point.* Its description says, in capitals, that the heading is written with /NO TODO keyword/ and that the state must be set afterwards with =org_set_todo=; it advertises no =body= parameter at all. The documentation was correct, prominent, and unread. Only the silence made the error survivable. A parameter the schema does not declare should be an error, not a no-op -- the same argument =org_set_todo= already accepts for an undeclared keyword, which returns /"WAIT is not a TODO keyword in this file (have: ...)"/ rather than proceeding.

*Where the check belongs is the open question.* If =claude-code-ide-mcp= dispatches by arity or by plist, an unknown key may be discarded before this module ever sees it, in which case this is an upstream report ([[id:9d6449c7-529f-49a0-b28f-015cce869715][9d6449c7]]) rather than a local fix. Establish which before proposing anything.

*Related defect found in the same reading:* =org_capture='s own description contained a truncated sentence -- /"Run org_outline first if you need to see what "/ ran straight into /"Returns a confirmation containing the "/, losing "categories exist." Repaired 2026-08-20. Evidence for this parent's premise that the advertising has never been read end to end.

/Body restored 2026-08-20 by =org_amend=; the original capture silently discarded it -- by the very defect this heading describes./

*Gating question answered 2026-08-20: the drop is upstream, so this is a report, not a local fix.* =claude-code-ide-mcp-http-server--validate-args= iterates over =arg-specs= -- the declared schema -- and pulls each declared name out of the supplied =args= alist. It never iterates =args=. A key the schema does not declare is therefore never looked at, and silence is structural rather than a missing check:

: (dolist (spec arg-specs (nreverse result))
:   (let* ((name (plist-get spec :name))
:          (optional (plist-get spec :optional))
:          (value (alist-get (intern name) args)))
:     (when (and (not optional) (not value))
:       (signal 'json-rpc-error (list -32602 (format "Missing required argument: %s" name))))
:     (push value result)))

Note the asymmetry: a *missing required* argument signals =-32602=, so the machinery for refusing a malformed call already exists and is used. Only the unknown-key direction is unguarded. That makes the upstream report a small one -- one pass over =args= for keys absent from =arg-specs= -- rather than a design change. Belongs with [[id:9d6449c7-529f-49a0-b28f-015cce869715][9d6449c7]] alongside the =200=-versus-=202= finding.

There is a second dispatch path, =claude-code-ide-mcp.el= line 352, which hands the whole alist to a handler; it was not examined. Establish whether it has the same gap before writing the report.

*The same reading turned up a live trap in this project's own schema, now fixed.* Keys are matched verbatim -- =(intern name)= against the JSON key -- so the advertised spelling is what a caller must send. =org_sort_children= advertised =sort-type= with a hyphen while every other multi-word argument in all fourteen tools uses snake_case. A model sending the conventional =sort_type= would have had it silently discarded, which is this heading's failure mode arriving by a different door. Renamed to =sort_type= 2026-08-20.

*The rest of the surface is clean, which is worth recording so it is not re-audited.* All fourteen tools were checked mechanically, comparing each advertised argument name against its handler's arglist: *zero mismatches* once snake_case is normalised to kebab-case. Every advertised parameter exists and every real parameter is advertised. The five failures on 2026-08-20 -- =todo_state=, =body=, =file=, =max_level=, and =body= again -- were invented wholesale, not misread from a bad schema. Fixing descriptions would not have prevented any of them.

*One measured consequence beyond the lost bodies:* an =org_outline= call passing =file= and =max_level= returned the *unrestricted default* rather than the scoped view it appeared to return, and was reported as scoped. A dropped argument does not merely fail to apply -- it silently substitutes a different answer to a different question.


* TODO org_capture swallows a title beginning with a TODO keyword :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       2b2db914-c96e-416a-a80a-e31028308de0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 18:50]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-26 Wed 18:50] \\
  still open; guard not yet written
:END:

*Open. Promised during 2026-08-26's session and not filed until the next morning* -- which is itself the pattern that produced the other six retcons.

*=org_capture= with a title beginning with a TODO keyword loses that word.* Capturing "NEXT belongs to containers; nominate rather than promote" produced =** NEXT belongs to containers; ...=: org parsed the leading =NEXT= as the *keyword*, so the heading acquired a state it was never given and its title lost its first word.

*The tool cannot prevent this by documentation.* Its description already says the heading is written with no TODO keyword, and that remains true -- the keyword does not come from the tool, it comes from the title.

*Caught only because =org_set_todo='s reply echoed the truncated title back*: "Queued todo -> DOING (was NEXT)" on a heading that had just been captured keywordless. Nothing else would have shown it; the heading looks entirely normal in the file.

*Cheap guard, since the tool already knows the keyword set.* Refuse, or escape, a title whose first word is one. Refusing is probably right: such a title is nearly always a mistake, and a silently renamed heading is worse than an error. Note =bin/lint-org= cannot catch it either -- the result is a well-formed heading.


* MAYBE Evaluate choice of shell and standardize all project scripts :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       84b7d8b3-5161-4146-9167-0e7e2100e365
:CREATED:  [2026-07-31 Fri 15:23]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from "TODO"       [2026-08-05 Wed 14:06] \\
  Auto-promoted: sole remaining TODO in its sibling group.
:END:
[[file:~/.claude/plans/script-shell-standardization.md][Plan]]

Surfaced 2026-07-31 while relocating the statusline script
(=bin/statusline.fish=) into the repo: every other script in
=bin/=/=bin/hooks/=/=.claude/hooks/= is bash (mostly no extension,
one =.sh=), but the statusline script is fish — the only fish
dependency anywhere in this project, and one =future users= (per the
README/=CLAUDE.md= discussion) can't assume is installed the way bash
can be. Named =.fish= explicitly specifically so this deviation is
visible from the file listing rather than buried in the shebang, but
that's a stopgap, not a resolution.

*Update 2026-07-31:* option 1 (pure Emacs) below now has a real case
study, not just a hypothetical — see the sibling heading above ("Case
study: apply \"maximum Emacs\" to the statusline script", ID
e202e93d-cfa7-4d0a-8721-8f06428bc1e4). The statusline's =jq=-based
model-name extraction moved into Elisp
(=claude-code-ide-org--statusline-model-name=), and the shell wrapper
became =bin/statusline.sh= — plain POSIX =sh=, no =jq=, no fish — since
once the logic moves out, there's nothing left in the wrapper that
needs a fancier shell. The fish-vs-bash framing above is now
historical for this one script specifically (it's =sh= now, not fish),
but the broader project-wide decision (which of the five options to
standardize on) is still open.

Needs an actual decision, not just noticing the inconsistency:
1. Pure Emacs, given Emacs is already a hard dependency for every
   script in this project (they all end in an =emacsclient -e= call
   regardless of which shell wraps it). Rather than the shell side
   parsing stdin JSON (via =jq= or Python) and interpolating specific
   fields into an elisp expression string, write stdin to a temp file
   and pass *that path* to =emacsclient -e=, letting Elisp read and
   parse the raw JSON itself (native =json-parse-string=, no external
   dependency) and do all the field-extraction, decision logic, *and*
   output formatting — mirroring the temp-file convention this project
   already uses in the opposite direction for output
   (=claude-code-ide-org-write-*-report=). The shell wrapper would
   shrink to an near-unconditional stub: write stdin to a temp file,
   call emacsclient with its path, cat/format whatever comes back.
   Beyond just cutting the jq/Python dependency, this would eliminate
   an entire class of bug this project has already had to work around
   once (see =bin/hooks/session-pause=/=session-resume='s =jq -R -s .=
   JSON-string-encoding, specifically to avoid unsafe shell-to-elisp
   string interpolation) — if the shell layer never interpolates a
   parsed field into an elisp expression at all, that whole hazard
   disappears by construction rather than being carefully avoided each
   time.
2. Python instead of shell for some or all of these. Every hook
   script that parses the Claude Code JSON payload currently shells
   out to =jq=; Python's stdlib =json= module does that natively, no
   external dependency, arguably clearer than =jq='s own query
   language for anyone unfamiliar with it. Tradeoff: gives up the
   one-liner brevity these scripts have today for more verbose (if
   more structured) code, and Python, while extremely common on
   macOS/Linux dev machines, is still one more runtime assumption on
   top of whatever shell gets picked for the scripts that stay
   shell — this wouldn't replace the bash/fish/sh decision below so
   much as sit alongside it (e.g. "simple wrapper: shell; anything
   parsing/branching on JSON: Python").
3. Broader: is bash itself the right universal baseline for "future
   users," or should everything target POSIX =sh= for maximum
   portability instead?
4. Decide fish is fine for this one script specifically (e.g. if it's
   meaningfully clearer/shorter in fish) and document *why* it's the
   one exception, so a future contributor doesn't "fix" it back to
   bash without understanding the tradeoff, or
5. Standardize on bash (matching every other script) and rewrite
   =bin/statusline.fish= to drop the fish dependency entirely.

Whatever's decided should also cover: should this be written down as
a project convention (e.g. in CLAUDE.md's "Repository layout" or a new
"Scripting conventions" note) so it doesn't have to be
re-litigated per script.


* TODO [1/1] Clearing a buffer's read-only flag for an MCP tool leaves it cleared :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Tools
:ID:       c8a97d9d-8b13-4b6a-a8b9-1a3f24b5e00b
:CREATED:  [2026-08-10 Mon 12:37]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-10 Mon 12:36]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:59] =>  0:23
- [2026-08-10 Mon 12:36]--[2026-08-10 Mon 12:59] assigned at review
:END:

Raised by the user 2026-08-10. None of the MCP tools bind
=inhibit-read-only=, so any of them fails outright with a
=buffer-read-only= error against a buffer the user has toggled
read-only (=C-x C-q=). The standing workaround -- documented in
CLAUDE.md's "Read-only buffers" note -- is to clear the flag and
proceed without asking, since the user sets it to guard against their
own stray keystrokes, not to block Claude.

*The gap:* nothing ever restores it. Every clear silently disables that
guard from then on, and the user only finds out by noticing. The
workaround was written as a way past an error, and the other half was
never specified.

*Leading fix, not settled:* have the tool wrappers bind
=inhibit-read-only= t around their own writes, so the user's flag is
never touched at all. Strictly better than clear-then-restore, which
has a failure window (a tool erroring mid-call leaves the buffer
cleared) and races badly with concurrent sessions -- two of them
interleaving clear/restore can restore a flag the other still needs
cleared, or leave it cleared anyway. Binding has no window and no
shared state.

*The one thing it rested on is now verified.* Read-only guards
modification, not saving, so =save-buffer= ought to succeed under
=inhibit-read-only= -- but every tool here saves, and an unverified
"ought to" in the save path is how this project has acquired bugs
before. Confirmed in passing 2026-08-10: this very heading was inserted
into =TODO.org= while its buffer was read-only, using exactly the
proposed binding. The text reached disk, the buffer came back unmodified,
and =buffer-read-only= was still =t= afterwards. So the fix is a small
edit to each wrapper, not a research question.

Cheap and self-contained; no dependency on
[[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]. Note the
refactor does *not* make it moot: after the cutover the review-and-apply
command becomes the only writer of live state, and it writes to exactly
the buffers the user is most likely to be viewing read-only at the time.

Two documents encode the current half-answer and must be updated
together with the fix: CLAUDE.md's "Read-only buffers" paragraph under
the MCP tools table, and the =feedback-todo-org-read-only= memory.

*Raised in priority 2026-08-10 by the interactive apply hitting it.* The
first real =claude-code-ide-org-review= apply against a read-only
=TODO.org= failed outright; toggling =C-x C-q= and re-applying
succeeded. Two things make this a sharper case than the MCP-tool one
above, rather than another instance of it:

- *The gesture is the opposite of a stray keystroke.* Read-only exists to
  stop the buffer being edited by accident. =M-x
  claude-code-ide-org-review=, then =m=, then =x= is about as deliberate
  as an action gets, and it is the human's own hands doing it. The guard
  fires on precisely the case it was never meant to catch.
- *After the cutover this blocks the only writer there is.*
  [[id:feba67eb-35b3-48bd-a892-8ecd47ca52e0][feba67eb]] makes apply the
  sole path by which anything reaches an org file. A user who habitually
  keeps =TODO.org= read-only while reading it would then find the entire
  system silently unable to record anything, with a raw
  =buffer-read-only= error as the only clue.

So the wrapper fix above should cover
=claude-code-ide-org--review-apply-item= too, not just the MCP tools.

*One property confirmed by the failure, worth keeping:*
=--review-apply= records only items that returned no error, so the
failed attempt marked nothing applied and the retry was clean -- no
double-application, no half-applied batch. Whatever the fix, do not
regress that.

*A worse instance than the one this heading was filed for, 2026-08-25.* The read-only flag did not merely inconvenience an MCP tool -- it *failed the human's own review pass*. =M-x claude-code-ide-org-review=, =x=, and "Applied 0 item(s); 1 failed".

*The human pressing =x= is unambiguous authorisation to write*, which is what makes this different in kind from a tool call arriving unannounced. The guard exists so the user's stray keystrokes cannot edit the buffer; it is not meant to stand between them and a command they just invoked. So the apply path is the one place where binding =inhibit-read-only= is not a judgement call.

*Caused by the restore half of this heading's own subject.* The flag was cleared for an =org_amend=, restored immediately afterwards as the convention requires, and the next review pass hit it. Cleared and restored roughly eight times over the session; the failure came from doing it *correctly*.

*Diagnosed in one call because [[id:df1cf43c-749f-435c-9f45-3d8df1d725ad][df1cf43c]] shipped this morning*, which is the clearest return that instrumentation has paid here. The bare message names nothing; the captured record named everything:

: (:id "df001961-..." :message "Buffer is read-only: #<buffer TODO.org>" :backtrace nil)

*One limitation of that instrumentation is now visible:* =:backtrace nil=. Capture is additive on top of a plain =condition-case=, so a backtrace only materialises when =debug-on-error= is set -- which it is under ERT and is not in normal use. The item and the message carried the whole diagnosis this time, so the gap cost nothing, but it will not always.

*Verified no data was lost*, which was the real worry given [[id:21c91613-76e0-4590-affe-e2af9f1321ca][21c91613]]: apply writes the file and marks events consumed as two independent operations, so a failed write could still have consumed. It did not. The applied ledger's last entry was =2026-08-25T15:36:32= before and after the failure, and the day node's last CLOCK line remained =15:33--15:36=.

*Narrow fix available and worth taking before the general one:* bind =inhibit-read-only= around the apply write only. The general case this heading describes -- every file-touching tool binding it -- is a larger change with a real argument against it, since a tool call is not a human keystroke. The apply path has no such ambiguity.


** DONE Bind inhibit-read-only in the apply path, where the human asked for the write
CLOSED: [2026-08-25 Tue 16:09]
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       97b030a4-50df-4b8b-a781-bd4acd2931d0
:CREATED:  [2026-08-25 Tue 16:09]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "DONE"       from              [2026-08-25 Tue 16:09] \\
  narrow fix; the general tool case stays open on the parent
:END:

*Shipped 2026-08-25, the same afternoon it bit.* =claude-code-ide-org--review-apply-item= binds =inhibit-read-only= alongside the re-entrancy guard it already binds, which covers both the capture path and the =--at-id= path in one place.

*Narrow on purpose, and the parent stays open because of it.* The general proposal -- every file-touching tool binds this -- has a real argument against it: a tool call arrives unannounced, and letting the flag stop one is defensible. Apply has no such ambiguity, because the human pressed =x=.

*A binding rather than a =setq=, which is the whole safety property.* The flag returns when the scope exits, including on a non-local exit, so a failure part-way through cannot leave the buffer writable. The test asserts exactly that: the buffer is still read-only *after* a successful apply. An implementation that cleared =buffer-read-only= instead would pass the write assertion and silently disarm the user's guard -- which is the defect the parent heading is named for, reintroduced by its own fix.

*Verified discriminating*: removing the one binding fails this test and nothing else.


* TODO The org 9.7+ dependency is real, undeclared, and unenforced :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       1ed7b2b4-a809-4547-b04f-099a3111fc1e
:CREATED:  [2026-08-14 Fri 13:35]
:END:

*Raised by the user 2026-08-14,* on reading
[[id:c084553c-0621-4a96-9fa1-f32850aeec6a][c084553c]]'s probe results
and asking whether the org version it pins is a dependency, and whether
anything says so. Measured immediately, and the answer is that it is
real and nothing says so anywhere.

*The dependency, confirmed live rather than recalled.* Against the
bundled org that =emacs --batch -Q= loads on this machine:

: org-version: 9.6.15
:   org-element-type-p           fboundp=nil
:   org-element-contents-end     fboundp=nil
:   org-element-at-point         fboundp=t

Against the straight-managed org Doom and =bin/test= use: =9.8.7=, with
both bound. =config.el= calls =org-element-type-p= three times and
=org-element-contents-end= twice — all five in the drawer code — so on
9.6.15 the module does not degrade, it hits =void-function=. Both
accessors arrived in org 9.7, which is the actual floor.

*Where it is expressed today: nowhere that binds.* =packages.el= names
only =org-ql= and =claude-code-ide=, and never mentions org. There is no
=version<= check, no =org-assert-version=, no minimum in any header.
The only two places that know are indirect — =bin/test='s glob for the
straight =build-*= directory, and a troubleshooting paragraph in the
org-dev skill explaining why a test might behave as though a newer org
feature does not exist.

*Why that is worse than it sounds.* Both indirect mechanisms fail
*silently*. The org-dev skill already records that =bin/test='s glob
"silently falls back to the bundled org again with no error", and the
symptom is tests behaving as if a newer org feature is missing — which
is indistinguishable from the feature being broken. The glob does
resolve today (=build-29.4=), so nothing is wrong right now; the point
is that nothing would say so if it stopped.

*Not yet decided, and the options differ in kind:* an =org-assert-version=
or explicit =version<= guard at load time (fails loudly, on the right
machine, at the right moment); a declared minimum in =packages.el=
(documents intent, enforces nothing under straight); or making
=bin/test= refuse to run rather than fall back when its glob misses
(catches it in CI-shaped usage but never at a user's install). They are
not exclusive, and the first is the only one that protects someone
installing this module on an Emacs whose org nobody here controls —
which is the same argument
[[id:4f9e552a-e1a9-48dd-8801-3f23a31b9e32][4f9e552a]] made for keeping
the =command emacs= convention after the environment fix moved out.

*Related:* [[id:1caed585-3209-4108-8501-eba16baf6a01][1caed585]] asks
what this module may change on a user's machine when installed; this is
the narrower question of what it may *assume*. And
[[id:d7849119-8224-4993-a8ab-83e1434df53c][d7849119]]'s README would be
the natural place a stated minimum lands for a human reader.


* TODO Concurrent sessions share one git index, and explicit staging protects nothing :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       2aeb65d6-350e-4c75-811e-4a748044fe2c
:CREATED:  [2026-08-12 Wed 16:20]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "NEXT"       from              [2026-08-12 Wed 16:22] \\
  user is blocked on this: no second session until a mechanism exists
- State "TODO"       from "NEXT"       [2026-08-17 Mon 12:11] \\
  Auto-demoted: superseded by sibling [[id:95087d8f-e16d-45d3-bdea-9b179dc672b4][Lint for headings that are valid org but structurally unintended]] becoming NEXT.
:END:

*This one actually happened, deliberately, as a resilience test.* On
2026-08-12 two sessions ran against this same checkout. This session
wrote a heading to TODO.org, staged it with an explicit pathspec
(=git add -- TODO.org=), and went to commit. The commit failed with
=nothing to commit, working tree clean=: the other session had already
committed, and its pathspec-less =git commit= took the whole index,
sweeping this session's 69 staged lines into
=a8dc693 "Review-buffer interaction fixes ..."= — a commit whose
message describes unrelated work and names two unrelated heading
=:ID:=s. Content survived; attribution did not. =36410e2= is an empty
commit recording that, since =a8dc693= was already pushed and
rewriting a pushed commit under a live concurrent session is worse
than the mislabelling.

*The mechanism is the index, not the files.* Every convention this
project already has is about file contents — check for a modified
buffer, stage explicitly, never =git add -A=. None of them touch the
actual shared mutable state, which is =.git/index=: one per checkout,
not one per session. Worse, following the convention *increased* the
damage. Staging early loaded this session's work into the index
*before* the other session committed; a pathspec-less =git commit= then
picked it up automatically. Had this session not staged at all, its
work would have been left alone.

*Not covered by the existing worktree heading, and this is the load-
bearing part.*
[[id:2e09adb7-6e29-499c-a40e-98cd2b327cff][2e09adb7]] concludes that
worktrees handle the *code* side — "one checkout + branch per session,
no shared-file collisions" — and that only the org files need to stay a
single canonical copy in the main checkout. Read against this incident
those two halves collide: the file that got swept up *was* TODO.org,
which by that heading's own =:ID:=-uniqueness and clock-fragmentation
argument is exactly the file that *cannot* be partitioned into a
worktree. So a worktree session must still write and commit TODO.org
in the main checkout, through the main checkout's index, and hits this
identically. *Worktrees do not fix this scenario; they fix everything
around it.* That is new information for 2e09adb7, which assumed the
code side was the whole problem.

*Related but distinct:*
[[id:3cb3f955-f10a-47cd-84ab-e629d73ea59d][3cb3f955]] already asks
whether =git commit -- TODO.org= is "safe," but scoped to a *single*
session not sweeping up its own unrelated hunks in the same file. This
heading is the cross-session case: another session's *staged* work, in
any file. Same command, different failure. Worth answering together.

*One mitigation is already verified.* Tested 2026-08-12 in a scratch
repo: with =A.txt= staged, =git commit -- B.txt= commits B only, leaves
A staged, and does not commit A. So a pathspec-scoped commit is
genuinely safe for the *other* session's work. The catch is that it
constrains the wrong party — safety depends on the *committing* session
complying, and the session at risk has no way to enforce it.

*Caveat found while defining the hook below:* =git commit -- <paths>=
*fails on an untracked file* — =error: pathspec 'NEW.txt' did not match
any file(s) known to git=. A new file must be =git add=-ed first, and
only then can be committed by pathspec. So for brand-new content (a new
plan, a new script) the staging window this mitigation exists to avoid
is unavoidable, and exposure persists between the add and the commit.
The mitigation is complete only for already-tracked files — which is
where this incident happened, TODO.org being tracked.

*Options for something robust, none chosen:*
- *Convention only:* ban =git add= outright in favour of
  =git commit -- <paths>=, in CLAUDE.md and the org skill. Cheapest,
  verified to work, and binds only sessions that follow it. Given both
  sessions here are Claude Code reading the same CLAUDE.md, this is
  less hollow than it sounds — but it is guidance, not a mechanism, and
  the user asked for a mechanism.
- *A =pre-commit= hook* in =.githooks/=. Defined below — the only
  option here that is a mechanism rather than guidance, and cheaper
  than first assumed.
- *Route git writes through the event queue* of
  [[id:b5f7c5c7-7ad6-4c68-9cce-3479db1f1644][b5f7c5c7]]. That queue
  already carries =session_id=/=agent_id= and already exists to stop
  concurrent sessions from mutating shared state directly, with a human
  applying batches later. Commits are the same shape of problem. The
  heaviest option and the most consistent with where the project is
  already going.

*The =pre-commit= option, defined.* Measured 2026-08-12 in a scratch
repo with a hook that printed its own environment. =GIT_INDEX_FILE=
identifies which command form invoked the commit:

| Command                  | =GIT_INDEX_FILE=            | Sweeps a staged file? |
|--------------------------+-----------------------------+-----------------------|
| =git commit=             | =.git/index=                | yes                   |
| =git commit --amend=     | =.git/index=                | yes                   |
| =git commit -a=          | =.git/index.lock=           | yes                   |
| =git commit -- <paths>=  | =.git/next-index-<pid>.lock= | no                   |

The hook can read *both* views at once: what the commit will contain
(=git diff --cached=) and what the real index holds
(=GIT_INDEX_FILE=$(git rev-parse --git-dir)/index git diff --cached=).
In the pathspec case those differ exactly as they should — commit
=B.txt=, real index still =A.txt=. =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= also
propagates into the hook, confirmed by printing it.

*Proposed rule:* refuse when the form is a whole-index one (basename of
=GIT_INDEX_FILE= is =index= or =index.lock=) *and* the real index is
non-empty; fire only when =CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID= is set. That is the
sweep case and nothing else. =git commit -- <paths>= always passes;
=git commit -a= against an empty index passes, since no one's work is at
risk; a human committing from a plain terminal is never affected. The
message should name the staged paths and print the pathspec form to use
instead.

*This retires the "needs ownership tracking" objection*, which the
first version of this heading asserted and which is wrong: the command
*form* is a sufficient discriminator and the safe form is
self-identifying. Per-session ownership of staged paths would only be
needed for a more permissive rule ("allow the sweep if everything
staged is mine"), not worth building first. If it ever is, the
precedent for where such state lives is
=claude-code-ide-org-queue-directory= — per-session runtime files,
deliberately outside the repo.

*Known limitations, all of them accepted rather than solved:*
- =--amend= is *indistinguishable* from a plain pathspec-less commit —
  both report =.git/index= — so it gets refused too, absent an escape
  hatch such as an =ALLOW_SWEEP= environment variable.
- =--no-verify= bypasses the hook entirely. This makes the mistake loud
  rather than silent; it does not make it impossible, and it still
  constrains the committing party rather than protecting the session at
  risk.
- Merge/rebase/cherry-pick paths are *untested* — the scratch-repo run
  errored before reaching them, and they need checking before shipping.
- Opt-in per clone via =core.hooksPath=, inheriting the same setup
  caveat =pre-push= already has.

*Testing* would follow the existing =bin/*-test= pattern
(=sync-plans-test=, =queue-append-test=): a scratch repo, each of the
four command forms, asserting exit status and message.

*Blocked on this in practice:* the user has stated they will not run a
second concurrent session until a mechanism is in place, so this gates
concurrent work generally rather than being one more backlog item.


* TODO A duration assertion matches the clocktable's own timestamp, three minutes a day :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       5a5e87c9-f432-44c6-a5a6-f6f3755e4347
:CREATED:  [2026-08-20 Thu 11:02]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
CLOCK: [2026-08-20 Thu 10:50]--[2026-08-20 Thu 11:09] =>  0:19
- [2026-08-20 Thu 10:50]--[2026-08-20 Thu 11:09] assigned at review
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-20 Thu 16:44] \\
  captured keywordless; state never set
:END:

=claude-code-ide-org-clock-report= prefixes its table with =#+CAPTION: Clock summary at [2026-08-20 Thu 11:00], ...=. Two tests assert on durations with =(string-match-p "1:00" result)= against that whole string, so the caption's own clock satisfies the match.

Observed, not theorised: =test-clock-report-block-today= and =test-clock-report-explicit-tstart-tend= passed at 10:59:52 on 2026-08-20, failed at 11:00:12, 11:00:20 and 11:00:44, then passed again at 11:01. The failing assertion is the negative one, =(should (not (string-match-p "1:00" result)))=, which is the shape a green suite cannot catch: it only fires when the substring appears for the wrong reason.

Three minutes a day for this duration -- 01:00, 11:00, 21:00. Other durations asserted the same way have the same exposure at their own minutes.

Approach: assert against the table row, =| *Total time* | *0:00* |=, rather than the whole report. Stripping the caption would also work but matching the row is what the assertion means.

/Body restored 2026-08-20 by =org_amend=; the original capture silently discarded it -- see/ [[id:1cd2df62-35e9-47dc-bcce-dda7bae3c522][1cd2df62]].

*Filed 2026-08-21, not built.* The 2026-08-21 cross-artifact audit found that prose conventions recur (UUID-from-memory, clock-in-at-drift) while mechanically checked ones fail loudly and get fixed -- and that duplicated conventions drift into contradiction (four found in CLAUDE.md, one in a memory, one in the next-session prompt). =bin/check-org-dev-skill= already embodies the fix for one file; generalize it: the CLAUDE.md and org-skill transition tables agree; every cited =:ID:=, defcustom, and function name resolves (=git grep= the symbol); every "is live"/"not built" claim carries a greppable anchor (e.g. =config.el:2792=) that still matches. Wire into =bin/hooks/pre-commit= beside the existing lint. Not a prose rule about keeping prose fresh -- that is the anti-pattern recursing.


* TODO A clock-report test fails every evening, because its fixture spills past midnight :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       c31b6c76-8ad8-45d4-a87b-39e085e31368
:CREATED:  [2026-08-22 Sat 23:16]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "TODO"       from              [2026-08-22 Sat 23:16] \\
  captured keywordless, setting initial state
:END:

*Measured 2026-08-22 at 23:16.* =claude-code-ide-org-test-clock-report-block-today= builds its fixture as "now, seconds zeroed" plus one hour, then asserts the report contains =1:00=. Run after 23:00 the interval crosses midnight, so =:block "today"= sees only the part before it and the table reads =*0:44*=:

: (string-match-p "1:00" "#+CAPTION: Clock summary at [2026-08-22 Sat 23:16], ...
: | *Total time* | *0:44* |")  => nil

So the test is red for the last hour of every day, and green the other 23. Confirmed pre-existing: it fails identically on an unmodified tree, which is how it was ruled out while [[id:eaeeb4ee-ceee-4094-8598-bbcdc8214b66][eaeeb4ee]] was being verified.

*Distinct from* [[id:5a5e87c9-f432-44c6-a5a6-f6f3755e4347][5a5e87c9]], which names this same test and is easy to mistake for it. That one is the =#+CAPTION:='s own timestamp satisfying a duration match for three minutes a day; this one is the fixture's *interval* leaving the queried window, and the assertion that fails is the positive one rather than the negative. Both are the same underlying habit -- a test whose fixture is pinned to =(current-time)= and whose assertion is a substring match over the whole report -- so they are worth fixing together even though neither is the other.

Approach: anchor the fixture to a fixed date rather than to now, which removes the dependence on when the suite runs instead of narrowing the window in which it breaks.


* MAYBE Render a session transcript to readable org, so scrollback stops being the index :code:
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Dev
:ID:       96ddf1ef-67fd-4d3c-b8cc-01ebb14a687d
:CREATED:  [2026-08-26 Wed 08:48]
:END:
:LOGBOOK:
- State "MAYBE"      from              [2026-08-26 Wed 08:49] \\
  gated on whether /config resolves it
:END:

*Prompted 2026-08-26 by the user*: composing a prompt often means re-reading something far back in the session, and submitting jumps the view to the bottom, losing both the spot and the train of thought.

*The proximate cause is probably configuration, not this.* =~/.claude/settings.json= carries =tui: "fullscreen"=, which uses the terminal's alternate screen buffer -- the client owns the viewport and re-renders it, so the terminal's own scrollback is bypassed. Changing that setting via =/config= is the one-line experiment and should be tried first. This heading is the fix that survives whatever that turns out to be.

*The observation this rests on:* the transcript is already a plain file, at =~/.claude/projects/<project-slug>/<session-id>.jsonl=, and the user works in Emacs. Reading *there* while composing has no scroll position to lose, gives isearch and folding, and does not move when the agent answers.

*Why a renderer rather than just reading the JSONL.* It is one JSON object per line with content as an array of typed blocks; tool calls and their results dwarf the prose. Readable means: one heading per turn, prose as body, tool calls collapsed into a drawer so =#+STARTUP: content= folds them away by default.

*Already demonstrated to be worth having, twice in one session, both times by hand.* The footnote-discipline audit ([[id:2758f3a0-3c47-405e-9662-a7669dcb60cc][2758f3a0]]) replayed =bin/hooks/footnote-check='s logic over 84 assistant text blocks pulled out of this file. The sleep-window reconstruction ([[id:1a5a5254-f62d-46e7-9a6c-3d84f2f52873][1a5a5254]]) cross-checked queue events against scratch-file mtimes. Both were one-off shell pipelines that were thrown away.

*Non-obvious detail worth capturing before it is rediscovered:* a =type: "user"= entry is *usually a tool result*, not a human turn. In this session's transcript, 218 of 231 were. Any renderer that treats them as prompts will report roughly twenty times too many turns -- which is exactly the error the footnote audit made on its first pass, reporting 81 of 83 blocks as end-of-turn before the filter was corrected to 11 of 84.

*=MAYBE= deliberately.* The =/config= change may resolve the pain entirely, and this costs a script plus its tests. Worth building only if reading the transcript in Emacs turns out to be something the user actually does, rather than something that sounds useful.


* Review and planning
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Meta
:DATE_TREE: t
:END:
Meta-work: the review pass, planning, and the time that goes into deciding what to do rather than doing it. Time is clocked against the *day node*, which is why the day node carries an =:ID:= and the year and month nodes above it do not -- they are org-datetree's own scaffolding, and =bin/lint-org= knows the difference ([[id:e30d52d7-960b-44f3-ab92-93846c81a1bd][e30d52d7]]).

* TODO Open today's node
SCHEDULED: <2026-08-24 Mon 07:00 ++1d>
:PROPERTIES:
:CATEGORY: Meta
:ID:       2aff1e6d-71cf-4b3f-8a3f-b3fc63d07c99
:CREATED:  [2026-08-21 Fri 13:20]
:END:

The ritual: a standing daily prompt to actually do review and planning.

*It reminds; it does not create.* Creating today's node is [[id:9575e65b-b589-465a-830c-286541780f96][9575e65b]]'s job and happens when work happens. A repeater that created the node instead would manufacture one per calendar day whether or not anything occurred, and re-couple the node's id to a schedule -- both of the things the on-demand resolver exists to avoid.

*A category is the one place in this repo a repeater is safe* ([[id:96a51c2f-ce11-4b40-bdff-11223d3d4eb7][96a51c2f]]): a repeater never reaches DONE, so it can never sit under a completable ancestor, and a category carries no keyword and completes nothing.

- [2026-08-24 Mon 10:52]--[2026-08-24 Mon 10:55] suggested span, accepted at review
:END:
The recurring pass: archive what is closed, so the live file stops carrying it.

*What to do,* in order -- the reasons are in =.claude/rules/org-conventions.md= under "Archiving", not here:

1. Apply the queue.
2. Consolidate every drawer ([[id:7ae6562d-25c8-453e-b78b-920e036c5665][7ae6562d]]).
3. Archive every =DONE= *and* =CANCELLED= heading that carries an outcome summary.

Paired with [[id:2aff1e6d-71cf-4b3f-8a3f-b3fc63d07c99][2aff1e6d]] at the same timestamp so the two land as one sitting. =SCHEDULED= not =DEADLINE=, and =++1d= not =+1d=: the first says the time is a suggestion, the second means a missed day is skipped rather than banked.

*Step 1 cannot be delegated* -- apply is human-triggered by design -- which bounds what any scheduler can do here. [[id:aa1ba915-ee68-48df-afea-197ae60aac63][aa1ba915]] is the prompt; it can prepare and remind, not perform.

*The existing backlog is [[id:7fbab3b3-7843-4290-94a5-d421413f2be4][7fbab3b3]], not this heading.* Clearing 36 accumulated headings is a one-time migration with a decision attached; this is the steady state after it.

/Body pared 2026-08-24, from 47 lines. It had grown to hold three different things -- this ritual, that backlog, and a set of standing conventions -- and a repeater never reaches/ =DONE=/, so nothing in the/ =:PLAN:= /lifecycle would ever have collected it. See/ [[id:ff92700e-1713-4862-a3fe-8fd972ad96fa][ff92700e]]./
